Time-changed Markov processes and space-time coupled non-local equations Thanks: The authors E. Scalas and B. Toaldo acknowledge financial support under the National Recovery and Resilience Plan (NRRP), Mission 4, Component 2, Investment 1.1, Call for tender No. 104 published on 2.2.2022 by the Italian Ministry of University and Research (MUR), funded by the European Union – NextGenerationEU– Project Title “Non–Markovian Dynamics and Non-local Equations” – 202277N5H9 - CUP: D53D23005670006 - Grant Assignment Decree No. 973 adopted on June 30, 2023, by the Italian Ministry of Ministry of University and Research (MUR) Thanks: The author B. Toaldo would like to thank the Isaac Newton Institute for Mathematical Sciences, Cambridge, for support and hospitality during the programme Stochastic Systems for Anomalous Diffusion, where work on this paper was undertaken. This work was supported by EPSRC grant EP/Z000580/1 Thanks: The authors would like to thank Prof. I. Biočić for fruitful discussions on several technical points that improved a previous version of this manuscript
Giacomo Ascione 1, Enrico Scalas 2, Bruno Toaldo 3, Lorenzo Torricelli 4

Abstract
. In this paper we study coupled fully non-local equations, where a linear non-local operator jointly acts on the time and space variables. We establish existence and uniqueness of the solution. A maximum principle is proved and used to derive uniqueness. Existence is established by providing a stochastic representation based on anomalous processes constructed as a time change via the undershooting of an independent subordinator. This leads to general non-stepped processes with intervals of constancy representing a sticky or trapping effect. Our theory allows these intervals to be dependent on the immediately subsequent jump. These processes include scaling limit of suitable coupled continuous time random walks previously studied in applications, in particular in the context of anomalous diffusion and option pricing. Here we exploit our general theory to obtain a non-local analog of the Black and Scholes equation, addressing the problem of determining the seasoned price of a derivative security, in case the price fluctuations are described by a process whose jumps are dependent on the previous interval.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
60G53, 60K50, 60K15

1.
Introduction

Continuous Time Random Walks (CTRWs) are a popular class of models for anomalous diffusion, see for instance
[
51
]
and references therein. The suitability of CTRWs to describe anomalous processes led to several contributions, for example in Hamiltonian chaos
[
69
;
70
;
71
]
, but other applications have been studied
[
8
;
9
;
27
;
25
;
26
;
29
;
50
;
51
;
52
]
.
CTRWs are stepped processes (their trajectories are step functions) and thus they can be seen as a tick-by-tick approximation of possibly continuous anomalous dynamics. From this point of view, there is an increasing interest in scaling limits of CTRWs, that form an interesting class of Markov and non-Markovian processes. Indeed, these limit processes can exhibit continuous trajectories with intervals of constancy, in which the process stays in a fixed position, so that they cannot satisfy the strong Markov property (in the time-homogeneous case). A systematic, but still non-exhaustive, study of CTRWs limit processes has been carried out, see for instance
[
6
;
10
;
41
;
49
;
46
;
47
]
and also
[
30
;
31
]
for the connection with the limit of an averaging-homogenization problem. In our context, reference
[
47
]
is particularly relevant, where the authors proved Markov embeddings of the aforementioned processes in
\R^{d}
and studied the corresponding transition semigroups.

CTRWs limit processes can exhibit both jumps and intervals of constancy. The presence of jumps justifies non-local operators in the space variable appearing in their governing equations. On the other hand, intervals of constancy imply the presence of non-local operators in the time variable. A prototypical CTRWs limit process exhibits a structure either of the form
M(L(t))
or
M(L(t)-)
, where
M
is a càdlàg Feller process and
L
is the inverse of a subordinator, i.e., a non-decreasing Lévy process. In general,
M
and
L
are not necessarily independent: independence arises, for instance, when the pre-limit CTRWs are made of independent jumps and inter-jump times. The governing equation, in this case, inherits the non-locality in time from the behavior of the process
L
, while the possible non-locality in space only depends on the jumps of the parent Feller process
M
. The independent case, called
uncoupled
by physicists, has been widely studied in literature (see, e.g.,
[
7
;
18
;
20
;
38
;
39
;
54
]
). Let us consider an example. If we assume that
M
is an
\alpha
-stable isotropic Lévy process on
\R^{d}
and
L
is the inverse of an independent
\beta
-stable subordinator, that typically happens when the generalized central limit theorem can be applied without using triangular array limits, the governing equation of the process
M(L(t))
is the well-known space-time fractional heat equation with Caputo fractional time derivative and spatial fractional Laplacian:

(1.1)

\left(\partial_{t}^{\beta}+(-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}\right)q(x,t)=0.

The dependent case arises when the CTRW has dependent jumps and inter-arrival time intervals between jumps (see
[
10
]
) while preserving the renewal temporal properties. It is of crucial importance in applications (see, e.g.,
[
37
;
65
]
), but the governing equation has not been fully understood. Some results (for processes in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
) can be found, for instance, in
[
6
;
34
;
60
]
; however the first reference does not provide an evolution equation (in space-time variables) with an initial condition, while in the second and third references, the dependence only induces spatial heterogeneity, that is, the intervals of constancy depends on the position.
According to the particular dependence between
M
and
L
, it is possible to observe several different behaviors
[
10
;
42
]
. Here, we consider the following setting: the parent Feller process, that in this case will be denoted as
M^{\phi}
, is of the form
M(S(t))
, where
M
is a cádlág Feller process and
S
is an independent subordinator, while
L
is the inverse of the very same subordinator
S
. As a consequence, the time-changed process
X(t)=M^{\phi}(L(t)-)
can be also rewritten as
X(t)=M(H(t))
, where
H(t)=S(L(t)-)
is the undershooting of the subordinator
S
.

In this research we study in detail the process
X(t)
introduced before. This process arises from CTRWs with coupled jumps and inter-event times and has trajectories with intervals of constancy followed by dependent jumps. If for instance the process
X(t)
admits an interval of constancy for
t\in[t_{1},t_{2}]
, the position it reaches after the jump is exactly
M(t_{2})
. As we shall prove in the paper, it turns out that the governing equation of such processes presents coupled non-localities in time and space. For instance, if
M
is a Brownian motion and
S
is
\frac{\alpha}{2}
-stable, with
\alpha\in(0,2)
, then the governing equation is a (coupled) fully fractional heat equation:

(1.2)

\left(\partial_{t}-\Delta\right)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}q(x,t)={\frac{t^{-\alpha}}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}q(x,0)},

that is a coupled counterpart of (
1.1
) for
\beta=\frac{\alpha}{2}
. Such operator has been already adopted in
[
57
]
in a different setting and it is strictly related to the fully fractional heat equation appearing in
[
5
;
28
]
, which is a particular master equation as discussed in
[
16
]
. Eq. (
1.2
) also appeared (in the one-dimensional case) in the heuristic discussion of reference
[
10
, Example 5.2]
, inspired by the modeling aspects presented in
[
65
]
.

A closely related line of research is provided by the theory of Lévy walks, where the duration of a motion and the corresponding spatial displacement are intrinsically coupled. In scaling limits, this space-time coupling naturally leads to pseudo-differential equations in the joint time-space variables and, in several homogeneous cases, to fractional material derivatives. The functional limit theorems and governing equations obtained in
[
40
;
44
]
provide a basic probabilistic and analytic prototype for such coupled non-local dynamics. Moreover, the pointwise representation and numerical treatment of the fractional material derivative developed in
[
55
]
provide complementary analytic and computational tools for equations arising from Lévy-walk limits. These works therefore give useful context for the present approach, in which the dependence between trapping intervals and subsequent spatial motion is encoded by a linear non-local operator acting jointly on time and space.

Anomalous diffusion is not the only possible context in which these processes appear.
A distinct line of research, focused on financial markets and their statistical analyses, considered CTRWs as a tick-by-tick model for price fluctuations. Then the corresponding fluid-dynamic limit is considered in order to gain insight on economic measures such as the return distributions, see e.g.
[
1
;
48
;
63
;
61
]
and recently
[
67
]
. Such limits typically produce stable-like processes (and thus discontinuous continuous-time trajectories) and variations thereof. A particularly consequential aspect of modelling financial markets prices with scaling limit of CTRWs is that the processes thus obtained are non-Markovian and exhibits periods of constant prices.
However, Markovianity can be recovered, as explained in
[
47
]
, by augmenting the state space with the one variable keeping count of the time elapsed since the particle last move (the “sojourn time” or “age”).

In general, being able to establish some form of finite higher-dimensional Markovianity is especially useful in the mathematical modeling of random phenomena. Concretely, in a financial valuation context, it can be interpreted as the possibility to establish the theoretical fair value of a contract based only on a finite set of market-observable data.
Two recent papers have tried to bring together some of these aspects. In
[
62
]
the authors chose a semi-Markov multiplicative CTRW model based on a sequence of independent and identically distributed log-normal random variables and an independent renewal counting process. The limit process turns out to be semi-Markov with continuous trajectories. Although the governing equation of the limit process is studied, the independence between the spatial log-normal innovations and the renewal process, implies that the magnitude of the price moves remain independent from the trade duration, that is, the sojourn time of the price process. A similar, but more general, model with independence is presented in
[
35
]
, generalizing the earlier suggestions in
[
45
]
and
[
19
]
. Crucially, in such work a time-changed model with dependence between temporal and spatial variables is also introduced. The authors use spectral methods to derive the option price for such a model, and comment on a weak form of semi-Markov property (Markov embedding) of the process as a whole, but do not study the corresponding governing Black and Scholes type equation. Furthermore a financial model with “stochastic volatility”, based on a system of SDEs subordinated to the inverse of an independent stable subordinator is proposed in
[
23
]
.

Therefore, besides the applications in the context of anomalous diffusion, one significant applied problem addressed by the theory developed in the present paper is the extension and unification and of the contributions of
[
62
]
and
[
35
]
, by answering the problem of pricing for, and determining the seasoned value of, an intraday call option written on an asset with dependent duration and returns as in
[
35
]
, using the techniques inspired from the semi-Markov analysis of the limiting process in
[
62
]
.

Structure of the article and summary of the results

As a guide to readers, below we give a summary of the main results and topics that are dealt with in each section.

•

In Section
2
we introduce basic notions on uniqueness classes, semigroup actions and Bernstein functions. All that will be relevant in the subsequent sections.

•

In Section
3
we define a family coupled non-local operators including (
1.2
) as very special case, and we study the domain and regularity properties. Moreover, we provide a notion for the solution of the related Cauchy problem.

•

In Section
4
we review the theory of time change according to subordinators and their inverses, introduce the time-changed Feller process related to the Cauchy problem of Section
3
, and establish the Markov-additivity of the pair process-subordinator;

•

In Section
5
the first main Theorem
5.2
, is presented and proved. This states that the expectation of the process defined in Section
4
is the stochastic solution, in the sense of Definition
3.6
, of the Cauchy problem related with our class of coupled non-local operators, subject to a further local uniform boundary condition. To prove the theorem, firstly we focus on regularity properties for the expectation, described in Subsection
5.1
. In Subsection
5.2
we give the details of the proof.

•

In Section
6
we move to the applications in finance of the theory discussed in the previous sections. We define a process for pricing an intraday call option written on an asset with dependent duration and returns, whose log-price follows a time-changed Feller process as the one of Section
4
. This leads to the second main result, Theorem
6.11
, which is the applied counterpart of Theorem
5.2
. A second result, proved separately is necessary because the call option initial (terminal) datum does not satisfy the regularity required in Theorem
5.2
.
The proof of this theorem is split into several auxiliary results, firstly proving regularity of the solution, then establishing existence, uniqueness and, finally, the renewal equation in separate subsections.

•

In the appendices some auxiliary results are proved.

2.
Preliminaries

We let
E
be a locally compact separable Hausdorff space and we set
\mathcal{E}=\cB(E)
, the Borel
\sigma
-algebra on
E
. We equip any interval
[0,T]\subseteq\R_{0}^{+}{:=}[0,+\infty)
and the half-axis
\R_{0}^{+}
with the Borel
\sigma
-algebra. We also denote
\R^{+}:=(0,+\infty)
. With an abuse of language, we say that a function
f:E\to\R
belongs to
\mathcal{E}
if it is Borel-measurable, so that
\cE
will denote the linear space of Borel-measurable real valued functions on
E
.
Furthermore, we denote by
C(E)
the space of continuous functions
f:E\to\R
and by
C_{\rm b}(E)
the space of bounded continuous functions
f:E\to\R
. The space
C_{\rm b}(E)
is a Banach space once it is equipped with the uniform norm

\left\|f\right\|_{C(E)}:=\sup_{x\in E}|f(x)|.

Furthermore, we denote by
C_{0}(E)
the space of continuous functions
f:E\to\R
vanishing at infinity, i.e. such that for all
\varepsilon>0
there exists a compact
K\subset E
such that

\sup_{x\not\in K}|f(x)|<\varepsilon,

which is still a Banach space when equipped with the uniform norm.

2.1.
Uniqueness classes and semigroup actions

Consider a linear operator
G:\cC\subseteq\cE\to\cE
, where
\cC
is a suitable subspace of
\cE
. For such an operator, we consider the following differential equation

(2.1)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}q(t,x)=Gq(t,x),&t>0,x\in E,\\
q(0,x)=u(x),&x\in E,\end{cases}

where
u\in\cE
is suitable initial data. To introduce a notion of solution, we take inspiration from the theory of ordinary differential equations (see
[
22
, Chapter
2
]
).

Definition 2.1
.

We say that a function
q:[0,T]\times E\to\R
is a local Carathéodory solution of (
2.1
) if and only if

(i)

q\in\cB([0,T]\times E)
,
q(\cdot,x)\in C[0,T]
for any
x\in E
and
q(t,\cdot)\in\cC
for any
t\in(0,T]
;

(ii)

q(0,x)=u(x)
for any
x\in E
;

(iii)

Gq(\cdot,x)\in L^{1}(0,T)
for any
x\in E
;

(iv)

For any
x\in E
and
t\geq 0

(2.2)

q(t,x)=u(x)+\int_{0}^{t}Gq(s,x)ds.

We say that
q:\R_{0}^{+}\times E\to\R
is a global Carathéodory solution of (
2.1
) if its restriction to
[0,T]
is a local Carathéodory solution for any
T>0
.

Remark 2.2
.

This definition is related to the so-called mild solution of an abstract Cauchy problem (see
[
2
, Definition 3.1.1]
). For instance, suppose that
G
is a closed linear operator on
\mathcal{C}\subset C_{0}\left(E\right)
. Assume that
q
is a global Carathéodory solution of (
2.1
) such that
Gq(t,\cdot)\in C_{0}(E)
and
t\in[0,T]\mapsto\left\|Gq(t,\cdot)\right\|_{C_{0}(E)}\in\R
belongs to
L^{1}[0,T]
for all
T>0
. Notice also that, by definition of Carathéodory solution,
q(t,\cdot)\in\cC
. Hence, for convenience, we can denote
q(t)=q(t,\cdot)
so that
q:\R_{0}^{+}\to C_{0}(E)
. Then, in particular, by
[
2
, Proposition 1.1.7]
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}Gq(s)ds\,=\,G\int_{0}^{t}q(s)ds.

If also
u\in C_{0}(E)
, we can rewrite (
2.2
) in terms of Bochner integrals as

q(t)=u+G\int_{0}^{t}q(s)ds,

i.e.,
q
is a mild solution of the abstract Cauchy problem in
C_{0}(E)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}q(t)=Gq(t),&t>0,\\[7.0pt]
q(0)=u.\end{cases}

Now we want to consider some classes of functions
\cC_{0}\subseteq\cE
and
\cC_{\rm sol}\subseteq\cB(\R_{0}^{+}\times E)
that will play the following role:
\cC_{0}
will be the set of initial data so that the problem (
2.1
) admits a unique solution belonging the class
\cC_{\rm sol}
. Let us formalize this (see, for instance,
[
53
, page
204
]
in the case of the heat equation).

Definition 2.3
.

We say that the couple
(\cC_{0},\cC_{\rm sol})
, where
\cC_{0}\subseteq\cE
and
\cC_{\rm sol}\subseteq\cB(\R_{0}^{+}\times E)
is a uniqueness class for
G
if and only if

(i)

For any
q\in\cC_{\rm sol}
and any
t>0
it holds
q(t,\cdot)\in\cC_{0}\cap\cC
;

(ii)

For any
u\in\cC_{0}
there exists a unique global Carathéodory solution
q\in\cC_{\rm sol}
to (
2.1
).

Let us give some examples.

Example 2.4
.

Consider
E=\R
,
\cC=C^{2}(\R)
and
G=\partial^{2}_{x}
. If we set
\cC_{0}=C_{0}(\R)
and

\cC_{\rm sol}=\{q\in C(\R_{0}^{+}\times\R)\cap C^{1}(\R^{+}\times\R):\ q(t,\cdot)\in C^{2}_{0}(\R),\ \forall t>0,\mbox{ and }q(0,\cdot)\in C_{0}(\R)\},

then
(\cC_{0},\cC_{\rm sol})
is a uniqueness class for
G
, as it is well-known that the heat equation

(2.3)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}q(t,x)=\partial^{2}_{x}q(t,x),&t>0,x\in\R,\\
q(0,x)=u(x),&x\in\R,\end{cases}

admits a unique solution in
C^{2}_{0}(\R)
if
u\in C_{0}(\R)
. It is important to notice that such a solution is not unique among all the measurable functions on
\R
, as we could find other unbounded solutions of the same equation, that are usually called non-physical solutions (see
[
17
, Theorem 2.3.1]
).

Example 2.5
.

Again, consider
E=\R
,
\cC=C^{2}(\R)
and
G=\partial^{2}_{x}
. Now set

\cC_{0}=\{u\in C(\R):\ \exists c_{1},c_{2}>0,\ \exists\gamma\in[0,2),\ |u(x)|\leq c_{1}e^{c_{2}|x|^{\gamma}},\ \forall x\in\R\}

and

\cC_{\rm sol}=\{q\in C(\R_{0}^{+}\times\R)\cap C^{1}(\R^{+}\times\R):\ q(t,\cdot)\in C^{2}(\R),\ \forall t>0,\\
\mbox{ and }\ \forall T>0,\exists c_{1},c_{2}>0,\exists\gamma\in[0,2),\ |q(t,x)|\leq c_{1}e^{c_{2}|x|^{\gamma}},\\
\ \forall x\in\R,\ \forall t\in[0,T]\}.

Then
(\cC_{0},\cC_{\rm sol})
is a uniqueness class for
G
as (
2.3
) admits a unique solution
q\in\cC_{\rm sol}
whenever
u\in\cC_{0}
(see
[
17
, Theorem 3.6.1]
). Let us stress that we could also consider
G
as the generator of the Brownian motion. In such a case, however, since
\cC_{0}\not\subset C_{0}(\R)
, the usual theory of Feller semigroups (see
[
58
, Definition III.6.5]
) does not apply.

Example 2.6
.

Now let
E=[a,b]\subset\R
for some
a<b
. As before, set
G=\partial^{2}_{x}
with
\cC=C^{2}(a,b)
. Set

\displaystyle\cC_{0}

\displaystyle=\{u\in C[a,b]:\ u(a)=u(b)=0\}

\displaystyle\cC_{\rm sol}

\displaystyle=\{q\in C(\R_{0}^{+}\times[a,b])\cap C^{2}(\R^{+}\times(a,b)):\ q(t,a)=q(t,b)=0,\ \forall t>0\}.

Then
(\cC_{0},\cC_{\rm sol})
is a uniqueness class for
G
. Indeed, in such a case,
q\in\cC_{\rm sol}
is a solution of (
2.1
) if and only if it solves

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}q(t,x)=\partial^{2}_{x}q(t,x),&t>0,\ x\in(a,b)\\
q(0,x)=u(x)&x\in[a,b]\\
q(t,a)=q(t,b)=0&t>0,\end{cases}

that is a Dirichlet-Cauchy problem and then admits a unique solution.

Example 2.7
.

Let now
(P_{t})_{t\geq 0}
be a Feller semigroup on
C_{0}(E)
with generator
(G,{\rm Dom}(G))
. Then we can set
\cC_{0}=C_{0}(E)
and
\cC_{\rm sol}=C(\R_{0}^{+};C_{0}\left(E\right))
. By standard Feller semigroups theory (see
[
2
, Chapter 3]
), we know that
(\cC_{0},\cC_{\rm sol})
is a uniqueness class for
G
. This implies that the abstract setting we are considering can be applied in general to Feller semigroups and their generators. We can also consider strong solutions in this framework by setting
\cC_{0}={\rm Dom}(G)
and
\cC_{\rm sol}=C^{1}\left(\mathbb{R}_{0}^{+};{\rm Dom}(G)\right)
.

Definition 2.8
.

For a linear operator
G:\mathcal{C}\subseteq\cE\to\cE
and a uniqueness class
(\cC_{0},\cC_{\rm sol})
for
G
, we define the family of linear operators
(P_{t})_{t\geq 0}
acting on
\cC_{0}
as follows:

(i)

P_{0}=I
, where
I
is the identity on
\cC_{0}
;

(ii)

For any
t>0
,
f\in\cC_{0}
and
x\in\cE
,
P_{t}u(x)=q(t,x)
, where
q
is the unique Carathéodory solution of (
2.1
) in
\cC_{\rm sol}
.

We call the family
(P_{t})_{t\geq 0}
the semigroup action induced by
G
on
(\cC_{0},\cC_{\rm sol})
.

The family
(P_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is indeed an action of the additive semigroup
(\R_{0}^{+},+)
on the linear space
\cC_{0}
. Indeed, fix any
u\in\cC_{0}
and
t,s>0
and consider
q(t,\cdot)=P_{t}u
. Now let
\bar{q}(s,\cdot)=P_{s}q(t,\cdot)
and observe that
\bar{q}
is the unique function in
\cC_{\rm sol}
such that for any
s\geq 0
and any
x\in E

(2.4)

\bar{q}(s,x)=q(t,x)+\int_{0}^{s}G\bar{q}(\tau,x)d\tau.

However, by definition,

q(t+s,x)=u(x)+\int_{0}^{t+s}Gq(\tau,x)d\tau\\
=q(t,x)+\int_{t}^{t+s}Gq(\tau,x)d\tau=q(t,x)+\int_{0}^{s}Gq(t+\tau,x)d\tau,

that is to say that the function
q(t+\cdot,\cdot)\in\cC_{\rm sol}
satisfies (
2.4
) and then

P_{t+s}u(x)=q(t+s,x)=\bar{q}(s,x)=P_{s}q(t,x)=P_{s}P_{t}u(x)

for any
s>0
and any
x\in E
. We use the name
semigroup action
in place of
semigroup
to avoid any possible confusion with Feller semigroups on Banach spaces, that are a particular case in this theory.

2.2.
Bernstein functions

We here recall some basic definitions and properties concerning Bernstein functions that will be used throughout the paper. We mainly refer to
[
64
]
where proofs of the theorems stated below can be found.

Definition 2.9
.

A function
\phi:\R^{+}\to\R^{+}_{0}
is said to be a Bernstein function if,
\phi(\lambda)\geq 0
,
\phi\in C^{\infty}(\R^{+})
and for any
n\in\N

(-1)^{n-1}\phi^{(n)}(\lambda)\geq 0\quad\forall\lambda\in\R^{+}.

We denote by
\mathfrak{B}
the convex cone of Bernstein functions.

Any function
\phi\in\mathfrak{B}
admits a special representation.

Theorem 2.10
.

For any
\phi\in\mathfrak{B}
there exists a unique triplet
(a_{\phi},b_{\phi},\nu_{\phi})
, where
a_{\phi},b_{\phi}\in\R^{+}
and
\nu_{\phi}
is a Borel measure on
(0,+\infty)
with the property

(2.5)

\int_{(0,\infty)}(1\wedge t)\,\nu_{\phi}(dt)<\infty,

such that

(2.6)

\phi(\lambda)=a_{\phi}+b_{\phi}\lambda+\int_{(0,\infty)}(1-e^{-\lambda t})\nu_{\phi}(dt).

Vice versa, any triplet
(a_{\phi},b_{\phi},\nu_{\phi})
with
a_{\phi},b_{\phi}>0
and
\nu_{\phi}
a Borel measure on
(0,+\infty)
satisfying (
2.5
) defines a Bernstein function
\phi
by means of (
2.6
).

Definition 2.11
.

The triplet
(a_{\phi},b_{\phi},\nu_{\phi})
is called the Lévy triplet of
\phi
and, in particular,
\nu_{\phi}
is called the Lévy measure of
\phi
. We denote by
\mathfrak{B}_{0}
the subset of
\mathfrak{B}
composed of Bernstein functions with triplet
(0,0,\nu_{\phi})
and such that
\nu_{\phi}(0,\infty)=\infty
.

Any Bernstein function defines the law of a subordinator in a unique way. Namely, for any
\phi\in\mathfrak{B}
there exists a (canonical) probability space and a (possibly killed) subordinator
\sigma:=\{\sigma(t),\ t\geq 0\}
, i.e. an increasing Lévy process, such that

\E[e^{-\lambda\sigma(t)}]=e^{-t\phi(\lambda)},\ \forall t\geq 0,\ \forall\lambda\geq 0,

where
\E
is the expected value on the aforementioned probability space. Vice versa, if
\sigma
is a subordinator on a suitable probability space, then the function

\phi(\lambda)=-\log(\E[e^{-\lambda\sigma(1)}])

belongs to
\mathfrak{B}
. For further details we refer to
[
64
, Chapter
5
]
,
[
11
, Chapter III]
and
[
12
]
. For the ease of the reader, for any
0\leq t_{1}<t_{2}<\infty
and any Borel-measurable
f:\R^{+}\to\R
, we shall denote

\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}f(s)\nu_{\phi}(ds):=\int_{(t_{1},t_{2}]}f(s)\nu_{\phi}(ds)\quad\mbox{and}\quad\int_{t_{1}}^{\infty}f(s)\nu_{\phi}(ds):=\int_{(t_{1},\infty)}f(s)\nu_{\phi}(ds).

In the next sections we shall use the following notation

\overline{\nu}_{\phi}(t):=\nu_{\phi}(t,\infty),\quad\mathcal{I}_{\phi}(t):=\int_{0}^{t}\overline{\nu}_{\phi}(s)ds,\quad\mathcal{J}_{\phi}(t):=\int_{0}^{t}s\nu_{\phi}(ds);

the previous quantities will play a crucial role later in computations.
A simple integration by parts argument leads to the relation

(2.7)

\mathcal{J}_{\phi}(t)=t\overline{\nu}_{\phi}(t)-\mathcal{I}_{\phi}(t).

A Bernstein function
\phi\in\mathfrak{B}
is said to be special if the function

\phi^{\star}(\lambda)=\frac{\lambda}{\phi(\lambda)}

still belongs to
\mathfrak{B}
. We call
\mathfrak{SB}_{0}
the set
\mathfrak{SB}_{0}:=\mathfrak{SB}\cap\mathfrak{B}_{0}
, where
\mathfrak{SB}
is the set of special Bernstein functions.

3.
The non-local operator

We here define the coupled non-local operator that will be used in the next sections.

Definition 3.1
.

Consider a linear operator
G:\cC\subseteq\cE\to\cE
, a uniqueness class
(\cC_{0},\cC_{\rm sol})
for
G
and its induced semigroup action
(P_{t})_{t\geq 0}
on
\cC_{0}
. Let
\phi\in\mathfrak{B}_{0}
and
f\in\cB(\R_{0}^{+}\times E)
such that, for any
t\geq 0
,
f(t,\cdot)\in\cC_{0}
. For
t>0
and
x\in E
we define

-\phi\left(\partial_{t}-G\right)f(t,x)=\int_{0}^{+\infty}(P_{s}f(t-s,x)\mathds{1}_{[0,t]}(s)-f(t,x))\nu_{\phi}(ds),

where, for any
t<0
, we set
f(t,x)\equiv 0
, provided the integral is well-defined.

Now we would like to find some conditions on
f
so that
-\phi\left(\partial_{t}-G\right)f(t,x)
is well-defined at least for some
(t,x)\in\R_{0}^{+}\times E
. This is true as a consequence of the following theorem.

Theorem 3.2
.

Let
G:\cC\subseteq\cE\to\cE
be a linear operator,
(\cC_{0},\cC_{\rm sol})
be a uniqueness class for
G
and
(P_{t})_{t\geq 0}
be the semigroup action induced by
G
on
\cC_{0}
. Let also
\phi\in\mathfrak{B}_{0}
. Consider a function
f\in\cB(\R_{0}^{+}\times E)
such that
f(t,\cdot)\in\cC_{0}
. Fix
(t,x)\in\R_{0}^{+}\times E
and assume further that:

(i)

It holds

\int_{0}^{t}|P_{s}{f}(t-s,x)-P_{s}{f}(t,x)|\nu_{\phi}(ds)<\infty;

(ii)

It holds

\int_{0}^{t}|P_{s}{f}(t,x)-{f}(t,x)|\nu_{\phi}(ds)<\infty.

Then
-\phi(\partial_{t}-G)f(t,x)
is well-defined.

Proof.

It is sufficient to split the integral defining
-\phi(\partial_{t}-G)f(t,x)
as follows:

\displaystyle-\phi(\partial_{t}-G)f(t,x)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}(P_{s}f(t-s,x)-P_{s}f(t,x))\nu_{\phi}(ds)

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}(P_{s}f(t,x)-f(t,x))\nu_{\phi}(ds)-\overline{\nu}_{\phi}(t)f(t,x),

where the two integrals are absolutely convergent by
(i)
and
(ii)
.
∎

In the following, we shall make explicit use of the Laplace transform. The following theorem provides sufficient conditions to guarantee that
-\phi(\partial_{t}-G)f(\cdot,x)
is Laplace transformable for fixed
x\in E
. The proof of this theorem is an immediate consequence of the existence conditions for Laplace transforms (see
[
2
, Section 1.4]
).

Theorem 3.3
.

Let
G:\cC\subseteq\cE\to\cE
be a linear operator,
(\cC_{0},\cC_{\rm sol})
be a uniqueness class for
G
and
(P_{t})_{t\geq 0}
be the semigroup action induced by
G
on
\cC_{0}
. Let also
\phi\in\mathfrak{B}_{0}
. Consider a function
f\in\cB(\R_{0}^{+}\times E)
such that
f(t,\cdot)\in\cC_{0}
. Fix
x\in E
and suppose that the assumptions of Theorem
3.2
hold on
(t,x)
for a.a.
t\in\R_{0}^{+}
. Assume further that

(i)

There exists
\lambda_{0}>0
such that for all
\lambda>\lambda_{0}

\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}\int_{0}^{t}|P_{s}{f}(t-s,x)-P_{s}{f}(t,x)|\,\nu_{\phi}(ds)\,dt<\infty;

(ii)

There exists
\lambda_{1}>0
such that for all
\lambda>\lambda_{1}

\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}\int_{0}^{t}|P_{s}{f}(t,x)-{f}(t,x)|\,\nu_{\phi}(ds)\,dt<\infty;

(iii)

There exists
\lambda_{2}>0
such that for all
\lambda>\lambda_{2}

\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}\overline{\nu}_{\phi}(t)|{f}(t,x)|\,dt<\infty.

Then, setting
\lambda_{\star}:=\max\{\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\}
, for all
\lambda>\lambda_{\star}
it holds

(3.1)

\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}|(-\phi(\partial_{t}-G)f(t,x))|dt<\infty

and

(3.2)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}(-\phi(\partial_{t}-G)f(t,x))dt=\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}(P_{s}f(t-s,x)\mathds{1}_{[0,t]}(s)-f(t,x))dt\,\nu_{\phi}(ds).

The assumptions of both Theorems
3.2
and
3.3
are quite general and must be verified separately depending on the properties of the operator
G
and the semigroup action
(P_{t})_{t\geq 0}
.

Furthermore, if we equip the state space
E
with a measure
\mu_{E}
, then we could ask for some sufficient conditions on
f
so that
-\phi(\partial_{t}-G)f(t,x)
is well-defined for a.a.
t\geq 0
and
\mu_{E}
-a.a.
x\in E
. This can be done by developing, for instance, a
L^{1}_{\rm loc}
-theory. Since, however, in our cases of interest it will be not necessary, we do not report these details.

On the other hand, we shall make use of a maximum principle, that holds under some additional assumptions on the semigroup action
(P_{t})_{t\geq 0}
. We recall here some specific properties.

Definition 3.4
.

We say that the semigroup action
(P_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is positivity preserving if for all
f\in\cC_{0}
with
f(x)\geq 0
for all
x\in E
it holds
P_{t}f(x)\geq 0
for all
x\in E
and
t\geq 0
.

We say that the semigroup action
(P_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is sub-Markov if there exists a one-parameter semigroup of operators
(P^{\rm b}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
acting on
C_{\rm b}(E)
and coinciding with
(P_{t})_{t\geq 0}
on
\cC_{0}\cap C_{\rm b}(E)
such that
P^{\rm b}_{t}1\leq 1
.

Throughout the paper we shall omit the superscript
{\rm b}
on the additional semigroup
(P_{t}^{\rm b})_{t\geq 0}
. With these definitions, we are ready to prove the following Positive Maximum Principle (PMP).

Proposition 3.5
.

Assume that the semigroup action
(P_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is positivity preserving and sub-Markov.

(i)

Assume there exists
(t_{\star},x_{\star})\in\R^{+}\times E
such that
f(t_{\star},x_{\star})\geq f(t,x)
for all
(t,x)\in(0,t_{\star}]\times E
and
f(t_{\star},x_{\star})>f(0,x)
, for all
x\in E
and
-\phi(\partial_{t}-G)f(t_{\star},x_{\star})
is well-defined. Then

-\phi(\partial_{t}-G)f(t_{\star},x_{\star})+\overline{\nu}_{\phi}(t_{\star})f(0,x_{\star})<0.

(ii)

Assume there exists
(t_{\star},x_{\star})\in\R^{+}\times E
such that
f(t_{\star},x_{\star})\leq f(t,x)
for all
(t,x)\in(0,t_{\star}]\times E
,
f(t_{\star},x_{\star})<f(0,x)
for all
x\in E
and
-\phi(\partial_{t}-G)f(t_{\star},x_{\star})
is well-defined. Then

-\phi(\partial_{t}-G)f(t_{\star},x_{\star})+\overline{\nu}_{\phi}(t_{\star})f(0,x_{\star})>0.

Proof.

We just prove
(i)
, since the proof of
(ii)
is analogous. For
t\in[0,t_{\star}]
and
x\in E
define
\overline{f}(t,x)=f(t_{\star},x_{\star})-f(t,x)\geq 0
. Since
P_{t}
is positivity preserving, we have
P_{s}\overline{f}(t_{\star}-s,x)\geq 0
for all
s\in[0,t_{\star}]
and
x\in E
, that in turn implies
P_{s}f(t_{\star}-s,x)\leq(P_{s}f(t_{\star},x_{\star}))(x)
for all
s\in[0,t_{\star}]
and
x\in E
. Furthermore, since
P_{s}
is sub-Markov and linear, we have

P_{s}f(t_{\star}-s,x)\leq f(t_{\star},x_{\star}),\ \forall s\in[0,t_{\star}]\ \forall x\in E.

Hence, it is sufficient to observe that

\displaystyle-\phi(\partial_{t}-G)

\displaystyle f(t_{\star},x_{\star})+\overline{\nu}_{\phi}(t_{\star})f(0,x_{\star})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t_{\star}}(P_{s}f(t_{\star}-s,x_{\star})-f(t_{\star},x_{\star}))\nu_{\phi}(ds)

\displaystyle\quad+\overline{\nu}_{\phi}(t_{\star})(f(0,x_{\star})-f(t_{\star},x_{\star}))<0.

∎

In the next section, we shall focus on the following equation:

(3.3)

\begin{cases}\phi(\partial_{t}-G)q(t,x)=\overline{\nu}_{\phi}(t)q(0,x)&t>0,\ x\in E\\
q(0,x)=u(x)&x\in E,\end{cases}

where
u
is suitable initial data. Let us give the definition of solution.

Definition 3.6
.

We say that
q:\R_{0}^{+}\times E\to\R
is a solution of (
3.3
) if
q
is continuous,
\phi(\partial_{t}-G)q(t,x)
is well-defined for all
(t,x)\in\R^{+}\times E
and the equality in (
3.3
) hold.

We remark that operators in the form of
\phi\left(\partial_{t}-G\right)
have already been considered in the literature. In
[
28
]
the authors considered the equation
\left(\partial_{t}-\Delta\right)^{\alpha}q=q^{p}
,
\alpha\in(0,1)
,
p>0
, for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
t\in(0,T)
, and studied the existence and behaviour of blow-up. In
[
5
]
the authors considered the equation
\left(\partial_{t}-\Delta\right)^{\alpha}q=0
,
\alpha\in(0,1)
, in connection with the obstacle problem for the fractional Laplacian. Furthermore, in
[
16
]
the authors considered coupled operators of this kind:
\phi\left(\partial_{t}-G\right)
, under suitable assumptions, and they provided Hölder estimates for integro-differential equations involving those operators.

In this paper, we are interested in proving the existence and uniqueness of the solutions to (
3.3
) and relating them to some appropriate non-Markov processes. We shall consider two specific cases of interest:

•

In the first case, we shall assume that
G
is the generator of a Feller semigroup on
C_{0}(E)
. In such a case (
3.3
) is shown to be the governing equation of a large family of time-changed Feller processes.

•

In the second case, we shall focus on a sub-diffusive Black-Scholes equation in the context of a sub-diffusive market. Notice that, in such a situation, we could consider
G
to be the generator of a geometric Brownian motion, however the initial (or final) data we consider does not belong to
C_{0}(\R^{+})
. For such a reason, a deep investigation of the uniqueness classes related to the problem is needed. Even in this case, however, the solution can be related to a suitably time-changed geometric Brownian motion.

In both cases, the existence of the solution can be proved using a stochastic representation, namely a suitable time-change of a Markov process generated by
G
. This idea will be described in the next section.

4.
The undershooting of a subordinator and related time-changed processes

Fix a filtered probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F}_{\infty},\{\mathcal{F}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0},\mathds{P})
. Throughout the paper, we will denote filtrations with a calligraphic letter without subscript, as, for instance,
\cF:=\{\cF_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
. Let
S=\{S(t),\ t\geq 0\}
be a real-valued, strictly increasing càdlàg process with independent and stationary increments. In particular, we have that
S_{0}=\{S_{0}(t),\ t\geq 0\}
defined as
S_{0}(t)=S(t)-S(0)
is a subordinator. We define the first-passage processes

\displaystyle L(t)

\displaystyle:=\inf\{u\geq 0:\ S(u)>t\}

\displaystyle L_{0}(t)

\displaystyle:=\inf\{u\geq 0:\ S_{0}(u)>t\},

where for all
t\geq 0
it holds
L(t)=L_{0}(t-S(0))
. Since
S
is assumed to be strictly increasing,
L
is continuous and increasing.
Denote by
D(\R_{0}^{+})
the space of real-valued càdlàg functions and for any
f\in D(\R_{0}^{+})
and
t>0
let
f(t-)=\lim_{\delta\uparrow 0}f(t+\delta)
, while we set
f(0-):=f(0)
. We define

(4.1)

\displaystyle H(t):=S_{0}(L(t)-)\qquad H_{0}(t):=S_{0}(L_{0}(t)-).

In particular,
H_{0}
is called the undershooting of the subordinator
S_{0}
, see
[
12
, Section 1.4]
. Concerning
H
, we have, for all
t\geq 0

H(t)=S(L(t)-)-S(0)=H_{0}(t-S(0)).

Throughout the paper, we assume that
\E[e^{-\lambda S_{0}(t)}]=e^{-t\phi(\lambda)}
, where
\phi\in\mathfrak{B}_{0}
. The potential measure of the subordinator
S_{0}
is defined as

U^{\phi}(t)=\E[L_{0}(t)].

It is well-known (see
[
64
, Theorem 10.3 and Equation (10.9)]
) that if
\phi\in\mathfrak{SB}_{0}
then there exists a non-increasing function
u^{\phi}:\R^{+}\to\R^{+}
(called the potential density) such that
\int_{0}^{1}u^{\phi}(t)dt<\infty
and

U^{\phi}(dt)=u^{\phi}(t)dt.

Since
u^{\phi}
is non-increasing and non-negative,
U^{\phi}
is concave. We recall (see
[
64
, Theorem 10.9]
) that
(u^{\phi},\bar{\nu}_{\phi})
constitute a Sonine pair, meaning that

\int_{0}^{t}u^{\phi}(\tau)\bar{\nu}_{\phi}(t-\tau)d\tau=1.

Such a condition, called Sonine condition, has been first introduced in
[
66
]
and became very popular in fractional calculus (see, e.g.,
[
32
;
38
;
43
]
).
In general, for all Borel sets
A\subseteq[0,t]
(see
[
12
, Lemma 1.10]
),

\mathds{P}(H_{0}(t)\in A)=\int_{A}\overline{\nu}_{\phi}(t-y)U^{\phi}(dy).

Furthermore, if
\phi\in\mathfrak{SB}_{0}
, it holds

(4.2)

\mathds{P}(H_{0}(t)\in A)=\int_{A}\overline{\nu}_{\phi}(t-y)u^{\phi}(y)\,dy.

Since it is a suitable time translation of the undershooting
H_{0}
of
S_{0}
, we refer to
H
as the undershooting of
S
. Since it is the composition of the càglàd function
S_{0}(\cdot-)
with the continuous function
L(\cdot)
, it is true that also
H
is càglàd.
Now, we want to use
H
as a time-change. The fact that it is not right-continuous implies that this is not a time-change in the usual sense, e.g.
[
56
, Definition V.1.2]
. However, a suitable theory of left-continuous time-changes has been developed in the context of subordinators and their inverses (see
[
47
]
).

To define our time-changed process, let
M=\left\{M(t),t\geq 0\right\}
be a Feller process independent of
S
. We define
M^{\phi}(t)=M(S_{0}(t))
and
\tau(t)=t+S(0)
for all
t\geq 0
. Since
(M,\tau)
is a Feller process, by Phillips’ Theorem (see
[
59
, Theorem 32.1]
) we know that
(M^{\phi},S)
, that is obtained by subordination via
S_{0}
, is still a Feller process with respect to its natural filtration. For the reader convenience we recall that Phillips’ Theorem states that, by subordinating (via a subordinator with Laplace exponent
\phi
) a strongly continuous contraction semigroup of operators on a Banach space, one obtains another strongly continuous semigroup on the same Banach space. The theorem then also provides a form for the generator of this new semigroup. In our context, this is used by observing that since
(M,\tau)
is a Feller process then it is associated with a Feller semigroup: the subordination of this semigroup then correponds the time-change of the process and this yields our previous assertion. In the following, it will be useful to consider the canonical construction of
(M,S)
, as in
[
56
, Chapter III.1]
, with the only exception that we are using the most general form of the Kolmogorov extension theorem, as in
[
68
, Theorem 2.4.3]
, so that we do not need
E
to be a Polish space. We recall here the canonical construction. We have
\Omega=D(\R^{+}_{0};E\times\R)
, i.e., the space of càdlàg functions with values in
E\times\R
, and we set for any
\omega\in\Omega
, denoting
\omega(t)=(\omega_{1}(t),\omega_{2}(t))\in E\times\R
for
t\geq 0
,
(M(t)(\omega),S(t)(\omega))=\omega(t)
. For
t\geq 0
, we let
\mathcal{F}_{t}^{0}=\sigma((M(s),S(s)),\ s\leq t)
and
\mathcal{F}_{\infty}^{0}=\sigma\left(\bigcup_{t\geq 0}\mathcal{F}^{0}_{t}\right)
. Then, for any
(x,v)\in E\times\R
, we construct, by using Kolmogorov extension theorem, the unique probability measure
\mathds{P}^{(x,v)}
on
(\Omega,\mathcal{F}^{0}_{\infty})
such that
(M,S)
admits the desired transition probability function and
\mathds{P}^{(x,v)}(M(0)=x,S(0)=v)=1
. Once this is done, we consider the completion
\cF_{\infty}
and
\cF_{t}
, for
t\geq 0
, of
\cF^{0}_{\infty}
and
\cF^{0}_{t}
, for
t\geq 0
, with respect to the measure
\mathds{P}^{(x,v)}
and we use as (family of) filtered probability spaces
(\Omega,\cF_{\infty},\{\cF_{t}\}_{t\geq 0},\mathds{P}^{(x,v)})
. In such a case,
(M,S)
is called a canonical Feller process. Now denote
\mathcal{G}_{t}^{0}=\sigma((M^{\phi}(s),{S}(s)),s\leq t)
,
\mathcal{G}_{\infty}^{0}:=\sigma\left(\bigcup_{t\geq 0}\cG_{t}^{0}\right)
and with
\mathcal{G}_{t}
and
\mathcal{G}_{\infty}
their completion with respect to
\mathds{P}^{(x,v)}
. Since the composition is a measurable operator (see
[
13
]
), we know that
\mathcal{G}_{\infty}\subset\mathcal{F}_{\infty}
. Furthermore, by Phillip’s theorem,
(M^{\phi},S)
is a Feller process with respect to the filtration
\cG:=\{\mathcal{G}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
. Before proceeding, let us recall the following definition, as in
[
21
, Definition 1.4]
.

Definition 4.1
.

Consider a probability space
(\Omega,\cF_{\infty},\mathds{P})
and a filtration
\cF:=\{\mathcal{F}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
on it. An
E\times\R^{m}
-valued stochastic process
(X,Y)
is said to be Markov additive with respect to
\cF
if it is
\cF
-Markov and for all
A\in\cE
,
B\in\cB(\R^{m})
and
0\leq s\leq t

\displaystyle P_{s,t}\mathds{1}_{A\times B}(x,y)=P_{s,t}\mathds{1}_{A\times(B-y)}(x,0),

where
B-y=\{z\in\R^{m}:\ z+y\in B\}
and
P_{s,t}
is the two-parameters transition semigroup of
(X,Y)
. Notice that the latter is equivalent to

(4.3)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathds{P}&((X(t),Y(t)-Y(s))\in A\times B\mid X(s),Y(s))\\
&=\mathds{P}((X(t),Y(t)-Y(s))\in A\times B\mid X(s)).\end{split}

We can prove the following result.

Lemma 4.2
.

The process
(M^{\phi},S)
is Markov additive with respect to its augmented natural filtration
\cG
.

Proof.

We already know that
(M^{\phi},S)
is a Feller process with respect to
\cG
, hence we just need to show (
4.3
). Let
s<t
,
A\in\cE
and
B\in\cB(\R)
, let
M^{\phi}(t)
play the role of
X(t)
and
S(t)
play the role of
Y(t)
and observe that, setting
\Delta S=S(t)-S(s)
,

\displaystyle\mathds{P}^{(x,v)}\left((M^{\phi}(t),\Delta S)\in A\times B\mid M^{\phi}(s),S(s)\right)

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\mathds{E}^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{E}^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{A}\left(M^{\phi}(t)\right)\mathds{1}_{B}\left(\Delta S\right)\mid M^{\phi}(s),S(s),\Delta S\right]\mid M^{\phi}(s),S(s)\right]

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\mathds{E}^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{B}\left(\Delta S\right)\mathds{E}^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{A}\left(M^{\phi}(t)\right)\mid M^{\phi}(s),S(s),\Delta S\right]\mid M^{\phi}(s),S(s)\right]

Now recall that
M^{\phi}(t)=M(S(t)-v)=M(\Delta S+S(s)-v)
and
M^{\phi}(s)=M(S(s)-v)
, where
S(s)<S(t)
. Now consider three Borel sets
B_{1}\in\cE
,
B_{2},B_{3}\subset\R
and the event

B^{\star}=\{M^{\phi}(s)\in B_{1}\}\cap\{S(s)\in B_{2}\}\cap\{\Delta S\in B_{3}\}\in\sigma(M^{\phi}(s),S(s),\Delta S).

It holds

\displaystyle\E^{(x,v)}[\mathds{1}_{B^{\star}}\mathds{1}_{A}(M^{\phi}(t))]

\displaystyle=\E^{(x,v)}[\mathds{1}_{B_{1}}(M^{\phi}(s))\mathds{1}_{B_{2}}(S(s))\mathds{1}_{B_{3}}(\Delta S)\mathds{1}_{A}(M(\Delta S+S(s)-v))]

\displaystyle=\E^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{B_{3}}(\Delta S)\E^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{B_{1}}(M^{\phi}(s))\mathds{1}_{B_{2}}(S(s))\mathds{1}_{A}(M(\Delta S+S(s)-v))\mid\Delta S\right]\right].

Once we notice that
\{M(t),\ t\geq 0\}
and
S(s)
are independent of
\Delta S
, if we set

F_{1}(y)=\E^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{B_{1}}(M^{\phi}(s))\mathds{1}_{B_{2}}(S(s))\mathds{1}_{A}(M(y+S(s)-v))\right],

it holds

\E^{(x,v)}[\mathds{1}_{B^{\star}}\mathds{1}_{A}(M^{\phi}(t))]=\E^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{B_{3}}(\Delta S)F_{1}(\Delta S)\right].

However, we can also write

(4.4)

F_{1}(y)=\E^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{B_{1}}(M^{\phi}(s))\mathds{1}_{B_{2}}(S(s))\E^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{A}(M(y+S(s)-v))\mid M^{\phi}(s),S(s)\right]\right].

Let us focus on the inner conditional expectation. Consider again two sets
B_{4}\in\cE
and
B_{5}\in\cE
and the event

B_{\star\star}=\{M^{\phi}(s)\in B_{4}\}\cap\{S(s)\in B_{5}\}.

Then

\displaystyle\E^{(x,v)}

\displaystyle\left[\mathds{1}_{B_{\star\star}}\mathds{1}_{A}(M(y+{S}(s)-v)\right]=\E^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{B_{4}}(M^{\phi}(s))\mathds{1}_{B_{5}}({S}(s))\mathds{1}_{A}(M(y+{S}(s)-v)\right]

\displaystyle=\E^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{B_{5}}(S(s))\E^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{B_{4}}(M^{\phi}(s))\mathds{1}_{A}(M(y+S(s)-v)\mid S(s)\right]\right].

Since
M
and
{S}
are independent, if we set, for
z\geq v
,

\displaystyle F_{2}(z)=\E^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{B_{4}}(M(z-v))\mathds{1}_{A}(M(y+z-v)\right],

it holds

\E^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{B_{\star\star}}\mathds{1}_{A}(M(y+S(s)-v)\right]=\E^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{B_{5}}(S(s))F_{2}(S(s))\right].

However, since
\{M(z-v)\in B_{4}\}\in\sigma(M(z-v))
, we have

\displaystyle F_{2}(z)

\displaystyle=\E^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{B_{4}}(M(z-v))\E^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{A}(M(y+z-v)\mid M(z-v)\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\E^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{B_{4}}(M(z-v))\E^{(M(z-v),v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{A}(M(y))\right]\right].

Hence

\displaystyle\E^{(x,v)}

\displaystyle\left[\mathds{1}_{B_{\star\star}}\mathds{1}_{A}(M(y+S(s)-v)\right]

\displaystyle=\E^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{B_{5}}(S(s))\mathds{1}_{B_{4}}(M^{\phi}(s))\E^{(M^{\phi}(s),v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{A}(M(y))\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\E^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{B_{\star\star}}\E^{(M^{\phi}(s),v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{A}(M(y))\right]\right].

Since the events of the form
B_{\star\star}
constitute a
\pi
-system generating
\sigma(M^{\phi}(s),S(s))
, this shows that

\E^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{A}(M(y+S(s)-v))\mid M^{\phi}(s),S(s)\right]=\E^{(M^{\phi}(s),v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{A}(M(y))\right].

Define

F_{3}(x,y)=\E^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{A}(M(y))\right]

so that going back to (
4.4
) we get

F_{1}(y)=\E^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{B_{1}}(M^{\phi}(s))\mathds{1}_{B_{2}}(S(s))F_{3}(M^{\phi}(s),\Delta S)\right]

and then

\E^{(x,v)}[\mathds{1}_{B^{\star}}\mathds{1}_{A}(M^{\phi}(t))]=\E^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{B^{\star}}F_{3}(M^{\phi}(s),\Delta S)\right],

i.e., since the events of the form
B^{\star}
constitute a
\pi
-system generating
\sigma(M^{\phi}(s),S(s),\Delta S)
,

\E^{(x,v)}[\mathds{1}_{A}(M^{\phi}(t))\mid M^{\phi}(s),S(s),\Delta S]=F_{3}(M^{\phi}(s),\Delta S).

Since
\Delta S
is independent of both
M^{\phi}(s)
and
S(s)
, we get (see Lemma
A.1
)

(4.5)

\mathds{P}^{(x,v)}((M^{\phi}(t),\Delta S)\in A\times B\mid M^{\phi}(s),S(s))=\E^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{B}(\Delta{S})F_{3}(M^{\phi}(s),\Delta S)\mid M^{\phi}(s)\right].

Use now (
4.5
) to see that

\displaystyle\mathds{P}^{(x,v)}

\displaystyle((M^{\phi}(t),\Delta S)\in A\times B\mid M^{\phi}(s))

\displaystyle=\E^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{B}(\Delta S)\mathds{1}_{A}(M^{\phi}({t}))\mid M^{\phi}(s)\right]

\displaystyle=\E^{(x,v)}\left[\E^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{B}(\Delta S)\mathds{1}_{A}(M^{\phi}({t}))\mid M^{\phi}(s),S(s)\right]\mid M^{\phi}(s)\right]

\displaystyle{=\E^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{P}^{(x,v)}((M^{\phi}(t),\Delta S)\in A\times B\mid M^{\phi}(s),S(s))\mid M^{\phi}(s)\right]}

\displaystyle=\E^{(x,v)}\left[\E^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{B}(\Delta S)F_{3}(M^{\phi}(s),\Delta S)\mid M^{\phi}(s)\right]\mid M^{\phi}(s)\right]

\displaystyle=\E^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{B}(\Delta S)F_{3}(M^{\phi}(s),\Delta S)\mid M^{\phi}(s)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathds{P}^{(x,v)}((M^{\phi}(t),\Delta S)\in A\times B\mid M^{\phi}(s),S(s)).\qed

Now we can define the process
X=\left\{X(t),t\geq 0\right\}
where
X(t)=M^{\phi}(L(t)-)
that is, if we remember that
H(t)=S_{0}\left(L(t)-\right)
, we have that
X(t)=M(H(t))
. Notice, in particular, that
X
is a càglàd process. We can highlight quite a special case. Indeed, if
E=\R^{d}
and
M
is a jump-diffusion (as in
[
47
]
), then also
M^{\phi}
is a jump-diffusion by
[
59
, Theorem 32.1]
. Hence, by combining Lemma
4.2
and
[
47
, Theorem 4.1]
, we know that the process
\{(X(t),\gamma(t)),\ t\geq 0\}
is a time-homogeneous simple Markov process, where
\gamma(t)=\max\{t-S(0)-H(t),0\}
. The process
\gamma
is usually referred to as the
age
process, while its right-limits, under
v=0
, coincide with the sojourn time of
X
in the current position, i.e., the r.v.
J_{X}(t):=t-(0\vee\sup\{s\leq t:\ X(s)\neq X(t)\})
, whenever
M
(and thus
M^{\phi}
) does not admit holding points (i.e., points on which
M
is stuck for an exponentially distributed random time). Such processes are related, as observed in
[
47
]
, on the one hand, to the limits of continuous time random walks and, on the other hand, to the theory of semi-Markov processes (see
[
33
]
). It is, however, worth noticing that the fact that the semi-Markov property holds for
X
has not been shown even for
E=\R^{d}
, but the simple Markov property of
(X,\gamma)
is already enough to provide interesting stochastic models. We conjecture that the process
X
is actually semi-Markov even in the general case in which
E
is a locally compact separable Hausdorff space: this problem is deferred to future research.

5.
A coupled fully non-local equation for time-changed Feller processes

In this section, we want to determine the governing evolution equation of the process
X(t)
under the condition
S(0)=0
, relating it with (
3.3
). To do this, we shall consider the case in which
G
is a generator of a Feller semigroup. Let us now dive into the details.

Let
M
be defined as in the previous section and consider its Feller semigroup
\{P_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
on
C_{0}(E)
. Denote by
G
its generator, with domain
{\sf Dom}(G)
. As in Example
2.7
,
(C_{0}(E),C(\R_{0}^{+};C_{0}(E)))
is a uniqueness class for
G
. To get uniqueness for our non-local equation (
3.3
), the condition
q(t,\cdot)\in C_{0}(E)
will not be sufficient: we need this to hold locally uniformly in time.

Definition 5.1
.

Let
u:\R_{0}^{+}\times E\to\R
and
T>0
. We say that

\lim_{x\to\infty}u(t,x)=0\quad\mbox{ uniformly with respect to }t\in[0,T]

if for all
\varepsilon>0
there exists a compact set
K\subset E
such that

\sup_{x\not\in K}|u(t,x)|<\varepsilon,\quad\forall t\in[0,T].

We say that

\lim_{x\to\infty}u(t,x)=0\quad\mbox{ locally uniformly with respect to }t\geq 0

if the same property holds uniformly with respect to
t\in[0,T]
for all
T>0
.

Theorem 5.2
.

Let
\phi\in\mathfrak{SB}_{0}
such that
\left\|\log(\cdot)u^{\phi}(\cdot)\right\|_{L^{1}[0,1]}<\infty
and
M
be a
E
-valued Feller process with semigroup
\{P_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
and generator
(G,{\sf Dom}(G))
. Let also
S
be a real-valued, strictly increasing càdlàg process with independent and stationary increments. Assume further that
S
is independent of
M
and that the subordinator
S_{0}(t)=S(t)-S(0)
is such that
\phi(\lambda)=\log\E^{(x,0)}[e^{-\lambda S_{0}(1)}]
. Let
H
be the undershooting of
S
and
X(t)=M(H(t))
. Finally, suppose that
S_{0}(t)
admits a density
g_{S_{0}}(\cdot;t)
under
\mathds{P}^{(x,0)}
. Then, for all
u\in{\sf Dom}(G)
, the function

q(t,x)=\E^{(x,0)}[u(X(t))]

is the unique solution of

(5.1)

\begin{cases}\phi(\partial_{t}-G)q(t,x)=\overline{\nu}_{\phi}(t)q(0,x),&t>0,\ x\in E,\\
q(0,x)=u(x),&x\in E\\
\lim_{x\to\infty}q(t,x)=0&\mbox{locally uniformly with respect to }t\geq 0,\end{cases}

i.e., it is the unique function
q:\mathbb{R}_{0}^{+}\times E\mapsto\mathbb{R}
that satisfies Definition
3.6
and the limit condition
\lim_{x\to+\infty}q(t,x)=0
locally uniformly with respect to
t\geq 0
.

Remark 5.3
.

Notice that if
E\subset\R^{d}
is a bounded open set with a suitably regular boundary,
f\in C_{0}(E)
if and only if
f\in C(\overline{E})
with
f(x)=0
on
\partial E
. Furthermore,

\lim_{x\to\infty}q(t,x)=0\quad\mbox{ locally uniformly with respect to }t\geq 0

if and only if
q\in C(\R_{0}^{+}\times\overline{E})
with
q(t,x)=0
for all
x\in\partial E
. In particular (
5.1
) becomes

(5.2)

\begin{cases}\phi(\partial_{t}-G)q(t,x)=\overline{\nu}_{\phi}(t)q(0,x),&t>0,\ x\in E,\\
q(0,x)=u(x),&x\in E,\\
q(t,x)=0&x\in\partial E,\end{cases}

i.e., it is a non-local Cauchy-Dirichlet problem. From a purely analytical point of view and in the special case
\phi(\lambda)=\lambda^{\alpha}
and
G=\Delta
these problems have been studied in
[
5
]
in connection with the obstacle problem for the fractional Laplacian and in
[
28
]
with a non-linear term. Also in
[
16
]
, equations of these forms are considered and Hölder estimates are provided.

In order to prove Theorem
5.2
, we need some preliminary regularity estimates on the function
q
to verify the hypotheses of Theorems
3.2
and
3.3
.

5.1.
Regularity of
q

First of all, in order to guarantee that we can apply Theorems
3.2
and
3.3
to the function
q
, we show that it satisfies the assumptions of both theorems.
This requires some smoothness properties. We start by proving that, for fixed
x\in E
,
q(\cdot,x)
is absolutely continuous.

Proposition 5.4
.

Under the hypotheses of Theorem
5.2
, for all
x\in E
the function
q(\cdot,x)
belongs to
{\rm AC}(\R_{0}^{+})
with a.e. derivative
\partial_{t}q(\cdot,x)
satisfying, for almost all
t\in[0,T]
,

(5.3)

|\partial_{t}q(t,x)|\leq C(\left\|Gu\right\|_{C(E)}+\left\|u\right\|_{C(E)})u^{\phi}(t)

for a suitable constant
C>0
(which is independent on
x\in E
). Remember that
u^{\phi}(t)
denotes the potential density.

Proof.

Consider the Feller semigroup
(\widetilde{P}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
acting on any function
w\in C_{0}(E\times\R)
as

\widetilde{P}_{t}w(x,s)=\E^{(x,0)}[w(M(t),t+s)].

Let
\widetilde{A}
be the generator of
(\widetilde{P}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
. By Phillips theorem (see
[
64
, Theorem 12.6]
), we obtain that the generator of
(M^{\phi},S)
(which is obtained by subordinating
\widetilde{P}
by means of the subordinator
S_{0}
) is given, on functions
w\in{\sf Dom}(\widetilde{A})
, by

\widetilde{A}^{\phi}w=\int_{0}^{\infty}(\widetilde{P}_{s}w-w)\nu_{\phi}(ds)

where the integral is meant in the sense of Bochner.
Now let
u\in{\sf Dom}(G)
and consider, for any
N\in\N
, a cut-off function of
[-N,N]
, i.e.
\eta_{N}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\R)
such that
\eta_{N}(t)=1
for
t\in[-N,N]
,
\eta_{N}(t)=0
for
t\in[-N-1,N+1]
and
\eta_{N}(t)\in[0,1]
for all
t\in\R
. Without loss of generality, we can assume that
\left\|\frac{d}{dt}\eta_{N}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\R)}\leq C_{\eta}
, where
C_{\eta}
is independent of
N
. We define
u_{N}(x,t)=u(x)\eta_{N}(t)
. Let us show that
u_{N}\in{\sf Dom}(\widetilde{A})
. Indeed,

\displaystyle\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{\widetilde{P}_{h}u_{N}(x,t)-u_{N}(x,t)}{h}

\displaystyle=\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{\E^{(x,0)}[u(M(h))\mid M(0)=x]\eta_{N}(t+h)-u(x)\eta_{N}(t)}{h}

\displaystyle=\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{\E^{(x,0)}[u(M(h))\mid M(0)=x]-u(x)}{h}\eta_{N}(t+h)+u(x)\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{\eta_{N}(t+h)-\eta_{N}(t)}{h}

\displaystyle=Gu(x)\eta_{N}(t)+u(x)\frac{d}{dt}\eta_{N}(t).

Since the pointwise generator coincides with the classical one (see
[
14
, Theorem 1.33]
), then this proves that
u_{N}\in{\sf Dom}(\widetilde{A})
and

(5.4)

\widetilde{A}u_{N}(x,t)=Gu(x)\eta_{N}(t)+u(x)\frac{d}{dt}\eta_{N}(t)\ \forall x\in E,\ \forall t\in\R.

By Phillips theorem, we know that
u_{N}\in{\sf Dom}(\widetilde{A}^{\phi})
and in particular

\displaystyle\widetilde{A}^{\phi}u_{N}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}(\widetilde{P}_{s}u_{N}-u_{N})\nu_{\phi}(ds)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{s}\widetilde{P}_{\tau}\widetilde{A}u_{N}d\tau\,\nu_{\phi}(ds)+\int_{1}^{\infty}(\widetilde{P}_{s}u_{N}-u_{N})\nu_{\phi}(ds).

By Bochner theorem, see
[
2
, Theorem 1.1.4]
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\widetilde{A}^{\phi}u_{N}\right\|_{C(E\times\R)}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{s}\left\|\widetilde{P}_{\tau}\widetilde{A}u_{N}\right\|_{C(E\times\R)}d\tau\,\nu_{\phi}(ds)

\displaystyle+\int_{1}^{\infty}\left\|\widetilde{P}_{s}u_{N}-u_{N}\right\|_{C(E\times\R)}\nu_{\phi}(ds)

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\widetilde{A}u_{N}\right\|_{C(E\times\R)}\mathcal{J}_{\phi}(1)+2\left\|u_{N}\right\|_{C(E\times\R)}\overline{\nu}_{\phi}(1),

where
\mathcal{J}_{\phi}(1)=\int_{0}^{1}s\nu_{\phi}(ds).

Using (
5.4
)
and recalling that
\left\|\eta_{N}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\R)}=1
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\widetilde{A}^{\phi}u_{N}\right\|_{C(E\times\R)}

\displaystyle\leq(\left\|Gu\right\|_{C(E)}+\left\|u\right\|_{C(E)}C_{\eta})\mathcal{J}_{\phi}(1)+2\left\|u\right\|_{C(E)}\overline{\nu}_{\phi}(1)

\displaystyle\leq C(\left\|Gu\right\|_{C(E)}+\left\|u\right\|_{C(E)}),

for some suitable constant
C
that is independent of
N
. Now let

(5.5)

q_{N}(t,x,s)=\E^{(x,s)}[u_{N}(X(t),S(L(t)-))]=\E^{(x,s)}[u_{N}(M^{\phi}(L(t)-),S(L(t)-))],

observe that
L(t)
is a stopping time with respect to the natural filtration of
(M^{\phi}(\cdot-),S(\cdot-))
and use Dynkin’s formula
[
14
, Equation (1.55)]
to write

q_{N}(t,x,s)=u_{N}(x,s)+\E^{(x,s)}\left[\int_{0}^{L(t)}\widetilde{A}^{\phi}u_{N}(M^{\phi}(\tau),S(\tau))d\tau\right],

where we also used the fact that
S
is stochastically continuous. Now fix
T>0
and consider
t\in\left[0,\frac{3}{2}T\right]
. We extend
q_{N}(t,x,s)
to
t\in\left[-\frac{T}{2},0\right]
by setting
q_{N}(t,x,s)={u_{N}}(x,s)
. Let
h\in\R
with
0<|h|<\frac{T}{2}
and define for any
\varphi:\left[-\frac{T}{2},\frac{3}{2}T\right]\to\R
the quantity

D^{h}\varphi(t):=\frac{\varphi(t+h)-\varphi(t)}{h}.

We argue first for
h>0
. Observe that, by (
5.5
), for almost all
t\in\left[0,T\right]
.

\displaystyle\left|D^{h}q_{N}(t,x,0)\right|

\displaystyle\leq h^{-1}\E^{(x,0)}\left[\int_{L(t)}^{L(t+h)}|\widetilde{A}^{\phi}u_{N}(M^{\phi}(\tau),S(\tau))|d\tau\right]

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\widetilde{A}^{\phi}u_{N}\right\|_{C(E\times\R)}h^{-1}\E^{(x,0)}[L(t+h)-L(t)]

\displaystyle\leq C(\left\|Gu\right\|_{C(E)}+\left\|u\right\|_{C(E)})h^{-1}(U^{\phi}(t+h)-U^{\phi}(t))

\displaystyle\leq C(\left\|Gu\right\|_{C(E)}+\left\|u\right\|_{C(E)})u^{\phi}(t).

If
h<0
instead, let us distinguish among two cases. If
|h|\leq\frac{t}{2}
then we have

\left|D^{h}q_{N}(t,x,0)\right|\leq C(\left\|Gu\right\|_{C(E)}+\left\|u\right\|_{C(E)})\frac{U^{\phi}(t)-U^{\phi}(t+h)}{|h|}\leq C(\left\|Gu\right\|_{C(E)}+\left\|u\right\|_{C(E)})u^{\phi}\left(\frac{t}{2}\right).

If, instead,
|h|\in\left[\frac{t}{2},t\right)
, then

\left|D^{h}q_{N}(t,x,0)\right|\leq C(\left\|Gu\right\|_{C(E)}+\left\|u\right\|_{C(E)})\frac{U^{\phi}(t)-U^{\phi}(t+h)}{|h|}\leq 2C(\left\|Gu\right\|_{C(E)}+\left\|u\right\|_{C(E)})\frac{U^{\phi}\left(t\right)}{t}.

Finally, if
|h|>t
, we get

\left|D^{h}q_{N}(t,x,0)\right|\leq C(\left\|Gu\right\|_{C(E)}+\left\|u\right\|_{C(E)})\frac{U^{\phi}(t)}{|h|}\leq C(\left\|Gu\right\|_{C(E)}+\left\|u\right\|_{C(E)})\frac{U^{\phi}\left(t\right)}{t}.

Hence, we have in general (for any
h\in\R
)

\left|D^{h}q_{N}(t,x,0)\right|\leq C(\left\|Gu\right\|_{C(E)}+\left\|u\right\|_{C(E)})\left(u^{\phi}(t)+u^{\phi}\left(\frac{t}{2}\right)+\frac{U^{\phi}(t)}{t}\right).

Now, since
u\in C_{0}(E)
and
\left\|\eta_{N}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\R)}=1
, we can use the dominated convergence theorem to take the limit as
N\to\infty
and get

(5.6)

\left|D^{h}q(t,x)\right|\leq C(\left\|Gu\right\|_{C(E)}+\left\|u\right\|_{C(E)})\left(u^{\phi}(t)+u^{\phi}\left(\frac{t}{2}\right)+\frac{U^{\phi}(t)}{t}\right).

We now use a suitable modification of
[
24
, Theorem 5.8.3]
in the case of
L^{1}
functions. For completeness we give here all the details of the proof. Consider any sequence
h_{n}\to 0
, fix
x\in{E}
and set, for simplicity,
g_{n}(t)=D^{h_{n}}q(t,x)
for
t\in[0,T]
. Before proceeding, notice that for any
T>0
it holds

\int_{0}^{T}\frac{U^{\phi}(t)}{t}\,dt=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{u^{\phi}(s)}{t}\,ds\,dt=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{s}^{T}\frac{u^{\phi}(s)}{t}\,dt\,ds=\log(T)U^{\phi}(T)-\int_{0}^{T}\log(s)u^{\phi}(s)\,ds<\infty,

where the last inequality follows by assumption. Set then
g^{\phi}(t)=u^{\phi}(t)+u^{\phi}\left(\frac{t}{2}\right)+\frac{U^{\phi}(t)}{t}
and notice that
g^{\phi}\in L^{1}_{\rm loc}(\R_{0}^{+})
and
g_{n}(t)\leq C(\left\|Gu\right\|_{C(E)}+\left\|u\right\|_{C(E)})g^{\phi}(t)
for all
t\geq 0
. We have then

\left\|g_{n}\right\|_{L^{1}[0,T]}\leq C(\left\|Gu\right\|_{C(E)}+\left\|u\right\|_{C(E)})\left\|g^{\phi}\right\|_{L^{1}[0,T]},\ \forall n\in\N.

Furthermore, since
g^{\phi}\in L^{1}[0,T]
, we know that for any
\varepsilon>0
there exists
\delta>0
such that for all measurable
A\subset[0,T]
such that
|A|<\delta
it holds

\int_{A}g_{n}(t)dt\leq C(\left\|Gu\right\|_{C(E)}+\left\|u\right\|_{C(E)})\int_{A}g^{\phi}(t)\,dt<\varepsilon,\ \forall n\in\N,

that implies that the sequence
\{g_{n}\}_{n\in\N}
is uniformly integrable in
[0,T]
. Hence, by Dunford-Pettis theorem
[
15
, Theorem 4.30]
we know that there exists a function
q\in L^{1}(0,T)
such that
g_{n}\upharpoonright g
. Next, let
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(0,T)
and observe that

\int_{0}^{T}D^{h}q(t,x)\varphi(t)dt=\int_{0}^{\frac{3}{2}T}D^{h}q(t,x)\varphi(t)dt=-\int_{0}^{\frac{3}{2}T}q(t,x)D^{-h}\varphi(t)dt

and, in particular,

\int_{0}^{T}g_{n}(t)\varphi(t)dt=-\int_{0}^{\frac{3}{2}T}q(t,x)D^{-{h_{n}}}\varphi(t)dt.

Taking the limit as
n\to+\infty
we have

\int_{0}^{T}g(t)\varphi(t)dt=-\int_{0}^{\frac{3}{2}T}q(t,x)\varphi^{\prime}(t)dt=-\int_{0}^{T}q(t,x)\varphi^{\prime}(t)dt.

It follows that
q(\cdot,x)\in W^{1,1}[0,T]
, where
W^{1,1}[0,T]
is the usual Sobolev space, with weak derivative
\partial_{t}q(t,x)=g(t)
. In particular, we recall that any function
W^{1,1}[0,T]
admits an absolutely continuous version. Since
q(\cdot,x)
is continuous, then it is absolutely continuous with a.e. derivative
\partial_{t}q(\cdot,x)
. Finally, notice that for
h>0
we actually have

\left|D^{h}q(t,x)\right|\leq C(\left\|Gu\right\|_{C(E)}+\left\|u\right\|_{C(E)})u^{\phi}(t)

and taking the limit as
h\to 0^{+}
we get the statement.
∎

Thanks to the previous result, now we can prove that
q
satisfies Items (i) of Theorem
3.2
and (i) of Theorem
3.3
.

Corollary 5.5
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
5.2
we have that for all
(t,x)\in\R_{0}^{+}\times E
,

\int_{0}^{t}\left\|P_{s}q(t-s,\cdot)-P_{s}q(t,\cdot)\right\|_{C(E)}\,\nu_{\phi}(ds)\leq C(\left\|Gu\right\|_{C(E)}+\left\|u\right\|_{C(E)})

and

\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}\int_{0}^{t}\left\|P_{s}q(t-s,\cdot)-P_{s}q(t,\cdot)\right\|_{C(E)}\,\nu_{\phi}(ds)\,dt\leq\frac{C(\left\|Gu\right\|_{C(E)}+\left\|u\right\|_{C(E)})}{\lambda}

for all
x\in E
and
\lambda>0
.

Proof.

Let us fix
x\in E
and observe that by (
5.3
) we have

\displaystyle\left|q(t-s,x)-q(t,x)\right|\,\leq\,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{s}\left|\partial_{t}q(t-\tau,x)\right|d\tau

(5.7)

\displaystyle\leq\,

\displaystyle C(\left\|Gu\right\|_{C(E)}+\left\|u\right\|_{C(E)})\int_{0}^{s}u^{\phi}(t-\tau)d\tau.

Since
P_{s}
is non-expansive, positive preserving and sub-Markov we have for all
x\in E

(5.8)

\displaystyle\begin{split}|P_{s}q(t-s,x)-P_{s}q(t,x)|&\leq P_{s}\left|q(t-s,\cdot)-q(t,\cdot)\right|(x)\\
&\leq C(\left\|Gu\right\|_{C(E)}+\left\|u\right\|_{C(E)})\int_{0}^{s}u^{\phi}(t-\tau)d\tau\end{split}

hence, taking the supremum, then integrating and using Fubini,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}

\displaystyle\left\|P_{s}q(t-s,\cdot)-P_{s}q(t,\cdot)\right\|_{C(E)}\,\nu_{\phi}(ds)

\displaystyle\leq C(\left\|Gu\right\|_{C(E)}+\left\|u\right\|_{C(E)})\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{s}u^{\phi}(t-\tau)\,d\tau\,\nu_{\phi}(ds)

\displaystyle=C(\left\|Gu\right\|_{C(E)}+\left\|u\right\|_{C(E)})\int_{0}^{t}u^{\phi}(t-\tau)\int_{\tau}^{t}\nu_{\phi}(ds)\,{d\tau}

\displaystyle\leq C(\left\|Gu\right\|_{C(E)}+\left\|u\right\|_{C(E)})\int_{0}^{t}u^{\phi}(t-\tau)\overline{\nu}_{\phi}(\tau)\,d\tau

\displaystyle=C(\left\|Gu\right\|_{C(E)}+\left\|u\right\|_{C(E)}),

where the first equality is due to the fact that
(\tau,s)\in[0,s]\times[0,t]
is equivalent to
(\tau,s)\in[0,t]\times[\tau,t]
.
Furthermore, we have

\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-\lambda t}\int_{0}^{t}\left\|P_{s}q(t-s,\cdot)-P_{s}q(t,\cdot)\right\|_{C(E)}\,\nu_{\phi}(ds)\,dt\leq\frac{C(\left\|Gu\right\|_{C(E)}+\left\|u\right\|_{C(E)})}{\lambda}

for all
\lambda>0
.

∎

Next, we show that
q(t,\cdot)
belongs to
{\sf Dom}(G)
and we control its uniform norm.

Proposition 5.6
.

Under the hypotheses of Theorem
5.2
, for all
t\geq 0
the function
q(t,\cdot)
belongs to
{\sf Dom}(G)
and

(5.9)

\left\|Gq(t,\cdot)\right\|_{C(E)}\leq\left\|Gu\right\|_{C(E)}.

Furthermore
\lim_{x\to\infty}q(t,x)=0
locally uniformly with respect to
t\geq 0
.

Proof.

Observe that, since
H(t)
defined in equation (
4.1
) is independent of
M
, we have

\displaystyle q(t,x)=\E^{(x,0)}[u(X(t))]=

\displaystyle\E^{(x,0)}[\E^{(x,0)}[u(X(t))\mid H(t)]]=\E^{(x,0)}[P_{H(t)}u(x)]

(5.10)

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}P_{\tau}u(x)\bar{\nu}_{\phi}(t-\tau)u^{\phi}(\tau)d\tau,

where we used the fact that
H(t)
admits density
\bar{\nu}_{\phi}(t-\tau)u^{\phi}(\tau)
for
\tau\in(0,t)
, see
[
12
, Lemma 1.10]
. Let us stress that

\int_{0}^{t}\left\|P_{\tau}u\right\|_{C(E)}\bar{\nu}_{\phi}(t-\tau)u^{\phi}(\tau)d\tau\leq\left\|u\right\|_{C(E)},

hence, a simple application of the dominated convergence theorem shows that
q(t,\cdot)\in C(E)
. Next, we prove that

(5.11)

\lim_{x\to\infty}q(t,x)=0\quad\mbox{ locally uniformly with respect to }t\geq 0,

which in turn implies that
q(t,\cdot)\in C_{0}(E)
. To do this, fix
T>0
and observe that
P_{t}u\in C_{0}(E)
for all
t\geq 0
and
(P_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is strongly continuous. Let us extend the semigroup by setting
P_{t}=I
for all
t\leq 0
, in such a way that
(P_{t})_{t\in\R}
is still a strongly continuous family of operators such that
P_{t}u\in C_{0}(E)
for all
t\in\R
. Fix
\varepsilon>0
. For all
t\in[0,T]
let
K(t)
be a compact set such that
\sup_{x\not\in K(t)}|P_{t}u(x)|<\frac{\varepsilon}{2}
. Since
P_{t+h}u
uniformly converges towards
P_{t}u
as
h\to 0
, we know that there exists
\delta(t)>0
such that for any
s\in(t-\delta(t),t+\delta(t))=:I(t)
it holds
\sup_{x\not\in K(t)}|P_{s}u(x)-P_{t}u(x)|<\frac{\varepsilon}{2}
. As a consequence, in particular,
\sup_{x\not\in K(t)}|P_{s}u(x)|<\varepsilon
. Now notice that
\bigcup_{t\in[0,T]}I(t)\supset[0,T]
, where the latter is a compact subset of
\R
, thus
\{I(t)\}_{t\in[0,T]}
is an open covering of
[0,T]
. Hence, we can extract a finite number of such open sets
I(t_{1}),\dots,I(t_{N})
such that
[0,T]\subset\bigcup_{i=1}^{N}I(t_{i})
. Let
K=\bigcup_{i=1}^{N}K(t_{i})
, which is a compact set since it is a finite union of compact sets. Let
s\in[0,T]
. Then there exists
i=1,\dots,N
such that
s\in I(t_{i})
and then

\sup_{x\not\in K}|P_{s}u(x)|\leq\sup_{x\not\in K(t_{i})}|P_{s}u(x)|<\varepsilon.

We have shown that, for all
s\in[0,T]
,

\sup_{x\not\in K}|P_{s}u(x)|<\varepsilon.

Hence, by (
5.20
) we get

\sup_{x\not\in K}|q(t,x)|\leq\int_{0}^{t}\overline{\nu}_{\phi}(t-s)u^{\phi}(s)\sup_{x\not\in K}|P_{s}u(x)|\,ds<\varepsilon\int_{0}^{t}\overline{\nu}_{\phi}(t-s)u^{\phi}(s)\,ds=\varepsilon.

Since
\varepsilon>0
and
T>0
are arbitrary, we proved that
\lim_{x\to\infty}q(t,x)=0
locally uniformly with respect to
t\geq 0
. Now we need to prove that
q(t,\cdot)\in{\sf Dom}(G)
and that (
5.9
) holds. Define

G_{s}=\frac{P_{s}-\mathds{I}}{s},\ s>0,

where
\mathds{I}
is the identity operator on
C_{0}(E)
. Setting
Q:\R_{0}^{+}\to C_{0}(E)
as
Q(t)=q(t,\cdot)
for
t\in\R_{0}^{+}
, we have

Q(t)=\int_{0}^{t}P_{\tau}u\bar{\nu}_{\phi}(t-\tau)u^{\phi}(\tau)d\tau.

Thus,

\displaystyle G_{s}Q(t)=G_{s}\int_{0}^{t}P_{\tau}u\,\bar{\nu}_{\phi}(t-\tau)u^{\phi}(\tau)d\tau=\,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}G_{s}P_{\tau}u\,\bar{\nu}_{\phi}(t-\tau)u^{\phi}(\tau)d\tau

(5.12)

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}P_{\tau}G_{s}u\,\bar{\nu}_{\phi}(t-\tau)u^{\phi}(\tau)d\tau,

where we used
[
2
, Proposition 1.1.6]
and the fact that
G_{s}
and
P_{\tau}
commute on
{\sf Dom}(G)
. Now, since
u\in{\sf Dom}(G)
, we have (see definition
2.8
)

G_{s}u=\frac{1}{s}\int_{0}^{s}P_{w}Gudw

and in particular
\left\|G_{s}u\right\|_{C(E)}\leq\left\|Gu\right\|_{C(E)}
. Furthermore, since
P_{\tau}
is contractive, we have

\left\|P_{\tau}G_{s}u\,\bar{\nu}_{\phi}(t-\tau)u^{\phi}(\tau)\right\|_{C(E)}\leq\bar{\nu}_{\phi}(t-\tau)u^{\phi}(\tau)\left\|Gu\right\|_{C(E)},

where the right-hand side is integrable in
[0,t]
and independent of
s
. Hence, taking the limit as
s\to 0
in (
5.12
), using the dominated convergence theorem for Bochner integrals
[
2
, Theorem 1.1.8]
and the fact that
P_{\tau}
is continuous, we get

\lim_{s\to 0}G_{s}Q(t)=\int_{0}^{t}P_{\tau}Gu\,\bar{\nu}_{\phi}(t-\tau)u^{\phi}(\tau)d\tau,

i.e.
q(t,\cdot)\in{\sf Dom}(G)
for all
t\geq 0
and

Gq(t,\cdot)=\int_{0}^{t}P_{\tau}Gu(\cdot)\,\bar{\nu}_{\phi}(t-\tau)u^{\phi}(\tau)d\tau.

Finally, again by Bochner theorem, the contractivity of
P_{\tau}
and the fact that
(\bar{\nu}_{\phi},u^{\phi})
is a Sonine pair, we have

\left\|Gq(t,\cdot)\right\|_{C(E)}\leq\left\|Gu\right\|_{C(E)}.

∎

Since
q(t,\cdot)\in{\sf Dom}(G)
, one has
Gq(t,\cdot)\in C_{0}\left(E\right)
. Hence, we get the following corollary, which guarantees that Items (ii) of Theorem
3.2
and (ii) of Theorem
3.3
hold.

Corollary 5.7
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
5.2
we have, for all
(t,x)\in\R^{+}\times E

\int_{0}^{t}\left\|P_{s}q(t,\cdot)-q(t,\cdot)\right\|_{C(E)}\,\nu_{\phi}(ds)\leq\left\|Gu\right\|_{C(E)}\mathcal{J}_{\phi}(t)

where
\mathcal{J}_{\phi}(t)=\int_{0}^{t}s\nu_{\phi}(ds)
and, for all
\lambda>0
and
x\in E
,

\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-\lambda t}\int_{0}^{t}\left\|P_{s}q(t,\cdot)-q(t,\cdot)\right\|_{C(E)}\,\nu_{\phi}(ds)\,dt<\infty.

Proof.

Since
q(t,\cdot)\in{\sf Dom}(G)\subset C_{0}(E)
, we have

P_{s}q(t,x)-q(t,x)=\int_{0}^{s}GP_{\tau}q(t,x)\,d\tau=\int_{0}^{s}P_{\tau}Gq(t,x)\,d\tau.

Taking the absolute value, using again the fact that
P_{\tau}
is non-expansive, positivity preserving and sub-Markov,

(5.13)

|P_{s}q(t,x)-q(t,x)|\leq\int_{0}^{s}|P_{\tau}Gq(t,x)|\,d\tau\leq\int_{0}^{s}P_{\tau}|Gq(t,x)|\,d\tau\leq\left\|Gq(t,\cdot)\right\|_{C(E)}s\leq\left\|Gu\right\|_{C(E)}s.

Hence, taking the supremum and then integrating,

\int_{0}^{t}\left\|P_{s}q(t,\cdot)-q(t,\cdot)\right\|_{C(E)}\,\nu_{\phi}(ds)\leq\left\|Gu\right\|_{C(E)}\mathcal{J}_{\phi}(t)

and it follows by (
2.7
), that
\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-\lambda t}\mathcal{J}_{\phi}(t)\,dt<\infty
for all
\lambda>0
.
∎

Next, we need to show that Item (iii) of Theorem
3.3
holds. Next proposition follows once we observe that
|q(t,x)|\leq\left\|u\right\|_{C(E)}
for all
t\geq 0
and
x\in E
.

Proposition 5.8
.

Under the hypotheses of Theorem
5.2
the following inequality holds, for all
\lambda>0
and
x\in E

\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-\lambda t}\overline{\nu}_{\phi}(t)q(t,x)dt\leq\frac{\phi(\lambda)}{\lambda}\left\|u\right\|_{C(E)}.

5.2.
Proof of Theorem
5.2

Proof.

First, notice that, by the discussion in Subsection
5.1
, we know that we are under the hypotheses of Theorems
3.2
and
3.3
. Hence, observe that by Theorem
3.2
,
\phi(\partial_{t}-G)q(t,x)
is well-defined for all
x\in E
and
t>0
. Furthermore, by Theorem
3.3
, we know that for all
x\in E
the function
\phi(\partial_{t}-G)q(\cdot,x)
is Laplace transformable for any
\lambda>0
.
Consider that
q(0,x)=u(x)
by definition. Next, observe that, for
\lambda>0
, the Laplace transform of the right-hand side of the first equation in (
5.1
) is given by

\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}\overline{\nu}_{\phi}(t)q(0,x)dt=\frac{\phi(\lambda)}{\lambda}q(0,x),

where
q(0,x)
does not play any role in the determination of the aforementioned Laplace transform. Hence, we need to prove that for all
x\in E

\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}\phi(\partial_{t}-G)q(t,x)dt=\frac{\phi(\lambda)}{\lambda}q(0,x){=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}\overline{\nu}_{\phi}(t)q(0,x)dt},

so that the injectivity of the Laplace transform then yields the first equation for fixed
x\in E
and almost all
t>0
.
For this, first observe that by Theorem
3.3
we have

\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}\phi(\partial_{t}-G)q(t,x)dt=-\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}(P_{s}q(t-s,x)\mathds{1}_{[0,t]}(s)-q(t,x))dt\,\nu_{\phi}(ds).

Set
Q
as in the proof of Proposition
5.6
. Then we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}P_{s}Q(t-s)\mathds{1}_{[0,t]}(s)dt

\displaystyle=P_{s}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}Q(t-s)\mathds{1}_{[s,+\infty)}(t)dt

\displaystyle=P_{s}\int_{s}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}Q(t-s)dt

\displaystyle=e^{-\lambda s}P_{s}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}Q(t)dt:=e^{-\lambda s}P_{s}\widetilde{Q}(\lambda),

where the first equality is due to the fact that
P_{s}
does not depend on
t
and that
s\in[0,t]
is equivalent to
t\in[s,\infty)
and where all the integrals are Bochner integrals on
C_{0}(E)
. Hence, in particular, the equality holds for all
x\in E
and then we get

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}P_{s}q(t-s,x)\mathds{1}_{[0,t]}(s)dt=e^{-\lambda s}P_{s}\widetilde{q}(\lambda,x),

where
\widetilde{q}(\lambda,x)=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}q(t,x)dt
. This implies

(5.14)

\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}\phi(\partial_{t}-G)q(t,x)dt=-\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(e^{-\lambda s}P_{s}\widetilde{q}(\lambda,x)-\widetilde{q}(\lambda,x)\right)\nu_{\phi}(ds).

Now we need to evaluate
\widetilde{q}(\lambda,x)
. First, we use Fubini’s theorem to write

\widetilde{q}(\lambda,x)=\E^{(x,0)}\left[\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-\lambda t}u(X(t))\,dt\right].

Then, we recall that
S
performs at most countably many jumps that are a.s. dense in
[0,+\infty)
and it increases continuously on a set with Lebesgue measure zero (see
[
59
, Chapter 4, Section 21]
) and also that
H(t)=S(y-)
for any
t\in(S(y-),S(y)]
, so that

\displaystyle\widetilde{q}(\lambda,x)

\displaystyle=\E^{(x,0)}\left[\sum_{y\geq 0}\int_{S(y-)}^{S(y)}e^{-\lambda t}u(M(S(y-)))dt\right]

\displaystyle=\E^{(x,0)}\left[\sum_{y\geq 0}\frac{e^{-\lambda S(y-)}-e^{-\lambda S(y)}}{\lambda}u(M(S(y-)))\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda}\E^{(x,0)}\left[\sum_{y\geq 0}e^{-\lambda S(y-)}(1-e^{-\lambda(S(y)-S(y-))})u(M(S(y-)))\right]

Now observe that all the previous argument can be applied to
u_{\pm}(x)=\max\{0,\pm u(x)\}
, so that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\lambda}\E^{(x,0)}\left[\sum_{y\geq 0}e^{-\lambda S(y-)}(1-e^{-\lambda(S(y)-S(y-))})u_{\pm}(M(S(y-)))\right]

(5.15)

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-\lambda t}\E^{x}[u_{\pm}(X(t))]dt<\infty.

Consider the
C_{\rm b}(\R_{0}^{+})
-valued stochastic processes:
Z_{\pm}:=\{Z_{\pm}(y),y\geq 0\}
defined, for all
y,z\geq 0
, as

Z_{\pm}(y)(z)=u_{\pm}(M(S(y-)))e^{-\lambda S(y-)}(1-e^{-\lambda z}).

Notice that
Z_{\pm}
is left-continuous, i.e. for all
y>0
and
y_{n}\uparrow y
it holds

\left\|Z_{\pm}(y_{n})-Z_{\pm}(y)\right\|_{C(E)}\leq|u_{\pm}(M(S(y-)))e^{-\lambda S(y-)}-u_{\pm}(M(S(y_{n}-)))e^{-\lambda S(y_{n}-)}|\to 0

as
n\to\infty
, hence it is a predictable process
Furthermore, observe that
\mathfrak{p}=\{\mathfrak{p}(y),\ y\geq 0\}
with
\mathfrak{p}(y)=S(y)-S(y-)
is a Poisson point process of intensity
\nu_{\phi}
. Hence, by the compensation formula (see
[
11
, Page
7
]
), we get

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\lambda}

\displaystyle\E^{(x,0)}\left[\sum_{y\geq 0}e^{-\lambda S(y-)}(1-e^{-\lambda(S(y)-S(y-))})u_{\pm}(M(S(y-)))\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda}\E^{(x,0)}\left[\int_{0}^{+\infty}\left(\int_{0}^{+\infty}(1-e^{-\lambda w})\nu_{\phi}(dw)\right)u_{\pm}(M(S(y-)))e^{-\lambda S(y-)}dy\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\phi(\lambda)}{\lambda}\E^{(x,0)}\left[\int_{0}^{+\infty}u_{\pm}(M(S(y-)))e^{-\lambda S(y-)}dy\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\phi(\lambda)}{\lambda}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\E^{(x,0)}\left[u_{\pm}(M(S(y-)))e^{-\lambda S(y-)}\right]dy

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\phi(\lambda)}{\lambda}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\E^{(x,0)}\left[u_{\pm}(M(S(y)))e^{-\lambda S(y)}\right]dy,

where in the last equality we used the fact that
S
is stochastically continuous and independent of
M
. Since the left-hand side of the previous equality is finite, so it is the right-hand side. Hence, subtracting term to term and using the fact that
u(x)=u_{+}(x)-u_{-}(x)
according to the definition of
u_{\pm}
, we get

\displaystyle\widetilde{q}(\lambda,x)=\frac{1}{\lambda}

\displaystyle\E^{(x,0)}\left[\sum_{y\geq 0}e^{-\lambda S(y-)}(1-e^{-\lambda(S(y)-S(y-))})u(M(S(y-)))\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\phi(\lambda)}{\lambda}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\E^{(x,0)}\left[u(M(S(y)))e^{-\lambda S(y)}\right]dy

\displaystyle=\frac{\phi(\lambda)}{\lambda}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-\lambda\tau}\E^{(x,0)}\left[u(M(\tau))\right]g_{S}(\tau;y)d\tau\,dy,

where
g_{S}(\tau;y)d\tau
is the law of
S(y)
under
\mathds{P}^{(x,0)}
and we used the independence of
M
and
S
. Substituting this equality into (
5.14
) we get

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}

\displaystyle\phi(\partial_{t}-G)q(t,x)dt

\displaystyle=-\frac{\phi(\lambda)}{\lambda}\int_{0}^{\infty}\left[e^{-\lambda s}P_{s}\left(\int_{0}^{+\infty}\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-\lambda\tau}\E^{(\cdot,0)}\left[u(M(\tau))\right]g_{S}(\tau;y)d\tau\,dy\right)(x)\right.

\displaystyle-\left.\int_{0}^{+\infty}\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-\lambda\tau}\E^{(x,0)}\left[u(M(\tau))\right]g_{S}(\tau;y)d\tau\,dy\right]\nu_{\phi}(ds).

Now observe that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{+\infty}

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-\lambda\tau}\left\|\E^{(\cdot,0)}[u(M(\tau))]\right\|_{C(E)}g_{S}(\tau;y)d\tau\,dy

\displaystyle\leq\left\|u\right\|_{C(E)}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-\lambda\tau}g_{S}(\tau;y)d\tau\,dy

\displaystyle=\left\|u\right\|_{C(E)}\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-y\phi(\lambda)}dy=\frac{\left\|u\right\|_{C(E)}}{\phi(\lambda)}<\infty,

hence, by
[
2
, Proposition 1.1.6]
and recalling that Bochner integrability in
C_{0}(E)
implies Lebesgue integrability for fixed
x\in E
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}

\displaystyle\phi(\partial_{t}-G)q(t,x)dt

\displaystyle=-\frac{\phi(\lambda)}{\lambda}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\left(e^{-\lambda(s+\tau)}P_{s}\E^{(\cdot,0)}\left[u(M(\tau))\right](x)\right.

(5.16)

\displaystyle\qquad-\left.e^{-\lambda\tau}\E^{(x,0)}\left[u(M(\tau))\right]\right)g_{S}(\tau;y)d\tau\,dy\nu_{\phi}(ds).

Next, we want to use Fubini theorem. To do this, we first observe that
\E^{(\cdot,0)}[u(M(\tau))]\in{\sf Dom}(G)
. Furthermore, the semigroup
\{\widetilde{P}_{s}\}_{s\geq 0}
where
\widetilde{P}_{s}=e^{-\lambda s}P_{s}
admits
G-\lambda I
as generator, with domain
{\sf Dom}(G)
. Hence,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{+\infty}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\left|e^{-\lambda(s+\tau)}P_{s}\E^{(\cdot,0)}\left[u(M(\tau))\right](x)-e^{-\lambda\tau}\E^{(x,0)}\left[u(M(\tau))\right]\right|g_{S}(\tau;y)d\tau\,dy\nu_{\phi}(ds)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-\lambda\tau}\left|\int_{0}^{s}\widetilde{P}_{w}(G-\lambda I)\E^{(\cdot,0)}\left[u(M(\tau))\right](x)dw\right|g_{S}(\tau;y)d\tau\,dy\nu_{\phi}(ds)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\int_{0}^{s}e^{-\lambda\tau}\left|\widetilde{P}_{w}(G-\lambda I)\E^{(\cdot,0)}\left[u(M(\tau))\right](x)\right|dw\,g_{S}(\tau;y)d\tau\,dy\,\nu_{\phi}(ds)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\int_{0}^{s}e^{-\lambda\tau}\left\|\widetilde{P}_{w}(G-\lambda I)\E^{(\cdot,0)}\left[u(M(\tau))\right]\right\|_{C(E)}dw\,g_{S}(\tau;y)d\tau\,dy\,\nu_{\phi}(ds)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-\lambda\tau}\left\|(G-\lambda I)\E^{(\cdot,0)}\left[u(M(\tau))\right]\right\|_{C(E)}\left(\int_{0}^{s}e^{-\lambda w}dw\right)g_{S}(\tau;y)d\tau\,dy\,\nu_{\phi}(ds)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\left\|Gu\right\|_{C(E)}+\lambda\left\|u\right\|_{C(E)}}{\lambda}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\int_{0}^{+\infty}{\int_{0}^{+\infty}}e^{-\lambda\tau}(1-e^{-\lambda s})g_{S}(\tau;y)d\tau\,dy\,\nu_{\phi}(ds)

\displaystyle=\frac{\left\|Gu\right\|_{C(E)}+\lambda\left\|u\right\|_{C(E)}}{\lambda}<\infty.

Hence, by Fubini theorem, changing the order of the integrals in (
5.16
), we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}

\displaystyle\phi(\partial_{t}-G)q(t,x)dt

\displaystyle=-\frac{\phi(\lambda)}{\lambda}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\left(e^{-\lambda(s+\tau)}P_{s}\E^{(\cdot,0)}\left[u(M(\tau))\right](x)\right.

\displaystyle\qquad-\left.e^{-\lambda\tau}\E^{(x,0)}\left[u(M(\tau))\right]\right)\left(\int_{0}^{+\infty}g_{S}(\tau;y)dy\right)\,d\tau\nu_{\phi}(ds)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\phi(\lambda)}{\lambda}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\left(e^{-\lambda(s+\tau)}P_{s}\E^{(\cdot,0)}\left[u(M(\tau))\right](x)\right.

\displaystyle\qquad-\left.e^{-\lambda\tau}\E^{(x,0)}\left[u(M(\tau))\right]\right)u^{\phi}(\tau)\,d\tau\nu_{\phi}(ds),

where we used the relation
u^{\phi}(\tau)=\int_{0}^{+\infty}g_{S}(\tau;y)dy
. Again, since
\E^{(\cdot,0)}[u(M(\tau))]\in{\sf Dom}(G)
, we can write
