\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}

\displaystyle\phi(\partial_{t}-G)q(t,x)dt

\displaystyle=-\frac{\phi(\lambda)}{\lambda}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\int_{0}^{s}e^{-\lambda(\tau+w)}(G-\lambda)P_{w}\E^{(\cdot,0)}\left[u(M(\tau))\right](x)u^{\phi}(\tau)dw\,d\tau\nu_{\phi}(ds).

Next, we use again Fubini theorem and we get, by observing that
s>w
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}

\displaystyle\phi(\partial_{t}-G)q(t,x)dt

\displaystyle=-\frac{\phi(\lambda)}{\lambda}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\int_{w}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda(\tau+w)}(G-\lambda)P_{w}\E^{(\cdot,0)}\left[u(M(\tau))\right](x)u^{\phi}(\tau)\nu_{\phi}(ds)\,dw\,d\tau

\displaystyle=-\frac{\phi(\lambda)}{\lambda}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-\lambda(\tau+w)}(G-\lambda)P_{w}\E^{(\cdot,0)}\left[u(M(\tau))\right](x)u^{\phi}(\tau)\overline{\nu}_{\phi}(w)\,dw\,d\tau

(5.17)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\phi(\lambda)}{\lambda}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-\lambda(\tau+w)}(G-\lambda)P_{w+\tau}u(x)\,u^{\phi}(\tau)\overline{\nu}_{\phi}(w)\,dw\,d\tau.

Now we set
w+\tau=v
in the inner integral so that (
5.17
) gives us (again, remember that
\tau\in[0,v]
)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}

\displaystyle\phi(\partial_{t}-G)q(t,x)dt

\displaystyle=-\frac{\phi(\lambda)}{\lambda}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\tau}^{+\infty}e^{-\lambda v}(G-\lambda)P_{v}u(x)\,u^{\phi}(\tau)\overline{\nu}_{\phi}(v-\tau)\,dv\,d\tau

\displaystyle=-\frac{\phi(\lambda)}{\lambda}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda v}(G-\lambda)P_{v}u(x)\,\left(\int_{0}^{v}u^{\phi}(\tau)\overline{\nu}_{\phi}(v-\tau)\,d\tau\right)\,dv

\displaystyle=-\frac{\phi(\lambda)}{\lambda}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda v}(G-\lambda)P_{v}u(x)dv

\displaystyle=\frac{\phi(\lambda)}{\lambda}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda v}P_{v}(\lambda-G)u(x)dv

(5.18)

\displaystyle=\frac{\phi(\lambda)}{\lambda}u(x),

where we used the fact that
u^{\phi}
and
\overline{\nu}_{\phi}
constitute a Sonine pair as a consequence of (
4.2
),
P_{v}
and
G
commute on
{\sf Dom}(G)
and

\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda v}P_{v}dv=(\lambda I-G)^{-1}.

Taking the inverse Laplace transform we get that, for all
x\in E
, there exists
I_{x}\subset\R^{+}
, with Lebesgue measure zero, such that for
t\not\in I_{x}
it holds

\phi(\partial_{t}-G)q(t,x)=\overline{\nu}_{\phi}(t)u(x).

Now we need to show that
I_{x}={\varnothing}
for all
x\in E
. To this purpose, first denote by
\mathcal{R}=\overline{\{{S}(t),\ t\geq 0\}}
the topological closure of the range of
S
. Since
H
can only jump when
t\in\mathcal{R}
and it is isolated on the left,

(5.19)

\mathds{P}^{(x,0)}(H(t)\not=H(t+))\leq\mathds{P}^{(x,0)}(t\in\mathcal{R})=0

by
[
12
, Proposition 1.9]
, since
\phi\in\mathfrak{SB}_{0}
. Once this has been established, notice that one can rewrite, given that
H
and
M
are independent,

q(t,x)=\E^{(x,0)}[u(M(H(t))]=\E^{(x,0)}\left[\E^{(x,0)}\left[u(M(H(t))\mid H(t)\right]\right]\\
=\E^{(x,0)}\left[P_{H(t)}u(x)\right]=\int_{0}^{t}P_{s}u(x)\overline{\nu}_{\phi}(t-s)u^{\phi}(s)\,ds.

Notice, however, that for any two fixed
x_{0},x\in E
it holds

(5.20)

q(t,x)=\int_{0}^{t}P_{s}u(x)\overline{\nu}_{\phi}(t-s)u^{\phi}(s)\,ds=\E^{(x_{0},0)}\left[P_{H(t)}u(x)\right].

Fix
t\in\R_{0}^{+}
and let
t_{n}\to t
. Then it holds

\left\|q(t_{n},\cdot)-q(t,\cdot)\right\|_{C(E)}\leq\E^{(x_{0},0)}\left[\left\|P_{H(t_{n})}u-P_{H(t)}u\right\|_{C(E)}\right].

Notice that
\lim_{n\to\infty}\left\|P_{H(t_{n})}u-P_{H(t)}u\right\|_{C(E)}=0
almost surely by (
5.19
) and the strong continuity of
(P_{t})_{t\geq 0}
. Furthermore,
\left\|P_{H(t_{n})}u-P_{H(t)}u\right\|_{C(E)}\leq 2\left\|u\right\|_{C(E)}
. Hence, by the dominated convergence theorem,

(5.21)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\left\|q(t_{n},\cdot)-q(t,\cdot)\right\|_{C(E)}=0.

In particular, this shows that
q
is continuous in both variables. Next, fix
t\in\R^{+}
and consider a sequence
t_{n}\downarrow t
. Then we have for all
x\in E

\displaystyle|\phi(\partial_{t}-G)q(t_{n},x)

\displaystyle-\phi(\partial_{t}-G)q(t,x)|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{t}|P_{s}q(t-s,x)-q(t,x)-P_{s}q(t_{n}-s,x))+q(t_{n},x)|\nu_{\phi}(ds)

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{t}^{t_{n}}|P_{s}q(t_{n}-s,x)-q(t_{n},x)|\nu_{\phi}(ds)

\displaystyle\qquad+|q(t,x)|(\overline{\nu}_{\phi}(t)-\overline{\nu}_{\phi}(t_{n}))+|q(t,x)-q(t_{n},x)|\overline{\nu}_{\phi}(t_{n}).

Taking the supremum over
x\in E
, this leads to

\displaystyle\left\|\phi(\partial_{t}-G)q(t_{n},\cdot)-\phi(\partial_{t}-G)q(t,\cdot)\right\|_{C(E)}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{t}\left\|P_{s}q(t-s,\cdot)-q(t,\cdot)-P_{s}q(t_{n}-s,\cdot))+q(t_{n},\cdot)\right\|_{C(E)}\nu_{\phi}(ds)

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{t}^{t_{n}}\left\|P_{s}q(t_{n}-s,\cdot)-q(t_{n},\cdot)\right\|_{C(E)}\nu_{\phi}(ds)

\displaystyle\qquad+\left\|q(t,\cdot)\right\|_{C(E)}(\overline{\nu}_{\phi}(t)-\overline{\nu}_{\phi}(t_{n}))+\left\|q(t,\cdot)-q(t_{n},\cdot)\right\|_{C(E)}\overline{\nu}_{\phi}(t_{n})

\displaystyle:=I_{n}+J_{n}+\left\|q(t,\cdot)\right\|_{C(E)}(\overline{\nu}_{\phi}(t)-\overline{\nu}_{\phi}(t_{n}))+\left\|q(t,\cdot)-q(t_{n},\cdot)\right\|_{C(E)}\overline{\nu}_{\phi}(t_{n}).

Concerning
I_{n}
, first notice that

\displaystyle\left\|P_{s}q(t-s,\cdot)-q(t,\cdot)-P_{s}q(t_{n}-s,\cdot))+q(t_{n},\cdot)\right\|_{C(E)}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\left\|P_{s}(q(t-s,\cdot)-q(t_{n}-s,\cdot){\color[rgb]{0,0,1})}\right\|_{C(E)}+\left\|q(t,\cdot)-q(t_{n},\cdot)\right\|_{C(E)}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\left\|q(t-s,\cdot)-q(t_{n}-s,\cdot)\right\|_{C(E)}+\left\|q(t,\cdot)-q(t_{n},\cdot)\right\|_{C(E)},

since
P_{s}
is non-expansive. Hence, taking the limit as
n\to\infty
and using (
5.21
) we have

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\left\|P_{s}q(t-s,\cdot)-q(t,\cdot)-P_{s}q(t_{n}-s,\cdot))+q(t_{n},\cdot)\right\|_{C(E)}=0.

Furthermore,

\displaystyle\left\|P_{s}q(t-s,\cdot)-q(t,\cdot)-P_{s}q(t_{n}-s,\cdot)+q(t_{n},\cdot)\right\|_{C(E)}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\left\|P_{s}q(t-s,\cdot)-P_{s}q(t,\cdot)\right\|_{C(E)}+\left\|P_{s}q(t,\cdot)-q(t,\cdot)\right\|_{C(E)}

\displaystyle+\left\|P_{s}q(t_{n}-s,\cdot)-P_{s}q(t_{n},\cdot)\right\|_{C(E)}+\left\|P_{s}q(t_{n},\cdot)-q(t_{n},\cdot)\right\|_{C(E)}

\displaystyle\leq 2C\left(\left\|Gu\right\|_{C(E)}+\left\|u\right\|_{C(E)}\right)\int_{0}^{s}u^{\phi}(t-\tau)d\tau+2\left\|Gu\right\|_{C(E)}s,

where we used (
5.8
), (
5.13
) and the fact that
u^{\phi}(t_{n}-\tau)\leq u^{\phi}(t-\tau)
since
u^{\phi}
is non-increasing. The right-hand side of the previous inequality is integrable against
\nu_{\phi}(ds)
, hence we can use the dominated convergence theorem to achieve

\lim_{n\to\infty}I_{n}=0.

Concerning
J_{n}
instead, we have

\displaystyle\left\|P_{s}q(t_{n}-s,\cdot)-q(t_{n},\cdot)\right\|_{C(E)}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\left\|P_{s}q(t_{n}-s,\cdot)-P_{s}q(t_{n},\cdot)\right\|_{C(E)}+\left\|P_{s}q(t_{n},\cdot)-q(t_{n},\cdot)\right\|_{C(E)}

\displaystyle\quad\leq C\left(\left\|Gu\right\|_{C(E)}+\left\|u\right\|_{C(E)}\right)\int_{0}^{s}u^{\phi}(t^{n}-\tau)d\tau+\left\|Gu\right\|_{C(E)}s,

where we used again (
5.8
) and (
5.13
). Hence

\displaystyle J_{n}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\left\|Gu\right\|_{C(E)}+\left\|u\right\|_{C(E)}\right)\int_{t}^{t_{n}}\int_{0}^{s}u^{\phi}(t^{n}-\tau)d\tau\nu_{\phi}(ds)+\left\|Gu\right\|_{C(E)}\int_{t}^{t_{n}}s\nu_{\phi}(ds)

\displaystyle=C\left(\left\|Gu\right\|_{C(E)}+\left\|u\right\|_{C(E)}\right)J_{n}^{1}+\left\|Gu\right\|_{C(E)}(\mathcal{J}_{\phi}(t_{n})-\mathcal{J}_{\phi}(t)).

By (
2.7
), it turns out that
\mathcal{J}_{\phi}
is right-continuous. Furthermore

\displaystyle J_{n}^{1}

\displaystyle=\int_{t}^{t_{n}}\int_{0}^{t}u^{\phi}(t^{n}-\tau)d\tau\nu_{\phi}(ds)+\int_{t}^{t_{n}}\int_{t}^{s}u^{\phi}(t^{n}-\tau)d\tau\nu_{\phi}(ds)

\displaystyle=\left(\overline{\nu}_{\phi}(t)-\overline{\nu}_{\phi}(t_{n})\right)(U^{\phi}(t_{n})-U^{\phi}(t_{n}-t))

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{t}^{t_{n}}u^{\phi}(t_{n}-\tau)(\overline{\nu}_{\phi}(\tau)-\overline{\nu}_{\phi}(t_{n}))\,d\tau

\displaystyle=\left(\overline{\nu}_{\phi}(t)-\overline{\nu}_{\phi}(t_{n})\right)(U^{\phi}(t_{n})-U^{\phi}(t_{n}-t))

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{t}^{t_{n}}u^{\phi}(t_{n}-\tau)\overline{\nu}_{\phi}(\tau)\,d\tau-\overline{\nu}_{\phi}(t_{n})U^{\phi}(t_{n}-t)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\overline{\nu}_{\phi}(t)-\overline{\nu}_{\phi}(t_{n})\right)(U^{\phi}(t_{n})-U^{\phi}(t_{n}-t))

\displaystyle\qquad+\overline{\nu}_{\phi}(t)U^{\phi}(t_{n}-t)-\overline{\nu}_{\phi}(t_{n})U^{\phi}(t_{n}-t)

\displaystyle=\left(\overline{\nu}_{\phi}(t)-\overline{\nu}_{\phi}(t_{n})\right)U^{\phi}(t_{n}),

hence, since
\overline{\nu}_{\phi}
is also right-continuous,
\lim_{n\to\infty}J_{n}^{1}=0
, thus
\lim_{n\to\infty}J_{n}=0
. This proves that

(5.22)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\left\|\phi(\partial_{t}-G)q(t_{n},\cdot)-\phi(\partial_{t}-G)q(t,\cdot)\right\|_{C(E)}=0.

Now fix
x\in E
and assume, by contradiction, that
I_{x}\not={\varnothing}
. Let
t\in I_{x}
and consider any interval of the form
[t,t+\delta]
for some
\delta>0
. Let
I_{x}^{\delta}=I_{x}\cap[t,t+\delta]
and observe that
|I_{x}^{\delta}|=0
, hence
|[t,t+\delta]\setminus I_{x}^{\delta}|=\delta=|[t,t+\delta]|
. Since the Lebesgue measure has full topological support (i.e., any non-empty open set has positive measure), then
[t,t+\delta]\setminus I_{x}^{\delta}
is dense in
[t,t+\delta]
and there exists a sequence
t_{n}\downarrow t
such that
t_{n}\not\in I_{x}
for all
n\in\N
. For such a sequence

\phi(\partial_{t}-G)q(t_{n},x)=\overline{\nu}_{\phi}(t_{n})q(t_{n},x).

However, taking the limit as
n\to\infty
, using (
5.21
), (
5.22
) and the fact that
\overline{\nu}_{\phi}
is right-continuous, we get

\phi(\partial_{t}-G)q(t,x)=\overline{\nu}_{\phi}(t)q(t,x),

that is absurd since
t\in I_{x}
. Hence
I_{x}={\varnothing}
and, since
x\in E
is arbitrary, this shows that

(5.23)

\phi(\partial_{t}-G)q(t,x)=\overline{\nu}_{\phi}(t)q(t,x),\quad\forall(t,x)\in\R^{+}\times E.

Combining the latter with the fact that
\lim\limits_{x\to\infty}q(t,x)=0
locally uniformly with respect to
t\geq 0
as shown in Proposition
5.6
, we finally have that
q
is solution of (
5.1
).

It remains to show that this is the unique solution. Indeed, let
q_{1}
be another solution of (
5.1
). Then
w=q-q_{1}
solves (
5.1
) with
u\equiv 0
. Now let
T>0
and assume, by contradiction, that
\varepsilon=\sup_{(t,x)\in[0,T]\times E}|w(t,x)|>0
. Let
K\subset E
be such that

\sup_{x\not\in K}|w(t,x)|<\varepsilon,\ \forall t\in[0,T].

Then, since
w
is continuous,

\varepsilon=\max_{(t,x)\in[0,T]\times K}|w(t,x)|.

Let
(t_{\star},x_{\star})\in[0,T]\times K
be such that
|w(t_{\star},x_{\star})|=\varepsilon
. If
w(t_{\star},x_{\star})=\varepsilon
, then
w(t_{\star},x_{\star})\geq w(t,x)
for all
(t,x)\in(0,t_{\star}]\times E
and
w(t_{\star},x_{\star})=\varepsilon>0=w(0,x)
for all
x\in E
. Hence, by Proposition
3.5
we have

0=-\phi(\partial_{t}-G)w(t_{\star},x_{\star})<0,

which is a contradiction. The same holds if
w(t_{\star},x_{\star})=-\varepsilon
. Hence
w\equiv 0
and this ends the proof.
∎

5.3.
Another uniqueness criterion

Before moving to option pricing, we give a further result concerning the uniqueness of the solution to our non-local problem.

Proposition 5.9
.

Let
q:\R_{0}^{+}\times E\to\R
be a function such that
q(t,\cdot)\in{\sf Dom}(G)
for all
t\geq 0
and
t\in\R_{0}^{+}\mapsto q(t,\cdot)\in C_{0}(E)
is measurable. Assume further that there exists
\lambda_{\star}>0
such that:

•

The function

\widetilde{q}(\lambda,x)=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}q(t,x)dt

is well-defined for all
x\in E
and
\lambda>\lambda_{\star}

•

It holds

\left\|\widetilde{q}\right\|_{C(E)}\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda_{\star}t}\left\|q(t,\cdot)\right\|_{C(E)}dt<\infty.

•

For all
x\in E
there exists
\mathcal{I}_{x}\subset\R_{0}^{+}
such that
|\mathcal{I}_{x}|=0
and
\phi(\partial_{t}-G)q(t,x)
is well-defined for all
t\in\R_{0}^{+}\setminus\mathcal{I}_{x}
.

•

For all
x\in E
and
\lambda>\lambda_{\star}
it holds

(5.24)

\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}\phi(\partial_{t}-G)q(t,x)dt=-\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}(P_{s}q(t-s,x)\mathds{1}_{(0,t]}(s)-q(t,x))dt\,\nu_{\phi}(ds)

•

For all
x\in E
and
t\in\R_{0}^{+}\setminus\mathcal{I}_{x}
it holds

(5.25)

\phi(\partial_{t}-G)q(t,x)=\overline{\nu}_{\phi}(t)q(0,x),\quad\mbox{ for almost all }t>0.

Then, for almost all
t\in\R_{0}^{+}
it holds

\left\|q(t,\cdot)-\E^{(\cdot,0)}[q(0,X(t))]\right\|_{C(E)}=0.

Proof.

Since
q(0,\cdot)\in{\sf Dom(G)}
, arguing as in the proof of Theorem
5.2
, we know that the function
q_{\star}(t,x)=\E^{(x,0)}[q(0,X(t))]
satisfies all the assumptions in the statement. As a consequence, also the function
q-q_{\star}
satisfies all the assumptions in the statement. Hence, without loss of generality, we can assume that
q(0,\cdot)\equiv 0
. Integrating (
5.25
) against
e^{-\lambda t}
for
\lambda>\lambda_{\star}
and using (
5.24
) we get

(5.26)

\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}(P_{s}q(t-s,x_{\star})\mathds{1}_{(0,t]}(s)-q(t,x))dt\,\nu_{\phi}(ds)=0.

Next, notice that

(5.27)

\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}P_{s}q(t-s,x)\mathds{1}_{(0,t]}(s)dt=e^{-\lambda s}\int_{0}^{\infty}P_{s}\left(e^{-\lambda(t-s)}q(t-s,\cdot)\mathds{1}_{(0,t]}(s)\right)(x)dt.

Now observe that

(5.28)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{0}^{\infty}\left\|e^{-\lambda(t-s)}q(t-s,\cdot)\mathds{1}_{(0,t]}(s)\right\|_{C(E)}dt&=\int_{0}^{\infty}\left\|e^{-\lambda(t-s)}q(t-s,\cdot)\mathds{1}_{\R_{0}^{+}}(t-s)\right\|_{C(E)}dt\\
&=\int_{-s}^{\infty}\left\|e^{-\lambda\tau}q(\tau,\cdot)\mathds{1}_{\R_{0}^{+}}(\tau)\right\|_{C(E)}d\tau\\
&=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda\tau}\left\|q(\tau,\cdot)\right\|_{C(E)}d\tau<\infty.\end{split}

Observing that, for fixed
s>0
, also
t\in\R_{0}^{+}\mapsto e^{-\lambda(t-s)}q(t-s,\cdot)\mathds{1}_{(0,t]}(s)\in C_{0}(E)
is measurable, we know by (
5.28
) and Bochner’s theorem (see
[
2
, Theorem 1.1.4]
) that the latter is Bochner-integrable and then, by
[
2
, Proposition 1.1.6]
and (
5.27
), we have

(5.29)

\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}P_{s}q(t-s,x)\mathds{1}_{(0,t]}(s)dt=e^{-\lambda s}P_{s}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda(t-s)}q(t-s,\cdot)\mathds{1}_{(0,t]}(s)(x)dt\right)=e^{-\lambda s}P_{s}\widetilde{q}(x)

and then (
5.26
) becomes

(5.30)

\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(e^{-\lambda s}P_{s}\widetilde{q}(x)-\widetilde{q}(x)\right)\nu_{\phi}(ds)=0.

Now let
\widetilde{P}_{s}=e^{-\lambda s}P_{s}
and notice that this is still a Feller semigroup with generator
G-\lambda I
, where
I
is the identity operator. In particular, by
[
2
, Proposition 1.1.7]
,
\widetilde{q}\in{\sf Dom}(G)\subset{\sf Dom}(G-\lambda I)
. In particular, this implies that

e^{-\lambda s}P_{s}\widetilde{q}(x)-\widetilde{q}(x)=\int_{0}^{s}\widetilde{P}_{\tau}(G-\lambda I)\widetilde{q}(x)\,d\tau.

Taking the norm we get

\displaystyle\left\|e^{-\lambda s}P_{s}\widetilde{q}-\widetilde{q}\right\|_{C(E)}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{s}\left\|\widetilde{P}_{\tau}(G-\lambda I)\widetilde{q}\right\|_{C(E)}\,d\tau\leq\frac{\left\|(G-\lambda I)\widetilde{q}\right\|_{C(E)}}{\lambda}(1-e^{-\lambda s}).

Integrating the right-hand side against
\nu_{\phi}(ds)
we get

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\left\|e^{-\lambda s}P_{s}\widetilde{q}-\widetilde{q}\right\|_{C(E)}\,\nu_{\phi}(ds)\leq\frac{\left\|(G-\lambda I)\widetilde{q}\right\|_{C(E)}}{\lambda}\phi(\lambda).

Thus, we can rewrite (
5.30
) in terms of Bochner integrals as

(5.31)

\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(e^{-\lambda s}P_{s}\widetilde{q}-\widetilde{q}\right)\nu_{\phi}(ds)=0.

Now consider the Feller semigroup
(P_{s}^{\phi})_{s\geq 0}
acting on
f\in C_{0}(E)
as

\widetilde{P}_{s}^{\phi}f=\int_{0}^{+\infty}\widetilde{P}_{\tau}fg_{S}(\tau;s)\,d\tau

and denote by
\left(-\phi(-(G-\lambda I)),{\sf Dom}(\phi(-(G-\lambda I)))\right)
its generator. By Phillips formula (see
[
64
, Theorem
12
.6]
) we know that
{\sf Dom}(G-\lambda I)\subset{\sf Dom}(\phi(-(G-\lambda I)))
and on
f\in{\sf Dom}(G-\lambda I)
it holds

-\phi(-(G-\lambda I))f=-\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(e^{-\lambda_{\star}s}P_{s}f-f\right)\nu_{\phi}(ds).

Since
\widetilde{q}\in{\sf Dom}(G-\lambda I)
, we get by (
5.31
)

(5.32)

-\phi(-(G-\lambda I))\widetilde{q}=0.

Finally, notice that for all
f\in{\sf Dom}(G-\lambda I)
it holds

\left\|\widetilde{P}_{s}^{\phi}f\right\|_{C(E)}\leq\int_{0}^{+\infty}\left\|\widetilde{P}_{\tau}f\right\|_{C(E)}g_{S}(\tau;s)\,d\tau\leq e^{-s\phi(\lambda)}\left\|f\right\|_{C(E)},

where
\phi(\lambda)>0
. Hence, by the Hille-Yosida Theorem (see
[
2
, Theorems 3.3.2 and 3.3.4]
) we know that
0
belongs to the resolvent set of
-\phi(\lambda I-G)
and thus the operator is invertible. As a consequence, (
5.32
) implies

\widetilde{q}(\lambda,\cdot)\equiv 0.

By injectivity of the Laplace transform (see
[
2
, Theorem 1.7.3]
), this ends the proof.
∎

Remark 5.10
.

Notice that the function
q
in Proposition
5.9
is not a solution of (
3.3
) in the sense of Definition
3.6
. Indeed, on the one hand we are not requiring that
q
is a continuous function of
(t,x)
, on the other hand
q
solves (
3.3
) not for all
(t,x)\in\R_{0}^{+}\times E
but only on a subset
\widetilde{E}\subset\R_{0}^{+}\times E
such that for all
x\in E
the set
\widetilde{I}_{x}=\{t\in\R_{0}^{+}:\ (t,x)\not\in\widetilde{E}\}
satisfies
|\widetilde{I}_{x}|=0
. With an abuse of notation, we could refer to such a function
q
as an almost-everywhere (a.e.) solution of (
3.3
). From this point of view, Proposition
5.9
tells us that the solution provided in Theorem
5.2
is also the unique a.e. solution satisfying some additional conditions on the Laplace transform. Finally, notice that we are not requiring that
\lim_{x\to\infty}q(t,x)=0
locally uniformly with respect to
t\geq 0
.

6.
Option pricing with the undershooting of the
\alpha
-stable subordinator

In this section we deal with the model with dependent returns and trade durations (DRD) introduced in
[
35
]
. In
[
35
, Theorem 6.2]
, an option pricing formula is obtained for such a model using Fourier methods. Here, we shall instead use the results of the previous sections, in particular Theorem
5.2
, to derive a suitable final problem for a non-local pseudo-differential equation, in the same spirit as for the local Black and Scholes differential equation.

6.1.
The price process

To describe the price process underlying our option-pricing method, we shall make use of the time-changed processes introduced in Section
4
. Let
B=\{B(t),\ t\geq 0\}
be a
1
-dimensional standard Brownian motion and consider the process
M(t)=e^{B(t)}
. Consider
S
as in Section
4
and assume
(B,S)
is a canonical Feller process, on the canonical filtered probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F}_{\infty},\{\cF_{t}\}_{t\geq 0},\mathds{P}^{(x,v)})
. By Lemma
4.2
we know that
(B^{\phi},S)
is a Markov additive Feller process with respect to its augmented natural filtration
\cG:=\{\mathcal{G}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
. Now let
X_{\rm e}(t)=B^{\phi}(L(t)-)=B(H(t))
and consider the age process

(6.1)

\gamma(t):=t-S(0)-H(t).

Then,
(X_{\rm e},\gamma)
is a càglàd process. Furthermore, notice that by Phillip’s theorem, since
C^{\infty}_{c}(\R)
is contained in the domain of the generator of
(B,S)
, we know that
C^{\infty}_{c}(\R)
is a subset of the domain of the generator of
(B^{\phi},S)
. Hence, we can use the Courrége-Waldenfels theorem (see
[
14
, Theorem 2.21]
) to establish the jump-diffusion form of the generator of
(B^{\phi},S)
. Thus, we have the following result.

Theorem 6.1
.

For
t\geq 0
set
\cH_{t}=\cG_{L(t)-}
. Then the process
(X_{\rm e},\gamma)
is a time-homogeneous Markov process with respect to the filtration
\cH:=\{\cH_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
.

Proof.

The proof is exactly the same of the ones of
[
47
, Theorems 3.2 and 4.1]
, with the only exception that since
(B^{\phi},{S})
is not a canonical Feller process in our setting, one has to modify the translation operator accordingly. Precisely, in our case we just need to set
\theta_{t}\omega(s)=(\omega_{1}(s),\omega_{2}(s+t))
for all
t,s\geq 0
. Indeed, for
s,t\geq 0
and
\omega\in\Omega

(B^{\phi}(\theta_{t}\omega,s),S(\theta_{t}\omega,s))=((B(\theta_{t}\omega),S(\theta_{t}\omega,s)),S(\theta_{t}\omega,s))\\
=(\omega_{1}(\omega_{2}(t+s)),\omega_{2}(t+s))=(B^{\phi}(\omega,t+s),S(\omega,t+s)).

∎

Now we set

(6.2)

X(t)=e^{X_{\rm e}(t)}=M(H(t))=M^{\phi}(L(t)-).

(X,\gamma)
is still a time-homogeneous Markov process with respect to
\cH
. Now define
\cN_{t}^{0}=\sigma((X_{\rm e}(s),\gamma(s)),s\leq t)
and
\cN_{t}
its completion with respect to
\mathds{P}^{(x,v)}
.
\cN:=\{\cN_{t}^{0}\}_{t\geq 0}
is the natural filtration of
(X,\gamma)
, as the latter is related to
(X_{\rm e},\gamma)
by means of a homeomorphism. Furthermore, since
(X,\gamma)
is
\cH
-Markov, then
\cN_{t}\subseteq\cH_{t}
for all
t\geq 0
and both
(X_{\rm e},\gamma)
and
(X,\gamma)
are
\cN
-Markov and time-homogeneous. Since we are going to work with càglàd processes, let us introduce now, for any
T>0
, the space
D_{-}[0,T]
of càglàd functions
f:[0,T]\to\R
and
\Lambda_{-}[0,T]
its subspace of non-decreasing and non-negative functions. As usual in this context, we need to introduce a new (family of) probability measures under which the process
X
is actually a
\cN
-martingale. From now on, whenever it is not ambiguous, we shall denote by
{\left.\kern-1.2ptX\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{[0,T]}}
,
{\left.\kern-1.2ptX_{\rm e}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{[0,T]}}
,
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\gamma\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{[0,T]}}
and
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\cN\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{[0,T]}}
the restriction of the considered quantities on the interval
[0,T]
. We need the following lemma.

Lemma 6.2
.

For any
m\in\mathbb{R}
and
x,v\in\R
, the process

Z^{m}(t):=e^{mX_{\rm e}(t)-\frac{m^{2}H(t)}{2}}

is a
\cN
-martingale under
\mathds{P}^{(x,v)}
.

Proof.

Z^{m}
is
\cN
-adapted by definition. To show that
Z^{m}(t)\in L^{1}(\Omega;\mathds{P}^{(x,v)})
for all
t\geq 0
, just notice that

\E^{(x,v)}[Z^{m}(t)]=\E^{(x,v)}\left[\E^{(x,v)}\left[\cE(mB)(H(t))\mid H(t)\right]\right],

where

\cE(mB)(\tau)=e^{mB(\tau)-\frac{m^{2}\tau}{2}}

is the Doleans-Dade exponential of
mB
. Since
B
and
H
are independent, we have

\E^{(x,v)}\left[\cE(mB)(H(t))\mid H(t)\right]=F_{1}(H(t)),

where

F_{1}(\tau):=\E^{(x,v)}\left[\cE(mB)(\tau)\right]={\E^{(x,0)}\left[\cE(mB)(\tau)\right]=e^{x}}.

Hence

\E^{(x,v)}[Z^{m}(t)]={e^{x}\int_{0}^{t-v}\,\overline{\nu}_{\phi}(t-v-s)u^{\phi}(s)\,ds=e^{x}<\infty}.

To show the martingale property, fix
0\leq s\leq t
and consider two Borel sets
A_{1},A_{2}\subset D_{-}[0,s]
. Consider the event

A_{\star}:=\{{\left.\kern-1.2ptX_{\rm e}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{[0,s]}}\in A_{1}\}\cap\{{\left.\kern-1.2pt\gamma\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{[0,s]}}\in A_{2}\}.

Since
B
and
H
are independent and
\gamma(t)=t-v-H(t)
for all
t\geq 0
, it holds

(6.3)

\E^{(x,v)}[Z^{m}(t)\mathds{1}_{A_{\star}}]=\E^{(x,v)}[\mathds{1}_{A_{2}}(\gamma)F_{2}({\left.\kern-1.2ptH\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{[0,t]}})],

where, for all
g\in\Lambda_{-}[0,t]
, we set

\displaystyle F_{2}(g)=\E^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{A_{1}}({\left.\kern-1.2ptB(g(\cdot))\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{[0,s]}})\cE(mB)(g(t))\right]\ \mbox{ and }\ ,

and we notice that

F_{2}({\left.\kern-1.2ptH\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{[0,t]}})=\E^{(x,v)}[Z^{m}(t)\mathds{1}_{A_{1}}(X_{\rm e})\mid H(\tau),\ \tau\geq 0].

Notice in particular that
\cE(mB)
is the Doleans-Dade exponential of
mB
. Since
A_{1}\subset D_{-}[0,s]
, the event
\{{\left.\kern-1.2ptB(g(\cdot))\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{[0,s]}}\in A_{1}\}
belongs to
\sigma(B(w),\ w\leq g(s))
. Furthermore, once we recall that
\cE(mB)
is a martingale and
g(s)\leq g(t)
, we have

F_{2}(g)=\E^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{A_{1}}(B(g(\cdot)))\cE(mB)(g(s))\right]=:\widetilde{F}({\left.\kern-1.2ptg\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{[0,s]}}).

However, we have that, still by independence between
B
and
H

\widetilde{F}_{2}({\left.\kern-1.2ptH\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{[0,s]}})=\E^{(x,v)}[Z^{m}(s)\mathds{1}_{A_{1}}(X_{\rm e})\mid H(\tau),\ \tau\geq 0].

Substituting it back into (
6.3
), we get

(6.4)

\E^{(x,v)}[Z^{m}(t)\mathds{1}_{A_{\star}}]=\E^{(x,v)}[\mathds{1}_{A_{2}}(\gamma)\widetilde{F}_{2}({\left.\kern-1.2ptH\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{[0,s]}})]=\E^{(x,v)}[Z^{m}(s)\mathds{1}_{A_{\star}}].

Since the events of the form
A_{\star}
constitute a
\pi
-system generating
\cN_{s}
, this ends the proof.
∎

From now on we set
m=-1/2
and we write
Z:=Z^{-\frac{1}{2}}
. Therefore we have

Z(t)=e^{-\frac{X_{\rm e}(t)}{2}-\frac{H(t)}{8}}

For any
T>0
define the probability measure

\cN_{T}\ni A\mapsto\widetilde{\mathds{P}}_{T}^{(x,v)}(A)=e^{\frac{x}{2}}\E^{(x,v)}\left[Z(T)\mathds{1}_{A}\right],

i.e., in terms of the Radon-Nykodim derivative:

\frac{d\widetilde{\mathds{P}}_{T}^{(x,v)}}{d{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mathds{P}^{(x,v)}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\cN_{T}}}}=Z(T).

Since
Z(T)
is an
\cN
-martingale, we have that
\widetilde{\mathds{P}}_{t}^{(x,v)}={\left.\kern-1.2pt\widetilde{\mathds{P}}_{T}^{(x,v)}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\cN_{t}}}
for all
T\geq 0
and
t\in[0,T]
. This guarantees that we can use the Kolmogorov extension theorem to construct a probability measure
\widetilde{\mathds{P}}^{(x,v)}
on
(\Omega,\cN_{\infty})
, where
\cN_{\infty}:=\sigma\left(\bigcup_{t\geq 0}\cN_{t}\right)
such that
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\widetilde{\mathds{P}}^{(x,v)}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\cN_{t}}}=\widetilde{\mathds{P}}^{(x,v)}_{t}
for all
t\geq 0
. The method of construction of such a measure is classical and we refer to
[
36
, Page 192]
. Furthermore, notice that since the Radon-Nykodim derivative is strictly positive, the measure
\widetilde{\mathds{P}}^{(x,v)}
is equivalent to
\mathds{P}^{(x,v)}
when restricted on
\cN_{t}
for any
t\geq 0
.

Now let us show that
X
defined in (
6.2
) is an
\cN
-martingale under such a measure.

Proposition 6.3
.

For any
(x,v)\in\R^{2}
, the process
X
is an
\cN
-martingale under
\widetilde{\mathds{P}}^{(x,v)}
.

Proof.

By definition
X
is
\cN
-adapted. Now we show that for all
t\in[0,T]
it is true that
X(t)\in L^{1}(\Omega;\widetilde{\mathds{P}}^{(x,v)})
. To do this, first notice that

\displaystyle\widetilde{\E}^{(x,v)}[X(t)]

\displaystyle=e^{\frac{x}{2}}\E^{(x,v)}\left[e^{X_{\rm e}(t)-\frac{X_{\rm e}(T)}{2}-\frac{H(T)}{8}}\right]

\displaystyle=\E^{(x,v)}\left[\E^{(x,v)}\left[e^{\frac{x}{2}+X_{\rm e}(t)-\frac{X_{\rm e}(T)}{2}-\frac{H(T)}{8}}\mid H(t),H(T)\right]\right].

Since
H(t)\leq H(T)
are independent of the Brownian motion
B
, if we set, for
s_{1}\leq s_{2}
,

F_{1}(s_{1},s_{2})=\E^{(x,v)}\left[e^{B(s_{1})-\frac{B(s_{2})}{2}+\frac{x}{2}-\frac{s_{2}}{8}}\right]

we get

\displaystyle\widetilde{\E}^{(x,v)}[X(t)]

\displaystyle=\E^{(x,v)}\left[F_{1}(H(t),H(T))\right].

However, by Girsanov’s theorem (see
[
36
, Theorem 5.1]
), we know that
\{B(s)+\frac{s}{2},\ s\in[0,s_{2}]\}
is a Brownian motion under the measure

A\in\cF_{s_{2}}\mapsto\E^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{A}e^{-\frac{B(s_{2})}{2}+\frac{x}{2}-\frac{s_{2}}{8}}\right],

hence in particular
e^{B(s)}=e^{B(s)+\frac{s}{2}-\frac{s}{2}}
is the Doleans-Dade exponential of
B(s)+\frac{s}{2}
and thus a martingale under the same measure. As a consequence, we have that

F_{1}(s_{1},s_{2})=\E^{(x,v)}\left[e^{B(s_{1})-\frac{B(s_{2})}{2}+\frac{x}{2}-\frac{s_{2}}{8}}\right]=e^{x}

and then

\displaystyle\widetilde{\E}^{(x,v)}[X(t)]

\displaystyle=\E^{(x,v)}\left[F_{1}(H(t),H(T))\right]=e^{x}<\infty.

Now we need to prove the martingale property. To do this, fix
s
and
t
such that
0\leq s\leq t
and let
A_{1},A_{2}\in D_{-}[0,s]
be Borel sets. Define the event

A_{\star}:=\{{\left.\kern-1.2ptX_{\rm e}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{[0,s]}}\in A_{1}\}\cap\{{\left.\kern-1.2pt\gamma\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{[0,s]}}\in A_{2}\}.

Then we have, for any
T>t
,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\E}^{(x,v)}\left[X(t)\mathds{1}_{A_{\star}}\right]=\E^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{A_{1}}({\left.\kern-1.2ptX_{\rm e}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{[0,s]}})\mathds{1}_{A_{2}}({\left.\kern-1.2pt\gamma\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{[0,s]}})e^{\frac{x}{2}+X_{\rm e}(t)-\frac{X_{\rm e}(T)}{2}-\frac{H(T)}{8}}\right]

(6.5)

\displaystyle=\E^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{A_{2}}({\left.\kern-1.2pt\gamma\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{[0,s]}})F_{2}({\left.\kern-1.2ptH\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{[0,T]}}{)}\right],

where for any
g\in\Lambda_{-}[0,T]
we set

F_{2}(g):=\E^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{A_{1}}({\left.\kern-1.2ptB(g(\cdot))\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{[0,s]}})e^{\frac{x}{2}+B(g(t))-\frac{B(g(T))}{2}-\frac{g(T)}{8}}\right]

and we notice that

F_{2}({\left.\kern-1.2ptH\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{[0,T]}})=\E^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{A_{1}}({\left.\kern-1.2ptX_{\rm e}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{[0,s]}})e^{\frac{x}{2}+X_{\rm e}(t)-\frac{X_{\rm e}(T)}{2}-\frac{H(T)}{8}}\mid\ {\left.\kern-1.2ptH\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{[0,T]}}\right].

Since
A_{1}\subset D_{-}[0,s]
, the event
\{{\left.\kern-1.2ptB(g(\cdot))\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{[0,s]}}\in A_{1}\}
belongs to
\sigma(B(w),\ w\leq g(s))
. Therefore we use again Girsanov’s theorem and use the martingale property of Brownian (conditionally on the independent
H|_{[0,T]}
) to get now

F_{2}(g)=\E^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{A_{1}}({\left.\kern-1.2ptB(g(\cdot))\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{[0,s]}})e^{\frac{x}{2}+B(g(s))-\frac{B(g(T))}{2}-\frac{g(T)}{8}}\right].

In particular, we have that

F_{2}({\left.\kern-1.2ptH\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{[0,T]}})=\E^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{A_{1}}({\left.\kern-1.2ptX_{\rm e}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{[0,s]}})e^{\frac{x}{2}+X_{\rm e}(s)-\frac{X_{\rm e}(T)}{2}-\frac{H(T)}{8}}\mid\ {\left.\kern-1.2ptH\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{[0,T]}}\right].

When we replace the latter equality back into (
6.5
), we obtain

\displaystyle\widetilde{\E}^{(x,v)}

\displaystyle\left[X(t)\mathds{1}_{A_{\star}}\right]=\E^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{A_{1}}({\left.\kern-1.2ptX_{\rm e}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{[0,s]}})\mathds{1}_{A_{2}}({\left.\kern-1.2pt\gamma\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{[0,s]}})e^{\frac{x}{2}+X_{\rm e}(t)-\frac{X_{\rm e}(T)}{2}-\frac{H(T)}{8}}\right]

(6.6)

\displaystyle=\E^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{A_{1}}({\left.\kern-1.2ptX_{\rm e}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{[0,s]}})\mathds{1}_{A_{2}}({\left.\kern-1.2pt\gamma\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{[0,s]}})e^{\frac{x}{2}+X_{\rm e}(s)-\frac{X_{\rm e}(T)}{2}-\frac{H(T)}{8}}\right]=\widetilde{\E}^{(x,v)}\left[X(s)\mathds{1}_{A_{\star}}\right].

Given that events of the form of
A_{\star}
are a
\pi
-system generating
\cN_{s}
, this completes the proof.
∎

We need, however, to guarantee that the change of measure does not affect the Markov property and the time-homogeneity of the process
(X,\gamma)
. As for the Markov property, this is considered in the following result, while the preservation of the time-homogeneity will be proved later.

Proposition 6.4
.

The process
(X,\gamma)
is
\cN
-Markov under
\widetilde{\mathds{P}}^{(x,v)}
.

Proof.

Consider any function
f\in L^{\infty}(\R^{2})
and let
0\leq s\leq t
. We have, for any
T>t
,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[f(X(t),\gamma(t))\mid\cN_{s}\right]

\displaystyle={\frac{\mathds{E}^{(x,v)}\left[f(X(t),\gamma(t))e^{-\frac{X_{e}(T)}{2}-\frac{H(T)}{8}}\mid\cN_{s}\right]}{\mathds{E}^{(x,v)}\left[e^{-\frac{X_{e}(T)}{2}-\frac{H(T)}{8}}\mid\cN_{s}\right]}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathds{E}^{(x,v)}\left[f(X(t),\gamma(t))e^{-\frac{\log(X(T))}{2}-\frac{T-v-\gamma(T)}{8}}\mid\cN_{s}\right]}{\mathds{E}^{(x,v)}\left[e^{-\frac{\log(X(T))}{2}-\frac{T-v-\gamma(T)}{8}}\mid\cN_{s}\right]}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathds{E}^{(x,v)}\left[f(X(t),\gamma(t))e^{-\frac{\log(X(T))}{2}-\frac{T-v-\gamma(T)}{8}}\mid X(s),\gamma(s)\right]}{\mathds{E}^{(x,v)}\left[e^{-\frac{\log(X(T))}{2}-\frac{T-v-\gamma(T)}{8}}\mid X(s),\gamma(s)\right]}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[f(X(t),\gamma(t))\mid X(s),\gamma(s)\right],

where we used the fact that
H(T)=T-v-\gamma(T)
and
X_{\rm e}(T)=\log(X(T))
under
\E^{(x,v)}
and the
\cN
-Markov property of
(X,\gamma)
with respect to
\mathds{P}^{(x,v)}
.
∎

Before proving that
(X,\gamma)
is also
time-homogeneous
, we need to investigate another property. Indeed, notice that both
X
and
\gamma
depend on
B
and
S
and in particular
X(0)
and
\gamma(0)
are
constrained
by the values
(x,v)
.
Observe that under
\mathds{P}^{(x,v)}
, one has that if
\sigma(0)=v
and thus
L(t)
is defined for
t\geq v
, as well as
\gamma(t)
. In practice, under
\mathds{P}^{(x,v)}
,
v\leq 0
, the position of process
(X,\gamma)=(X(w),\gamma(w))
,
w\geq 0
, at time
t
, conditionally to
X(s),\gamma(s)
, does note see the choice of
x
and
v
. Hence, as a first step, we need to show that, under
\widetilde{\E}^{(x,v)}
, the distribution of
(X(t),\gamma(t))
given
(X(s),\gamma(s))
is independent of the choice of
(B(0),S(0))
, given that
S(0)\leq s
at least. This is done in the next proposition, in which we also prove that the process
(X,\gamma)
is time-homogeneous with respect to the measure
\widetilde{\mathds{P}}^{(x,v)}
.

Proposition 6.5
.

Let
0\leq s\leq t
. Then, for all
x,x^{\prime}\in\R
and
v,v^{\prime}\leq s
it holds

(6.7)

\widetilde{\E}^{(x,v)}\left[f(X(t),\gamma(t))\mid X(s),\ \gamma(s)\right]=\widetilde{\E}^{(x^{\prime},v^{\prime})}\left[f(X(t),\gamma(t))\mid X(s),\ \gamma(s)\right].

In particular, one can define, for
0\leq s\leq t
, the transition semigroup
\widetilde{Q}_{s,t}
of
(X,\gamma)
under
\widetilde{\mathds{P}}^{(x,v)}
acting on bounded measurable functions
f:\R^{+}\times\R_{0}^{+}\to\R
in such a way that

(6.8)

\widetilde{Q}_{s,t}f(X(s),\gamma(s))=\widetilde{\E}^{(x,v)}\left[f(X(t),\gamma(t))\mid X(s),\gamma(s)\right],

for any
x\in\R
and
v\leq s
. Furthermore,
\widetilde{Q}_{s,t}
only depends on
t-s
and then
(X,\gamma)
is time-homogeneous.

Proof.

Let
(Q_{t})_{t\geq 0}
be the transition semigroup under
\mathds{P}^{(x,v)}
of the time-homogeneous process
(X,\gamma)
, i.e. for all bounded measurable functions
f:\R^{+}\times\R_{0}^{+}\to\R
and for all
t\geq 0
,
Q_{t}f:\R^{+}\times\R_{0}^{+}\to\R
is the bounded measurable function such that for any
s\geq 0
,
x\in\R^{+}
and
v\in[0,s]

(6.9)

Q_{t}f(X(s),\gamma(s))=\E^{(x,v)}[f(X(t+s),\gamma(t+s))\mid X(s),\gamma(s)].

The independence on
(x,v)
is justified by observing that the transition semigroup of
(X_{\rm e},\gamma)
is already independent of
(x,v)
by
[
47
, Theorems 3.2 and 4.1]
.

Notice that (
6.9
) holds whenever
f:\R^{+}\times\R_{0}^{+}\to\R
is a measurable function such that

\E^{(x,v)}\left[|f(X(t),\gamma(t))|\right]<\infty

for all
t\geq 0
and
x,v\in\R
. Now fix
0\leq s\leq t
,
x,x^{\prime}\in\R^{+}
and
v,v^{\prime}\leq s
. Then, as a consequence of Proposition
6.4
, for any
T>t
, we have

(6.10)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\E}^{(x,v)}\left[f(X(t),\gamma(t))\mid X(s),\gamma(s)\right]=\frac{\E^{(x,v)}\left[f(X(t),\gamma(t))e^{-\frac{\log(X(T))}{2}-\frac{T-{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}v-}\gamma(T)}{8}}\mid X(s),\gamma(s)\right]}{\E^{(x,v)}\left[e^{-\frac{\log(X(T))}{2}-\frac{T-{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}v-}\gamma(T)}{8}}\mid X(s),\gamma(s)\right]}.

To deal with the numerator note that, by Lemma
6.2
, we can use the martingale property to get, for any
y
,
w
and
T>t
, that for the Markov semigroup
Q_{t}
,
t\geq 0
, it is true that

(6.11)

\displaystyle Q_{T-t}f(y,w)=f(y,w).

Hence, for the numerator in (
6.10
), we have

(6.12)

\displaystyle\E^{(x,v)}\left[f(X(t),\gamma(t))e^{-\frac{\log(X(T))}{2}-\frac{T-v-\gamma(T)}{8}}\mid X(s),\gamma(s)\right]=\E^{(x,v)}\left[f(X(t),\gamma(t))Q_{T-t}g(X(t),\gamma(t))\mid X(s),\gamma(s)\right],

where

g(y,w)=e^{-\frac{\log(y)}{2}-\frac{T{-S(0)}-w}{8}}.

Next, let

h(y,w)=f(y,w)Q_{T-t}g(y,w).

Given that
f
is bounded, we can write

\displaystyle\E^{(x,v)}\left[|h(X(t),\gamma(t))|\right]

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sup_{(y,w)\in\R^{+}\times\R_{0}^{+}}|f(y,w)|\right)\E^{(x,v)}\left[g(X(T),\gamma(T))\right]

\displaystyle=e^{x}\left(\sup_{(y,w)\in\R^{+}\times\R_{0}^{+}}|f(y,w)|\right)<\infty.

Hence (
6.12
) can be rewritten as

(6.13)

\E^{(x,v)}\left[f(X(t),\gamma(t))e^{-\frac{\log(X(T))}{2}-\frac{T-v-\gamma(T)}{8}}\mid X(s),\gamma(s)\right]=Q_{t-s}h(X(s),\gamma(s)).

For the denominator of (
6.10
), we have

\displaystyle\E^{(x,v)}\left[e^{-\frac{\log(X(T))}{2}-\frac{T-v-\gamma(T)}{8}}\mid X(s),\gamma(s)\right]=Q_{T-s}g(X(s),\gamma(s)).

Hence, (
6.10
) can be rewritten as

(6.14)

\widetilde{\E}^{(x,v)}\left[f(X(t),\gamma(t))\mid X(s),\gamma(s)\right]=\frac{Q_{t-s}h(X(s),\gamma(s))}{Q_{T-s}g(X(s),\gamma(s))}.

The right-hand side is independent of the choice of
(x,v)
, therefore we get (
6.7
). Now, let us select
T=t+1
, so that
h(y,w)=f(y,w)Q_{1}g(t,w)
, and let us set

\widetilde{Q}_{s,t}f(y,w)=\frac{Q_{t-s}\left(f(\cdot,\cdot)Q_{1}g(\cdot,\cdot)\right)(y,w)}{Q_{t-s+1}g(y,w)}.

Then we see that
\widetilde{Q}_{s,t}
only depends on
t-s
and (
6.8
) holds by virtue of (
6.14
).
∎

With the previous proposition in mind, we can directly define
\widetilde{Q}_{t-s}:=\widetilde{Q}_{s,t}
. Finally, we show the equivalent of the Cameron-Martin formula in this context.

Proposition 6.6

(Cameron-Martin formula)
.

For any
(x,v)\in\R^{2}
and any
T>0
, the distribution of
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\left(X_{\rm e}+\frac{H}{2},\gamma\right)\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{[0,T]}}
under
\widetilde{\mathds{P}}^{(x,v)}
coincides with the one of
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\left(X_{\rm e},\gamma\right)\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{[0,T]}}
under
\mathds{P}^{(x,v)}
. As a consequence, the distribution of
(X,\gamma)
under
\widetilde{\mathds{P}}^{(x,v)}
coincides with the one of
\left(Xe^{-\frac{H}{2}},\gamma\right)
under
\mathds{P}^{(x,v)}
.

Proof.

Fix
T>0
and consider a bounded and measurable function
f:D_{-}[0,T]\times\Lambda_{-}[0,T]\to\R
. Then we have

(6.15)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\E}^{(x,v)}

\displaystyle\left[f\left({\left.\kern-1.2pt\left(X_{\rm e}+\frac{H}{2}\right)\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{[0,T]}},{\left.\kern-1.2pt\gamma\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{[0,T]}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\E^{(x,v)}\left[f\left({\left.\kern-1.2pt\left(X_{\rm e}+\frac{H}{2}\right)\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{[0,T]}},{\left.\kern-1.2pt\gamma\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{[0,T]}}\right)e^{-\frac{X_{\rm e}(T)}{2}-\frac{H(T)}{8}}\right]

(6.16)

\displaystyle=\E^{(x,v)}\left[F({\left.\kern-1.2ptH\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{[0,T]}})\right],

where we set, for any
g\in\Lambda_{-}[0,T]
,

\widetilde{\gamma}(g)(t):=t-v-g(t)\ \mbox{ and }\ F(g)=\E^{(x,v)}\left[f\left({\left.\kern-1.2pt\left(B(g(\cdot))+\frac{g(\cdot)}{2}\right)\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{[0,T]}},{\left.\kern-1.2pt\widetilde{\gamma}(g)\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{[0,T]}}\right)e^{-\frac{B(g(T))}{2}-\frac{g(T)}{8}}\right]

and we notice that

F({\left.\kern-1.2ptH\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{[0,T]}})=\E^{(x,v)}\left[f\left({\left.\kern-1.2pt\left(X_{\rm e}+\frac{H}{2}\right)\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{[0,T]}},{\left.\kern-1.2pt\gamma\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{[0,T]}}\right)e^{-\frac{X_{\rm e}(T)}{2}-\frac{H(T)}{8}}\mid{\left.\kern-1.2ptH\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{[0,T]}}\right].

By Girsanov theorem, however, we know that under the measure

A\in\cF_{g(T)}\mapsto\E^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{A}e^{-\frac{B(g(T))}{2}-\frac{g(T)}{8}}\right]

the process
t\in[0,g(T)]\mapsto B(t)+\frac{t}{2}
is a Brownian motion. Hence, the distribution of
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\left(B(g(\cdot))+\frac{g(\cdot)}{2}\right)\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{[0,T]}}
under the aforementioned measure coincides with the distribution of
{\left.\kern-1.2ptB(g(\cdot))\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{[0,T]}}
under
\mathds{P}^{(x,v)}
and then

F(g)=\E^{(x,v)}\left[f({\left.\kern-1.2ptB(g(\cdot))\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{[0,T]}},{\left.\kern-1.2pt\widetilde{\gamma}(g)\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{[0,T]}})\right].

In particular, we notice that, by independence of
B
and
H
,

F({\left.\kern-1.2ptH\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{[0,T]}})=\E^{(x,v)}\left[f({\left.\kern-1.2ptX_{\rm e}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{[0,T]}},{\left.\kern-1.2pt\gamma\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{[0,T]}})\mid{\left.\kern-1.2ptH\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{[0,T]}}\right].

Substituting this into (
6.16
) we have

(6.17)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\E}^{(x,v)}\left[f\left({\left.\kern-1.2pt\left(X_{\rm e}+\frac{H}{2}\right)\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{[0,T]}},{\left.\kern-1.2pt\gamma\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{[0,T]}}\right)\right]=\E^{(x,v)}\left[f({\left.\kern-1.2ptX_{\rm e}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{[0,T]}},{\left.\kern-1.2pt\gamma\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{[0,T]}})\right].

Since
f
is arbitrary, we have the equality in distribution.

Next, notice that with the same argument as in Proposition
6.4
, one can prove that
\left(X_{\rm e}+\frac{H}{2},\gamma\right)
is a
\cN
-Markov process under
\widetilde{\mathds{P}}^{(x,v)}
. Recalling also that
(X_{\rm e},\gamma)
is
\cN
-Markov with respect to the measure
\E^{(x,v)}
, we have that since the distribution of
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\left(X_{\rm e}+\frac{H}{2},\gamma\right)\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{[0,T]}}
under
\widetilde{\mathds{P}}^{(x,v)}
and
{\left.\kern-1.2pt(X_{\rm e},\gamma)\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{[0,T]}}
under
\mathds{P}^{(x,v)}
coincide for all
T>0
, then the distribution of the whole trajectories must coincide. Finally, the distribution of
(X,\gamma)
under
\widetilde{\mathds{P}}^{(x,v)}
coincides with the one of
\left(Xe^{-\frac{H}{2}},\gamma\right)
under
\mathds{P}^{(x,v)}
since both of them are expressed as deterministic functions of
\left(X_{\rm e}+\frac{H}{2},\gamma\right)
and
\left(X_{\rm e},\gamma\right)
.
∎

6.2.
The coupled non-local Black-Scholes equation

We can now introduce the option price process. Fix a maturity
T>0
and a strike price
K>0
. We want to focus on the case of a call option, hence we define the option price process by

\mathcal{X}(t):=\widetilde{\E}^{(x,v)}\left[({X}(T)-K)_{+}\mid\cN_{t}\right],\qquad t\in[0,T]

where
(x)_{+}=\max(x,0)
.
By Proposition
6.4
,
{X}(t)
is given by

\mathcal{X}(t)=\widetilde{\E}^{(x,v)}\left[(\mathcal{X}(T)-K)_{+}\mid X(t),\ \gamma(t)\right]=\widetilde{Q}_{T-t}u(X(t),\gamma(t)),

where

u(y,w)=(y-K)_{+}.

Hence, let us set

q_{\star}(t,y,w)\coloneqq\widetilde{Q}_{T-t}u(y,w).

For a call option with maturity
T>0
, strike price
K>0
, when the interest rate is
0
, the classical Black and Scholes equation is

(6.18)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}q_{\rm BS}(T-t,x)\,=\,-\frac{1}{2}x^{2}\partial_{x}^{2}q_{\rm{BS}}(T-t,x),\qquad t\in[0,T),\,x\in\mathbb{R}^{+},

under the (final) condition
q_{\text{BS}}(T-t,x)\,\mid_{t=T}=\,(x-K)_{+}
.
In practice, denoting

(6.19)

q(t,x)=q_{\star}(T-t,x,0)=\widetilde{Q}_{t}u(x,0)

and, for
w>0
,

(6.20)

\displaystyle q(t,x,w)=q_{\star}(T-t,x,w)=\widetilde{Q}_{t}u(x,w),

in this section we show that the function (
6.19
) is the solution of a Black-and-Scholes type (non-local) equation containing the coupled operator introduced in Section
3
; moreover, we provide a renewal-type equation that gives the price (for any
w>0
) in (
6.20
).

In our analysis we limit ourselves to the case when the process
S
is an
\alpha
-stable subordinator, that is
\phi(\lambda)=\lambda^{\alpha}
, and we furthermore impose, for tehcnical reasons, the condition
\alpha>\frac{1}{2}
.

Remark 6.7
.

The undershoot of an inverse stable subordinator is particularly tractable, in that its marginal distributions are known. Indeed, the law of
H(t)
coincides with that of
tB
where
B
is a Beta
(\beta,1-\ \beta)
random variable (
[
10
]
, Example 5.5). Based on this, after the usual conditioning argument it can be deduced that the characteristic function
\varphi_{X(t)}(z)
of
X(t)
is given by

\varphi_{X(t)}(z)=_{1}\hskip-2.84544ptF_{1}(\alpha,t/2),\qquad z\in\R,

where
{}_{1}F_{1}
is the confluent hypergeometric function. The above follows as a particular case of
[
35
]
, Theorem 6.2, (6.4), second line.

Let us begin with some preliminary considerations. By Remark
6.7
, denote the density of the undershooting
H_{0}
by

(6.21)

{g_{H_{0}}(s;t)}=\frac{s^{\alpha-1}(t-s)^{-\alpha}\mathds{1}_{(0,t)}(s)}{\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(1-\alpha)}.

A general form of (
6.21
) for general subordinators is available in
[
12
, Lemma 1.10]
.
Now we recall that, since when
v=0
it must hold
X_{\rm e}(0)=B(0)
,

q(t,x)=\widetilde{\E}^{(\log(x),0)}\left[(X(t)-K)_{+}\right].

By the time-changed Cameron-Martin formula given in Proposition
6.6
, recalling that, under
v=0
,
H
and
H_{0}
coincide, we do have

(6.22)

q(t,x)=\E^{(\log(x),0)}\left[(X(t)e^{-\frac{H(t)}{2}}-K)_{+}\right]=\int_{0}^{+\infty}q_{\rm BS}(s,x)g_{H}(s;t)ds,

where

\displaystyle q_{\rm BS}(s,x)

\displaystyle=\E^{(\log(x),0)}\left[\left(B(s)e^{-\frac{s}{2}}-K\right)_{+}\right]

\displaystyle=x\Phi\left(\frac{2\log(x)-2\log(K)+s}{2\sqrt{s}}\right)-K\Phi\left(\frac{2\log(x)-2\log(K)-s}{2\sqrt{s}}\right)

and

\Phi(z):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{z}e^{-\frac{x^{2}}{2}}dx.

The function
q_{\rm BS}
is the solution of the classical Black and Scholes equation (
6.18
) with respect to the variable
s=T-t
, i.e., the quantity
q_{\text{BS}}(s,x)
, for fixed
(s,x)
, represents the price, at time zero, of a plain-vanilla call option with maturity
s>0
and the corresponding function satisfies the Black and Scholes equation with the operator
2^{-1}x^{2}\partial_{x}^{2}
(with positive sign). The positive sign in front of the space operator is due to the fact that we are taking the derivative with respect to variable
s>0
that, in this context, is the maturity. Precisely, let, for any
\beta\geq 0

\cC_{0}(\beta):=\{u\in C(\R^{+}):\ \exists C>0,\ |u(x)|\leq Ce^{\beta|\log(x)|}\}

and define

\cC_{0}=\bigcup_{\beta\geq 0}\cC_{0}(\beta).

Furthermore, we say that a function
f\in\cC_{\rm sol}
if and only if

(i)

f\in C(\R_{0}^{+}\times\R^{+})\cap C^{1}(\R^{+}\times\R^{+})

(ii)

f(t,\cdot)\in C^{2}(\R^{+})
for all
t>0

(iii)

For all
T>0
there exist
\beta_{T},C_{T}>0
such that
|f(t,x)|\leq C_{T}e^{\beta_{T}|\log(x)|}
for all
t\in[0,T]
and
x\in\R^{+}
.

For
u\in\cC_{0}(\beta)
, we define the quantity

[u]_{\beta}:=\sup_{x\in\R^{+}}|u(x)|e^{-\beta|\log(x)|}.

Finally, let us introduce
\cC_{0,\ {\rm loc}}(\R_{0}^{+})
as the set of functions
f\in C(\R_{0}^{+}\times\R^{+})
such that for all
T>0
there exist
\beta_{T},C_{T}>0
with the property that
|f(t,x)|\leq C_{T}e^{\beta_{T}|\log(x)|}
for all
t\in[0,T]
and
x\in\R^{+}
. Notice that
\cC_{\rm sol}\subset\cC_{0,\ {\rm loc}}(\R_{0}^{+})
.

Before proceeding to introduce the Black-Scholes equation, let us give a simple lemma, which will be useful in the following.

Lemma 6.8
.

It holds

\displaystyle\E^{(x,v)}[e^{\lambda_{1}|B(t)|+\lambda_{2}B(t)}]

\displaystyle=e^{(\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2})x+\frac{(\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2})^{2}}{2}t}\Phi\left(-\frac{(\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2})t+x}{\sqrt{t}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+e^{-(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})x+\frac{(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})^{2}}{2}t}\Phi\left(-\frac{(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})t-x}{\sqrt{t}}\right)

The proof of such a lemma is given in Appendix
B
. Now we can introduce the operator
G:=\frac{x^{2}}{2}\partial_{x}^{2}
and provide some well-known properties of such an operator.

Lemma 6.9
.

For all
f\in\cC_{0}
there exists a unique classical solution
u_{f}\in\cC_{\rm sol}
of

(6.23)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}u(t,x)=Gu(t,x),&t>0,\ x\in\R^{+}\\
u(0,x)=f(x),&x>0,\end{cases}

i.e.,
(\cC_{0},\cC_{\rm sol})
is a uniqueness class for
G
. If we denote by
(P_{t})_{t\geq 0}
the associated semigroup action, then the following properties hold true.

(1)

For all
f\in\cC_{0}
we have that

(6.24)

\displaystyle P_{t}f(x)\,=\,\int_{0}^{+\infty}f(y)p_{\rm GBM}(t,y;x)dy

where GBM stands for geometric Brownian motion and

(6.25)

p_{\rm GBM}(t,y;x):=x^{\frac{1}{2}}e^{-\frac{t}{8}}\frac{p(t,\log(y)-\log(x))}{y^{\frac{3}{2}}}\ \mbox{ and }\ p(t,w)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi t}}e^{-\frac{w^{2}}{2t}}.

As a consequence, the semigroup
(P_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is Markovian and positivity preserving.

(2)

Let
\beta\geq 0
and assume
f\in\cC_{0}(\beta)
. Then
\partial_{x}P_{t}f\in\cC_{0}(\beta+1)
and

[\partial_{x}P_{t}f(x)]_{\beta+1}\leq 4[f]_{\beta}\exp\left(\beta\frac{t}{2}+\frac{\beta^{2}t^{2}}{2}\right).

(3)

For all
f\in\cC_{0}\cap\cC
such that
\partial_{x}f\in\cC_{0}
and
Gf\in\cC_{0}
it holds

P_{t}Gf=GP_{t}f.

(4)

Fix
\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\in\R
and let
f_{\beta_{1},\beta_{2}}(x)=e^{\beta_{1}|\log(x)|+\beta_{2}\log(x)}
. Then

\displaystyle P_{t}f_{\beta_{1},\beta_{2}}(x)

\displaystyle=e^{(\beta_{1}+\beta_{2})\log(x)+\frac{(\beta_{1}+\beta_{2})(\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}-1)}{2}t}\Phi\left(-\left(\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}-\frac{1}{2}\right)\sqrt{t}-\frac{\log(x)}{\sqrt{t}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+e^{-(\beta_{1}-\beta_{2})\log(x)+\frac{(\beta_{1}-\beta_{2})(\beta_{1}-\beta_{2}+1)}{2}t}\Phi\left(-\left(\beta_{1}-\beta_{2}+\frac{1}{2}\right)\sqrt{t}+\frac{\log(x)}{\sqrt{t}}\right).

(5)

If
f\in\cC_{0}(\beta)
, then
P_{t}f\in\cC_{0}(\beta)
with

[P_{t}f]_{\beta}\leq 2[f]_{\beta}e^{\frac{\beta(\beta+1)}{2}t}.

We give, for completeness, the proof of the previous lemma in Appendix
C
.

Concerning the function
q_{\rm BS}
, we recall the following properties, that can be verified by direct evaluation.

Lemma 6.10
.

For all
t,x>0
it holds

(6.26)

\displaystyle\partial_{x}q_{\rm BS}(t,x)

\displaystyle=\Phi\left(\frac{2\log(x/K)+t}{2\sqrt{t}}\right),

(6.27)

\displaystyle\partial^{2}_{x}q_{\rm BS}(t,x)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{K}{2\pi tx^{3}}}\exp\left(-\frac{4\log^{2}\left(x/K\right)+t^{2}}{8t}\right),

(6.28)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}q_{\rm BS}(t,x)

\displaystyle=G{q}_{\rm BS}(t,x)=\sqrt{\frac{Kx}{8\pi t}}\exp\left(-\frac{4\log^{2}\left(x/K\right)+t^{2}}{8t}\right).

In particular,

(6.29)

\displaystyle\left|q_{\rm BS}(t,x)\right|

\displaystyle\leq e^{|\log(x)|}

(6.30)

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{x}q_{\rm BS}(t,x)\right|

\displaystyle\leq 1,

(6.31)

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{t}q_{\rm BS}(t,x)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|Gq_{\rm BS}(t,x)\right|\leq\sqrt{\frac{Kx}{8\pi t}}.

Furthermore, for all
a>0
and
x\geq a
we have

(6.32)

\left|\partial_{x}^{2}q_{\rm BS}(t,x)\right|\leq\sqrt{\frac{K}{8\pi a^{3}t}}.

In particular
q_{BS}
is the unique solution of

(6.33)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}q_{\rm BS}(t,x)=Gq_{\rm BS}(t,x),&t>0,\ x\in\R^{+}\\
q_{\rm BS}(0,x)=(x-K)_{+},&x>0,\\
q_{\rm BS}\in\cC_{0,{\rm loc}}(\R_{0}^{+}).\end{cases}

The remainder of this section is devoted to the proof of the following theorem, i.e., the main result of this part. Notice that we focus on the case
\phi(\lambda)=\lambda^{\alpha}
with
\alpha>\frac{1}{2}
. While it will be clear that some arguments apply to a generic
\Phi
, most of the proof relies on the specific form of
\Phi
, in particular on the self-similarity of
H_{0}
under this choice. Furthermore, as it will be clear in the following, the condition
\alpha>\frac{1}{2}
is dictated by regularity issues.

Theorem 6.11
.

Let
\phi(\lambda)=\lambda^{\alpha}
for
\alpha\in\left(\frac{1}{2},1\right)
. Let also, for any
h\in\R
,
A\in\cB(\R)
and
w\geq 0

(6.34)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}(A,h)=\int_{0}^{+\infty}\mathds{1}_{A}(s)p(s,h)\frac{\alpha s^{-\alpha-1}}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}ds\qquad\mathcal{K}(d\tau,dh)=\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}(d\tau,h)dh

and

\mathcal{K}_{w}(A)=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{K}(A\cap(\R\times[w,+\infty)))}{\mathcal{K}(\R\times[w,+\infty))}&w>0\\[7.0pt]
\delta_{(0,0)}(A)&w=0.\end{cases}

Then

q_{\star}(t,x,0)=q(T-t,x)

and, for
w>0
and any
v<t-w
,

(6.35)

q_{\star}(t,x,w)=\left(xe^{-\frac{t-v-w}{2}}-K\right)_{+}\mathcal{K}_{w}(x;\R^{d}\times[w+T-t,\infty))\\
+\int_{\R\times[w,w+T-t)}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!q(T-t+w-\tau,\log(x)+y+t-w+\tau-v)\mathcal{K}_{w}(d\tau,dy),

where
q
is the unique solution of

(6.36)

\begin{cases}(\partial_{t}-G)^{\alpha}q(t,x)=\frac{t^{-\alpha}}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}q(0,x)\\
q(0,x)=(x-K)_{+}\\
q\in\cC_{0,{\rm loc}}(\R_{0}^{+}).\end{cases}

This will be proved using several preliminary results:

(1)

First we study the regularity of the function
q
in (
6.22
);

(2)

next, we prove that
q
is solution of (
6.36
);

(3)

we then prove that the solution to (
6.36
) is unique for initial data in a suitable class (to which
(x-K)_{+}
belongs);

(4)

finally, we prove Theorem
6.11
.

Remark 6.12
.

Notice that (
6.35
) is a formula that determines the value of
q_{\star}
uniquely for positive sojourn time. Indeed, the only quantity that is involved is the function
q
which is related to the value of
q_{\star}
on renewal states, i.e. for zero sojourn time, which is the unique solution with at most power growth at
0
and
\infty
of the fully nonlocal Black-Scholes equation (
6.36
). This is also underlined by the fact that (
6.35
) can be rewritten as

(6.37)

q_{\star}(t,x,w)=\left(xe^{-\frac{t-v-w}{2}}-K\right)_{+}\mathcal{K}_{w}(x;\R^{d}\times[w+T-t,\infty))\\
+\int_{\R\times[w,w+T-t)}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!q_{\star}(t-w+\tau,\log(x)+y+t-w+\tau-v,0)\mathcal{K}_{w}(d\tau,dy),

that only involves the function
q_{\star}(t,x,0)
, which is given by the solution of (
6.36
).We remark that
(x-K)_{+}
appears as an initial condition in (
6.36
) but, given that the fair price of our call option at time
t>0
is
q_{\star}(t,x,w)
, then

(6.38)

\displaystyle(x-K)_{+}=q(0,x)\,=\,q_{\star}(T,x,0)

and thus
(x-K)_{+}
is the usual final condition for

q_{\star}(\cdot,\cdot,0)
. It follows that, according to our model, the fair price at time
t>0
of a(n) (intraday) plain vanilla call option (with zero interest rate, strike price
K
and maturity
T>t
) can be determined by solving the initial value problem (
6.36
) then changing the variable
t\rightsquigarrow T-t
and finally using (
6.37
).

6.2.1.
Regularity of
q

In this section, we provide some regularity results on the function
q
that will be needed to apply Theorems
3.2
and
3.3
. Before proceeding, let us recall that for any
f\in L^{\infty}(\R)
, by (
6.21
), it holds

(6.39)

\displaystyle\E^{(x,0)}[f(H(t))]=\int_{0}^{t}f(y)\frac{(t-y)^{-\alpha}y^{\alpha-1}}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)\Gamma(\alpha)}\,dy=\int_{0}^{1}f(ts)\frac{(1-s)^{-\alpha}s^{\alpha-1}}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)\Gamma(\alpha)}\,ds=\E^{(x,0)}[f(tH(1))],

i.e.,
H(t)\overset{d}{=}tH(1)
. We shall use this self-similarity property throughout the proof.

Now, we prove that for all
x>0
the function
q(\cdot,x)
belongs to
{\rm AC}(\R_{0}^{+})
and we provide a suitable upper bound on its a.e. derivative.

Proposition 6.13
.

Under the hypotheses of Theorem
6.11
, for all
x>0
the function
q(\cdot,x)\in{\rm AC}(\R_{0}^{+})
and its a.e. derivative
\partial_{t}q(\cdot,x)
satisfies, for a.a.
t\geq 0
,

(6.40)

|\partial_{t}q(t,x)|\leq\frac{\Gamma\left(\alpha+\frac{1}{2}\right)}{\pi\Gamma(\alpha)}\sqrt{\frac{Kx}{t}}.

Proof.

For any
t>0
, let
h\in\left(-\frac{t}{2},1\right)
and consider
D^{h}q(t,x)=\frac{q(t+h,x)-q(t,x)}{h}
. Then we have, by (
6.39
),

|D^{h}q(t,x)|\leq\int_{0}^{1}\left|\frac{q_{\rm BS}(ts+hs,x)-q_{\rm BS}(ts,x)}{hs}\right|\frac{s^{\alpha}(1-s)^{-\alpha}}{\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(1-\alpha)}ds.

By Lagrange theorem, we know that there exists
\xi\in[\min\{ts,ts+hs\},\max\{ts,ts+hs\}]
such that

\left|\frac{q_{\rm BS}(ts+hs,x)-q_{\rm BS}(ts,x)}{hs}\right|=\left|\partial_{t}q_{\rm BS}(x,\xi)\right|\leq\sqrt{\frac{Kx}{8\pi\xi}}\leq\sqrt{\frac{Kx}{4\pi ts}},

where we also used (
6.31
). Hence

|D^{h}q(t,x)|\leq\sqrt{\frac{Kx}{4\pi t}}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{s^{\alpha-\frac{1}{2}}(1-s)^{-\alpha}}{\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(1-\alpha)}ds=\frac{\Gamma\left(\alpha+\frac{1}{2}\right)}{\pi\Gamma(\alpha)}\sqrt{\frac{Kx}{t}}.

The right-hand side is independent of
h
and belongs to
L^{1}(0,T)
for all
T>0
, thus we can argue as in Proposition
5.4
and get the result.
∎

As claimed, the previous proposition implies that
q
satisfies Item (i) of both Theorems
3.2
and
3.3
. We show this in the next result.

Corollary 6.14
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
6.11
we have that for all
t,x>0

\int_{0}^{t}|P_{s}q(t-s,x)-P_{s}q(t,x)|\frac{\alpha s^{-\alpha-1}}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}ds<\infty

and, for all
\lambda>\frac{3}{8}
and
x>0
,

\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{t}e^{-\lambda t}|P_{s}q(t-s,x)-P_{s}q(t,x)|\frac{\alpha s^{-\alpha-1}}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}ds\,dt<\infty.

Proof.

Fix
x>0
, and note that for
0<s<t
,

(6.41)

\left|q(t-s,x)-q(t,x)\right|\leq\int_{0}^{s}\left|\partial_{t}q(t-\tau,x)\right|d\tau\\
\leq\frac{\Gamma\left(\alpha+\frac{1}{2}\right)}{\pi\Gamma(\alpha)}\sqrt{Kx}\int_{0}^{s}(t-\tau)^{-1/2}d\tau=\frac{\Gamma\left(\alpha+\frac{1}{2}\right)s}{\pi\Gamma(\alpha)}\sqrt{\frac{Kx}{t-s}}.

This proves that
\left|q(t-s,\cdot)-q(t,\cdot)\right|\in\cC_{0}(1/2)
. We get, by also employing Items
(1)
and
(5)
of Lemma
6.9
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}

\displaystyle|P_{s}q(t-s,x)-P_{s}q(t,x)|\frac{\alpha s^{-\alpha-1}}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}ds

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{t}P_{s}|q(t-s,\cdot)-q(t,\cdot)|(x)\frac{\alpha s^{-\alpha-1}}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}ds

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\alpha\Gamma\left(\alpha+\frac{1}{2}\right)\sqrt{K}}{\pi\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(1-\alpha)}e^{\frac{1}{2}|\log(x)|+\frac{3}{8}t}\int_{0}^{t}s^{-\alpha}(t-s)^{-\frac{1}{2}}ds

\displaystyle=\frac{2\alpha\Gamma\left(\alpha+\frac{1}{2}\right)\sqrt{K}}{\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma\left(\frac{3}{2}-\alpha\right)\sqrt{\pi}}t^{\frac{1}{2}-\alpha}e^{\frac{1}{2}|\log(x)|+\frac{3}{8}t},

where we also used
[
3
, Lemma 1.11]
. Furthermore, for any
\lambda>\frac{3}{8}
it holds

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}e^{-\lambda t}|P_{s}q(t-s,x)-P_{s}q(t,x)|\frac{\alpha s^{-\alpha-1}}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}ds\,dt

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\alpha\Gamma\left(\alpha+\frac{1}{2}\right)\sqrt{K}}{\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma\left(\frac{3}{2}-\alpha\right)\sqrt{\pi}}t^{\frac{1}{2}-\alpha}e^{\frac{1}{2}|\log(x)|}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\frac{1}{2}-\alpha}e^{-\left(\lambda-\frac{3}{8}\right)t}\,dt

\displaystyle=\frac{2\alpha\Gamma\left(\alpha+\frac{1}{2}\right)\sqrt{K}}{\Gamma(\alpha)\sqrt{\pi}}t^{\frac{1}{2}-\alpha}e^{\frac{1}{2}|\log(x)|}\left(\lambda-\frac{3}{8}\right)^{\alpha-\frac{3}{2}}.

∎

Now we discuss the regularity of
q
in
x
. This is done by means of the following proposition.

Proposition 6.15
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
6.11
, the following equalities hold for all
t,x>0
:

(6.42)

\displaystyle\partial_{x}q(t,x)\,=\,\frac{1}{\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(1-\alpha)}\int_{0}^{1}\partial_{x}q_{\rm BS}(\tau t,x)\,\tau^{\alpha-1}(1-\tau)^{-\alpha}d\tau,

(6.43)

\displaystyle\partial_{x}^{2}q(t,x)\,=\,\frac{1}{\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(1-\alpha)}\int_{0}^{1}\partial_{x}^{2}q_{\rm BS}(\tau t,x)\,\tau^{\alpha-1}(1-\tau)^{-\alpha}d\tau,

(6.44)

\displaystyle Gq(t,x)\,=\,\frac{1}{\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(1-\alpha)}\int_{0}^{1}Gq_{\rm BS}(\tau t,x)\,\tau^{\alpha-1}(1-\tau)^{-\alpha}d\tau.

Furthermore
q(t,\cdot)\in\cC_{0}(1)
,
\partial_{x}q(t,\cdot)\in\cC_{0}(0)
and
Gq(t,\cdot)\in\cC_{0}(1/2)
for all
t>0
, where

(6.45)

\displaystyle|q(t,x)|

\displaystyle\leq e^{|\log(x)|}

(6.46)

\displaystyle|\partial_{x}q(t,x)|

\displaystyle\leq 1

(6.47)

\displaystyle|Gq(t,x)|

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{Kx}{2t}}\frac{\Gamma\left(\alpha-\frac{1}{2}\right)}{2\pi\Gamma(\alpha)}.

In particular,
q\in\cC_{0,{\rm loc}}(\R_{0}^{+})
.

Proof.

By (
6.22
) and (
6.29
) we already know that
q\in\cC_{0}(1)
with
[q]_{1}\leq 1
. Moreover, by (
6.30
), we know that we can apply the dominated convergence theorem to take the derivative inside the integral sign in (
6.22
) and then using (
6.39
), getting (
6.42
). Furthermore, (
6.30
) also implies that
\partial_{x}q(t,\cdot)\in\cC_{0}(0)
with
[\partial_{x}q(t,\cdot)]_{0}\leq 1
.

To argue with the second derivative, fix
a>0
and notice that for all
x\geq a
it holds, by (
6.32
),

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\left|\partial_{x}^{2}q_{\rm BS}(ts,x)\right|\frac{s^{\alpha-1}(1-s)^{-\alpha}}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)\Gamma(\alpha)}ds

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{K}{8\pi a^{3}t}}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{s^{\alpha-\frac{3}{2}}(1-s)^{-\alpha}}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)\Gamma(\alpha)}ds

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\sqrt{\frac{K}{2a^{3}t}}\frac{\Gamma\left(\alpha-\frac{1}{2}\right)}{\Gamma(\alpha)}<\infty,

since
\alpha>\frac{1}{2}
. Hence, by dominated convergence, we get (
6.43
) and finally, multiplying both sides of (
6.43
) by
\frac{x^{2}}{2}
, we also get (
6.44
). Finally, notice that (
6.44
) and (
6.31
) imply that
Gq(t,\cdot)\in\cC_{0}(1/2)
for all
t>0
and in particular

\left|Gq(t,x)\right|\leq\sqrt{\frac{Kx}{8\pi t}}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{s^{\alpha-\frac{3}{2}}(1-s)^{-\alpha}}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)\Gamma(\alpha)}ds=\sqrt{\frac{Kx}{2t}}\frac{\Gamma\left(\alpha-\frac{1}{2}\right)}{2\pi\Gamma(\alpha)}.

∎

Remark 6.16
.

Notice that Proposition
6.13
, Corollary
6.14
, (
6.42
), (
6.45
) and (
6.46
) hold for any
\alpha\in(0,1)
. The condition
\alpha>1/2
comes into play only for (
6.43
), (
6.44
) and (
6.47
). However, it is worth highlighting that, using a finer estimate in place of (
6.32
), it is possible to prove that (
6.43
) and (
6.44
) still hold for all
t,x>0
with
x\not=K
if
\alpha\leq 1/2
. However, notice that, for
h\in[0,1]
, by Lagrange’s theorem,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial_{x}q_{\rm BS}(ts,K+h)-\partial_{x}q_{\rm BS}(ts,K)}{h}

\displaystyle=\partial_{x}^{2}q_{\rm BS}(ts,\xi)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{K}{2\pi ts\xi^{3}}}\exp\left(-\frac{4\log^{2}(\xi/K)+t^{2}s^{2}}{8ts}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\sqrt{\frac{K}{2\pi ts(K+1)^{3}}}\exp\left(-\frac{4\log^{2}\left(1+\frac{h}{K}\right)+t^{2}s^{2}}{8ts}\right)

where
\xi\in[K,K+h]
. Hence, by (
6.42
), we have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial_{x}q(t,K+h)-\partial_{x}q(t,K)}{h}

\displaystyle\qquad=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\partial_{x}q_{\rm BS}(ts,K+h)-\partial_{x}q_{\rm BS}(ts,K)}{h}\frac{s^{\alpha-1}(1-s)^{-\alpha}}{\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(1-\alpha)}\,ds

\displaystyle\qquad\geq\frac{\sqrt{K}}{\sqrt{2\pi t(K+1)^{3}}}\int_{0}^{1}\exp\left(-\frac{4\log^{2}\left(1+\frac{h}{K}\right)+t^{2}s^{2}}{8ts}\right)\frac{s^{\alpha-\frac{3}{2}}(1-s)^{-\alpha}}{\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(1-\alpha)}\,ds.

Taking the limit as
h\to 0^{+}
we have, as a consequence of the monotone convergence theorem,

\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}\frac{\partial_{x}q(t,K+h)-\partial_{x}q(t,K)}{h}\\
\geq\frac{\sqrt{K}}{\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(1-\alpha)\sqrt{2\pi t(K+1)^{3}}}\int_{0}^{1}\exp\left(-\frac{ts}{8}\right)s^{\alpha-\frac{3}{2}}(1-s)^{-\alpha}\,ds=\infty,

if
\alpha\leq\frac{1}{2}
. Hence, in such a case,
q(t,\cdot)
cannot admit a second order derivative in
x=K
. Suppose that we have already shown that
q
is solution of (
6.36
), then this lack of parabolic smoothing is also common in time-nonlocal equations, see for instance
[
4
, Proposition 3.3]
.

Now we prove that
q
satisfies Item (ii) of both Theorems
3.2
and
3.3
.

Proposition 6.17
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
6.11
it holds, for all
t,x>0
,

\int_{0}^{t}|P_{s}q(t,x)-q(t,x)|\,\nu_{\alpha}(ds)<\infty,

and, for all
x>0
and
\lambda>\frac{3}{8}
,

\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{t}e^{-\lambda t}|P_{s}q(t,x)-q(t,x)|\,\nu_{\alpha}(ds)\,dt<\infty

where
\nu_{\alpha}(ds)
is the Lévy measure corresponding to
\phi(\lambda)=\lambda^{\alpha}
,
\alpha\in(1/2,1)
, i.e.,

(6.48)

\displaystyle\nu_{\alpha}(ds)=\alpha s^{-\alpha-1}/\Gamma(1-\alpha)ds.

Proof.

By Proposition
6.15
we know that
q(t,\cdot)\in\cC_{0}
hence we can write

P_{s}q(t,x)-q(t,x)=\int_{0}^{s}GP_{\tau}q(t,x)d\tau.

Recalling that, still by Proposition
6.15
,
\partial_{x}q(t,\cdot),Gq(t,\cdot)\in\cC_{0}
, by Item
(3)
of Lemma
6.9
we have

(6.49)

{P_{s}q(t,x)-q(t,x)=\int_{0}^{s}P_{\tau}Gq(t,x)d\tau.}

In particular,
Gq(t,\cdot)\in\cC_{0}(1/2)
and, by (
6.47
)

[Gq(t,\cdot)]_{1/2}\leq\sqrt{\frac{K}{2t}}\frac{\Gamma\left(\alpha-\frac{1}{2}\right)}{2\pi\Gamma(\alpha)}.

Hence, by Item
(5)
of Lemma
6.9
we get that
P_{\tau}Gq(t,\cdot)\in\cC_{0}(1/2)
with

[P_{\tau}Gq(t,\cdot)]_{1/2}\leq\sqrt{\frac{2K}{t}}e^{\frac{3}{8}\tau}\frac{\Gamma\left(\alpha-\frac{1}{2}\right)}{2\pi\Gamma(\alpha)}.

Using this into (
6.49
) we have

\displaystyle\left|P_{s}q(t,x)-q(t,x)\right|

\displaystyle{\leq}\int_{0}^{s}\left|P_{\tau}Gvq(t,x)\right|d\tau\leq\sqrt{\frac{2K}{t}}e^{\frac{1}{2}|\log(x)|}\frac{\Gamma\left(\alpha-\frac{1}{2}\right)}{2\pi\Gamma(\alpha)}\int_{0}^{s}e^{\frac{3}{8}\tau}\,d\tau

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{2K}{t}}e^{\frac{1}{2}|\log(x)|}\frac{\Gamma\left(\alpha-\frac{1}{2}\right)}{2\pi\Gamma(\alpha)}e^{\frac{3}{8}s}s.

Integrating the latter against
\nu_{\phi}
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\left|P_{s}q(t,x)-q(t,x)\right|\frac{\alpha s^{-\alpha-1}}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}ds

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{2K}{t}}e^{\frac{1}{2}|\log(x)|}\frac{\Gamma\left(\alpha-\frac{1}{2}\right)}{2\pi\Gamma(\alpha)}\int_{0}^{t}e^{\frac{3}{8}s}\frac{\alpha s^{-\alpha}}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}\,ds

\displaystyle\leq e^{\frac{1}{2}|\log(x)|}\frac{\alpha\Gamma\left(\alpha-\frac{1}{2}\right)\sqrt{2K}}{2\pi\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(2-\alpha)}e^{\frac{3}{8}t}t^{\frac{1}{2}-\alpha}.

Finally, integrating the latter against
e^{-\lambda t}
for
\lambda>\frac{3}{8}
we get

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{t}

\displaystyle e^{-\lambda t}\left|P_{s}q(t,x)-q(t,x)\right|\frac{\alpha s^{-\alpha-1}}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}\,ds\,dt

\displaystyle\leq e^{\frac{1}{2}|\log(x)|}\frac{\alpha\Gamma\left(\alpha-\frac{1}{2}\right)\sqrt{2K}}{2\pi\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(2-\alpha)}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\left(\lambda-\frac{3}{8}\right)t}t^{\frac{1}{2}-\alpha}\,dt

\displaystyle=e^{\frac{1}{2}|\log(x)|}\frac{\alpha\Gamma\left(\alpha-\frac{1}{2}\right)\sqrt{2K}}{2\pi\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(2-\alpha)}\left(\lambda-\frac{3}{8}\right)^{\alpha-\frac{3}{2}}\Gamma\left(\frac{3}{2}-\alpha\right).

∎

The last thing we need to check is Item (iii) of Theorem
3.3
.

Proposition 6.18
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
6.11
for all
x,\lambda>0
it holds

\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}\overline{\nu}_{\alpha}(t)|q(t,x)|\,dt<\infty,

where
\bar{\nu}_{\alpha}(t)
is the tail of (
6.48
).

Proof.

Since
\overline{\nu}_{\phi}(t)=\frac{t^{-\alpha}}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}
, we just use the inequality (
6.45
) to get

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}\frac{t^{-\alpha}}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}|q(t,x)|\,dt\leq\frac{e^{|\log(x)|}}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{-\alpha}e^{-\lambda t}dt=\frac{e^{|\log(x)|}}{\lambda^{1-\alpha}}.

∎

6.2.2.
Existence of the solution

We are now ready to prove a first bit of Theorem
6.11
, which is summarized in the next proposition.

Proposition 6.19
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
6.11
,
q
is a solution of (
6.36
).

Proof.

In view of the results in Subsection
6.2.1
, we know by Theorem
3.2
that
(\partial_{t}-G)^{\alpha}q(t,x)
is well-defined for all
x,t>0
. Moreover, for any
x>0
we know by Theorem
3.3
that
(\partial_{t}-G)^{\alpha}q(\cdot,x)
admits Laplace transform for any
\lambda>\frac{3}{8}
.
We divide the proof into two parts. First we check that for all
x>0
and
\lambda>\frac{3}{8}
we have

(6.50)

\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}(\partial_{t}-G)^{\alpha}q(t,x)dt=\lambda^{\alpha-1}(x-K)_{+},

which is the first equation in (
6.36
) after taking the Laplace transform in the
t
variable on both sides. Then we use this to prove that

(6.51)

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}-G)^{\alpha}q(t,x)=\frac{t^{-\alpha}}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}q(0,x).

We begin with (
6.50
).
Consider
x>0
,
\lambda>\frac{3}{8}
and denote

\widetilde{q}(\lambda,x)=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}q(t,x)\,dt.

Let us first evaluate an alternative formula for
\widetilde{q}
. Set
u_{K}(x)=(x-K)_{+}
and notice that, by (
6.22
), we get

\displaystyle\widetilde{q}(\lambda,x)\,=\,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(1-\alpha)}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}\int_{0}^{t}s^{\alpha-1}(t-s)^{-\alpha}q_{\rm BS}(s,x)\,ds\,dt

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda^{\alpha-1}}{\Gamma(\alpha)}\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-\lambda s}s^{\alpha-1}q_{\rm BS}(s,x)\,ds

(6.52)

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda^{\alpha-1}}{\Gamma(\alpha)}\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-\lambda s}s^{\alpha-1}P_{s}u_{K}(x)\,ds,

where, in the first step, we used the convolution Theorem for Laplace transform (e.g.,
[
2
, Proposition 1.6.4]
) and then we applied Tonelli’s theorem since
q_{\rm BS}
is non-negative. As a consequence of (
3.2
), we have that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}

\displaystyle\left(\partial_{t}-G\right)^{\alpha}q(x,t)\,dt\,

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}(P_{s}q(t-s,x)\mathds{1}_{(0,t]}(s)-q(t,x))\,dt\,\nu_{\alpha}(ds)

\displaystyle\,=\,

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{s}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}q(t-s,y)p_{\rm GBM}(s,y;x)\,dy\,dt-\widetilde{q}(\lambda,x)\right)\,\nu_{\alpha}(ds)

\displaystyle\,=\,

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(e^{-\lambda s}\int_{0}^{\infty}\widetilde{q}(\lambda,y)p_{\rm GBM}(s,y;x)\,dy-\widetilde{q}(\lambda,x)\right)\,\nu_{\alpha}(ds)

\displaystyle\,=\,

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(e^{-\lambda s}P_{s}(\widetilde{q}(\lambda,\cdot))(x)-\widetilde{q}(\lambda,x)\right)\,\nu_{\alpha}(ds),

where in the third equality we used again Tonelli’s theorem to exchange the order of the integrals (since
q
and
p_{\rm GBM}
are non-negative) after having made explicit the action of the operator
P_{s}
in the second step. Now we can replace
\widetilde{q}
in the previous identity by making use of (
6.52
). To do this, we first observe that

\displaystyle P_{s}\left(\widetilde{q}(\lambda,\cdot)\right)(x)\,=\,

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda^{\alpha-1}}{\Gamma(\alpha)}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-\lambda\tau}\tau^{\alpha-1}P_{\tau}u_{K}(y)p_{\rm GBM}(s,y;x)d\tau\,dy

(6.53)

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda^{\alpha-1}}{\Gamma(\alpha)}\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-\lambda\tau}\tau^{\alpha-1}P_{s+\tau}u_{K}(x)\,d\tau

and then we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}\left(\partial_{t}-G\right)^{\alpha}q(x,t)\,dt

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle-\frac{\alpha\lambda^{\alpha-1}}{\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(1-\alpha)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\tau^{\alpha-1}s^{-\alpha-1}\left(e^{-\lambda(s+\tau)}P_{s+\tau}u_{K}(x)\,-e^{-\lambda\tau}P_{\tau}u_{K}(x)\,\right)d\tau\,ds

(6.54)

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle-\frac{\alpha\lambda^{\alpha-1}}{\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(1-\alpha)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\tau^{\alpha-1}s^{-\alpha-1}e^{-\lambda\tau}P_{\tau}\left(e^{-\lambda s}q_{\rm BS}(s,x)\,-u_{K}(x)\,\right)d\tau\,ds,

where we used the fact that
P_{\tau}
is a semigroup action and the explicit form of
\nu_{\alpha}(\cdot)
given in (
6.48
). However, if we observe that

\displaystyle e^{-\lambda s}q_{\rm BS}(s,x)\,-u_{K}(x)\,=\,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{s}\partial_{w}e^{-\lambda w}q_{\rm BS}(w,x)\,dw

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{s}e^{-\lambda w}\left(\partial_{w}q_{\rm BS}(w,x)-\lambda q_{\rm BS}(w,x)\right)\,dw

(6.55)

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{s}e^{-\lambda w}\left(G-\lambda\right)\,q_{\rm BS}(w,x)\,dw

and we explicitly write the integral in
P_{\tau}
, we get

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}\left(\partial_{t}-G\right)^{\alpha}q(x,t)\,dt

(6.56)

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle-\frac{\alpha\lambda^{\alpha-1}}{\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(1-\alpha)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\int_{0}^{s}\tau^{\alpha-1}s^{-\alpha-1}e^{-\lambda(\tau+w)}\left(G-\lambda\right)\,q_{\rm BS}(w,y)\,p_{\rm GBM}(\tau,y;x)\,dw\,dy\,d\tau\,ds.

We want to use Fubini’s theorem to change the order of the integrals. To do this, we notice that (
6.28
) implies
Gq_{BS}\geq 0
and then

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{+\infty}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\int_{0}^{s}\tau^{\alpha-1}s^{-\alpha-1}e^{-\lambda(\tau+w)}\left|(G-\lambda)q_{\rm BS}(w,y)\right|p_{\rm GBM}(\tau,y;x)\,dw\,dy\,d\tau\,ds

(6.57)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{+\infty}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\int_{0}^{s}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\tau^{\alpha-1}s^{-\alpha-1}e^{-\lambda(\tau+w)}(G+\lambda)q_{\rm BS}(w,y)p_{\rm GBM}(\tau,y;x)\,dy\,dw\,d\tau\,ds

(6.58)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{+\infty}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\int_{0}^{s}\tau^{\alpha-1}s^{-\alpha-1}e^{-\lambda(\tau+w)}P_{\tau}(G+\lambda)q_{\rm BS}(w,x)\,dw\,d\tau\,ds

Since
q_{\rm BS}(t,\cdot)\in\cC_{0}
,
\partial_{x}q_{\rm BS}\in\cC_{0}
and
Gq_{\rm BS}\in\cC_{0}
, we can exchange the operator
(G+\lambda)
with
P_{\tau}
, getting

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{+\infty}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\int_{0}^{s}\tau^{\alpha-1}s^{-\alpha-1}e^{-\lambda(\tau+w)}\left|(G-\lambda)q_{\rm BS}(w,y)\right|p_{\rm GBM}(\tau,y;x)\,dw\,dy\,d\tau\,ds

(6.59)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{+\infty}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\int_{0}^{s}\tau^{\alpha-1}s^{-\alpha-1}e^{-\lambda(\tau+w)}(G+\lambda)P_{\tau}q_{\rm BS}(w,x)\,dw\,d\tau\,ds

(6.60)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{+\infty}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\int_{0}^{s}\tau^{\alpha-1}s^{-\alpha-1}e^{-\lambda(\tau+w)}(G+\lambda)q_{\rm BS}(\tau+w,x)\,dw\,d\tau\,ds,

where we also used the fact that
P_{\tau}q_{\rm BS}(w,x)=P_{\tau}P_{w}u_{K}(x)=P_{\tau+w}u_{K}(x)=q_{\rm BS}(w+\tau,x)
. By (
6.28
) we have

(6.61)

Gq_{\rm BS}(\tau+w,x)\leq\sqrt{\frac{Kx}{8\pi(\tau+w)}}\exp\left(-\frac{\log^{2}\left(\frac{x}{K}\right)}{2(\tau+w)}\right).

Consider the function
r\in\R^{+}\mapsto\sqrt{r}\exp\left(-r\frac{\log^{2}\left(\frac{x}{K}\right)}{2}\right)\in\R
. It is possible to check that such a function has a maximum at
r_{\star}=\log^{-2}\left(\frac{x}{K}\right)
and then

\sqrt{r}\exp\left(-r\frac{\log^{2}\left(\frac{x}{K}\right)}{2}\right)\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{e}}\left|\log^{-1}\left(\frac{x}{K}\right)\right|.

Plugging the latter into (
6.61
), adding
\lambda q_{\rm BS}(\tau+w,x)
and also using (
6.29
) we get

(G+\lambda)q_{\rm BS}(\tau+w,x)\leq\sqrt{\frac{Kx}{8\pi e}}\left|\log^{-1}\left(\frac{x}{K}\right)\right|+\lambda e^{|\log(x)|}.

Plugging this inequality into (
6.60
) we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{+\infty}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\int_{0}^{s}\tau^{\alpha-1}s^{-\alpha-1}e^{-\lambda(\tau+w)}\left|(G-\lambda)q_{\rm BS}(w,y)\right|p_{\rm GBM}(\tau,y;x)\,dw\,dy\,d\tau\,ds

\displaystyle\leq\,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\lambda}\left(\sqrt{\frac{Kx}{8\pi e}}\left|\log^{-1}\left(\frac{x}{K}\right)\right|+\lambda e^{|\log(x)|}\right)\int_{0}^{+\infty}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\tau^{\alpha-1}(1-e^{-\lambda s})s^{-\alpha-1}e^{-\lambda\tau}\,d\tau\,ds

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\lambda}\left(\sqrt{\frac{Kx}{8\pi e}}\left|\log^{-1}\left(\frac{x}{K}\right)\right|+\lambda e^{|\log(x)|}\right)\left(\int_{0}^{+\infty}\tau^{\alpha-1}e^{-\lambda\tau}\,d\tau\right)\left(\int_{0}^{+\infty}s^{-\alpha-1}(1-e^{-\lambda s})\,ds\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\lambda}\left(\sqrt{\frac{Kx}{8\pi e}}\left|\log^{-1}\left(\frac{x}{K}\right)\right|+\lambda e^{|\log(x)|}\right)\frac{\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(1-\alpha)}{\alpha}<\infty

where, in the last step, we used the integral representation of the Gamma function that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{+\infty}s^{-\alpha-1}(1-e^{-\lambda s})\,ds=\frac{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}{\alpha}\lambda^{\alpha}.

We can now safely use Fubini’s theorem in (
6.56
), obtaining

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}\left(\partial_{t}-G\right)^{\alpha}q(x,t)\,dt

\displaystyle=-\frac{\alpha\lambda^{\alpha-1}}{\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(1-\alpha)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\int_{w}^{+\infty}\tau^{\alpha-1}s^{-\alpha-1}e^{-\lambda(\tau+w)}\left(G-\lambda\right)\,q_{\rm BS}(w,y)\,p_{\rm GBM}(\tau,y;x)\,ds\,dy\,d\tau\,dw

\displaystyle=-\frac{\lambda^{\alpha-1}}{\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(1-\alpha)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\tau^{\alpha-1}w^{-\alpha}e^{-\lambda(\tau+w)}P_{\tau}\left(G-\lambda\right)\,q_{\rm BS}(w,x)\,d\tau\,dw

\displaystyle=-\frac{\lambda^{\alpha-1}}{\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(1-\alpha)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\tau^{\alpha-1}w^{-\alpha}e^{-\lambda(\tau+w)}\left(G-\lambda\right)\,q_{\rm BS}(\tau+w,x)\,d\tau\,dw

\displaystyle=-\frac{\lambda^{\alpha-1}}{\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(1-\alpha)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{w}^{+\infty}(z-w)^{\alpha-1}w^{-\alpha}\left(G-\lambda\right)\,e^{-\lambda z}q_{\rm BS}(z,x)\,dz\,dw

\displaystyle=-\frac{\lambda^{\alpha-1}}{\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(1-\alpha)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(G-\lambda\right)\,e^{-\lambda z}q_{\rm BS}(z,x)\left(\int_{0}^{z}(z-w)^{\alpha-1}w^{-\alpha}\,dw\right)\,dz

(6.62)

\displaystyle=-\lambda^{\alpha-1}\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(G-\lambda\right)\,e^{-\lambda z}q_{\rm BS}(z,x)dz,

where in the third equality we used that
P_{\tau}q_{\rm BS}(w,y)=P_{\tau}P_{w}u_{K}(x)=P_{\tau+w}u_{K}(x)=q_{\rm BS}(\tau+w,x)
, in the fourth equality we used the change of variables
\tau=z-w
, in the fifth equality we used again Fubini’s theorem and in the last equality we used the integral representation of the Beta function. Now notice that (
6.29
), (
6.30
) and (
6.31
) guarantee that we can take the Laplace transform in the variable
t
on both sides of the first equation of (
6.33
), leading to

\lambda\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-\lambda z}q_{\rm BS}(z,x)dz-u_{K}(x)=\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-\lambda z}Gq_{\rm BS}(z,x)dz.

This equality can be rearranged to obtain

\int_{0}^{+\infty}(\lambda-G)e^{-\lambda z}q_{\rm BS}(z,x)dz=u_{K}(x)

which, replaced into (
6.62
) gives (
6.50
).

By injectivity of the Laplace transform, we know that for all
x>0
there exists a measurable set
\mathcal{I}_{x}\subset\R^{+}
such that
|\mathcal{I}_{x}|=0
and for all
t\in\R^{+}\setminus\mathcal{I}_{x}
one has

(\partial_{t}-G)^{\alpha}q(t,x)=\frac{t^{-\alpha}}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}q(0,x).

We want to prove that
\mathcal{I}_{x}=\varnothing
for all
x>0
. To do this, we first show that
q
is continuous. Indeed, let
t,x>0
and consider a sequence
(t_{n},x_{n})\to(t,x)
. By (
6.22
) and (
6.39
) we have

q(t_{n},x_{n})=\int_{0}^{1}q_{\rm BS}(t_{n}s,x_{n})\frac{(1-s)^{-\alpha}s^{\alpha-1}}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)\Gamma(\alpha)}ds.

Since
x_{n}\to x
, there exist
0<a<b
such that
x_{n}\in[a,b]
for all
n\in\N
and then
|q_{\rm BS}(t_{n}s,x_{n})|\leq e^{\max\{|\log(a)|,|\log(b)|\}}
. Hence, we can use the dominated convergence theorem to get

\lim_{n\to+\infty}q(t_{n},x_{n})=\int_{0}^{1}q_{\rm BS}(ts,x)\frac{(1-s)^{-\alpha}s^{\alpha-1}}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)\Gamma(\alpha)}ds=q(t,x).

Now we prove that for fixed
x>0
the function
(\partial_{t}-G)^{\alpha}q(\cdot,x)
is right-continuous. Indeed, let
t_{n}\downarrow t
for some
t>0
and observe that

\displaystyle|(\partial_{t}-G)^{\alpha}q(t_{n},x)-(\partial_{t}-G)^{\alpha}q(t,x)|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\alpha}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}\int_{0}^{t}|P_{s}q(t-s,x)-q(t,x)-P_{s}q(t_{n}-s,x)+q(t_{n},x)|s^{-1-\alpha}ds

\displaystyle+\frac{\alpha}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}\int_{t}^{t_{n}}|P_{s}q(t_{n}-s,x)-q(t_{n},x)|s^{-1-\alpha}ds

\displaystyle+\frac{t^{-\alpha}-t_{n}^{-\alpha}}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}|q(t,x)|+|q(t,x)-q(t_{n},x)|\frac{t_{n}^{-\alpha}}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}

\displaystyle:=\frac{\alpha}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}\left(I_{n}(x)+J_{n}(x)+\frac{t^{-\alpha}-t_{n}^{-\alpha}}{\alpha}|q(t,x)|+|q(t,x)-q(t_{n},x)|\frac{t_{n}^{-\alpha}}{\alpha}\right).

Let us first consider
I_{n}(x)
. We have

\displaystyle|P_{s}q(t-s,x)

\displaystyle-q(t,x)-P_{s}q(t_{n}-s,x)+q(t_{n},x)|

\displaystyle\leq|P_{s}q(t-s,x)-P_{s}q(t_{n}-s,x)|+|q(t,x)-q(t_{n},x)|

\displaystyle\leq P_{s}|q(t_{n}-s,\cdot)-q(t-s,\cdot)|(x)+|q(t,x)-q(t_{n},x)|

Arguing as in the proof of Corollary
6.14
, we have that
|q(t-s,\cdot)-q(t_{n}-s,\cdot)|\in\cC_{0}(1/2)
with
t_{n}-s-(t_{n}-t)

[|q(t-s,\cdot)-q(t_{n}-s,\cdot)|]_{1/2}\leq\frac{\Gamma\left(\alpha+\frac{1}{2}\right)(t_{n}-t)}{\pi\Gamma(\alpha)}\sqrt{\frac{K}{t-s}}.

Hence, by Item
(5)
of Lemma
6.9
we have

\displaystyle P_{s}|q(t_{n}-s,\cdot)-q(t-s,\cdot)|(x)\leq 2\frac{\Gamma\left(\alpha+\frac{1}{2}\right)(t_{n}-t)}{\pi\Gamma(\alpha)}\sqrt{\frac{K}{t-s}}e^{\frac{3}{8}s+\frac{|\log(x)|}{2}}.

Combining this inequality with the fact that
q
is continuous, for fixed
s\in[0,t)
, we find

\lim_{n\to\infty}|P_{s}q(t-s,x)-q(t,x)-P_{s}q(t_{n}-s,x)+q(t_{n},x)|=0.

Hence, it is sufficient to show that we can use the dominated convergence theorem to take the limit inside the integral sign in
I_{n}(x)
. To do this, observe that

(6.63)

\displaystyle\begin{split}|P_{s}q(t-s,x)&-q(t,x)-P_{s}q(t_{n}-s,x)+q(t_{n},x)|\\
&\leq|P_{s}q(t-s,x)-P_{s}q(t,x)|+|P_{s}q(t,x)-q(t,x)|\\
&\qquad+|P_{s}q(t_{n}-s,x)-P_{s}q(t_{n},x)|+|P_{s}q(t_{n},x)-q(t_{n},x)|\\
&\leq P_{s}|q(t-s,\cdot)-q(t,\cdot)|(x)+|P_{s}q(t,x)-q(t,x)|\\
&\qquad+P_{s}|q(t_{n}-s,\cdot)-q(t_{n},\cdot)|(x)+|P_{s}q(t_{n},x)-q(t_{n},x)|.\end{split}

Again recall that
|q(t-s,\cdot)-q(t,\cdot)|\in\cC_{0}(1/2)
with

[|q(t-s,\cdot)-q(t,\cdot)|]_{1/2}\leq\frac{\Gamma\left(\alpha+\frac{1}{2}\right)s}{\pi\Gamma(\alpha)}\sqrt{\frac{K}{t-s}},

hence, by Item
(5)
of Lemma
6.9
,

\displaystyle P_{s}|q(t-s,\cdot)-q(t,\cdot)|(x)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\Gamma\left(\alpha+\frac{1}{2}\right)s}{\pi\Gamma(\alpha)}\sqrt{\frac{K}{t-s}}e^{\frac{3}{8}t+\frac{|\log(x)|}{2}}

(6.64)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\Gamma\left(\alpha+\frac{1}{2}\right)s}{\pi\Gamma(\alpha)}\sqrt{\frac{K}{t-s}}e^{\frac{3}{8}t_{1}+\frac{|\log(x)|}{2}}

\displaystyle P_{s}|q(t_{n}-s,\cdot)-q(t_{n},\cdot)|(x)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\Gamma\left(\alpha+\frac{1}{2}\right)s}{\pi\Gamma(\alpha)}\sqrt{\frac{K}{t_{n}-s}}e^{\frac{3}{8}t_{n}+\frac{|\log(x)|}{2}}
